## I Dodging Submarines to Cover the Biggest Game of All / I 躲潜艇，去报道天下头号大赛

===Original===
Wednesday, July 18. A Lake Michigan Port.

I kept an appointment today with a gentleman from Somewhere in Connecticut.

“How,” said he, “would you like to go to France?”

I told him I’d like it very much, but that I was thirty-two years old, with a dependable wife and three unreliable children.

“Those small details,” he said, “exempt you from military duty. But we want you as a war correspondent.”

I told him I knew nothing about war. He said it had frequently been proved that that had nothing to do with it. So we hemmed and we hawed, pro and con, till my conscientious objections were all overruled.

“In conclusion,” said he, “we’d prefer to have you go on a troopship. That can be arranged through the War Department. There’ll be no trouble about it.”

===Chinese===
7 月 18 日，星期三。密歇根湖畔某港。

今天我赴了一位绅士的约，他来自康涅狄格某地（Somewhere in Connecticut）。

「怎么样，」他说，「你想不想去法国？」

我说想得很，只是我三十二岁了，家里有一个靠得住的老婆和三个靠不住的娃。

「这类小事，」他说，「正好免了你服兵役。不过我们要请你当战地记者。」

我说我对战争一窍不通。他说，事实证明这向来不相干。于是我们嗯嗯啊啊，正说反说，直到我的良心异议全数被驳回。

「最后，」他说，「我们倒希望你搭运兵船去。这事可以由陆军部安排，毫无问题。」

===Original===
Monday, July 30. A Potomac Port.

Today I took the matter up with the War Department, through Mr. Creel.

“Mr. Creel,” I said, “can I go on a troopship?”

“No,” said Mr. Creel.

There was no trouble about it.

===Chinese===
7 月 30 日，星期一。波托马克河畔某港。

今天我通过克里尔先生（Mr. Creel），向陆军部提了这件事。

「克里尔先生，」我说，「我能搭运兵船吗？」

「不能。」克里尔先生说。

果然毫无问题。

===Original===
Wednesday, August 1. An Atlantic Port.

The young man in the French Consulate has taken a great fancy to me. He will not visé my passport till I bring him two more autographed pictures of myself.

George W. Gloom of the steamship company said there would be a ship sailing Saturday.

“Are we convoyed through the danger zone?” I inquired.

“We don’t guarantee it,” said he. “There has never been an accident on this line,” he added.

“What I was thinking about,” said I, “wouldn’t be classed as an accident.” Further questioning developed the comforting fact that the ship I am taking has never been sunk.

===Chinese===
8 月 1 日，星期三。大西洋沿岸某港。

法国领事馆里的那位年轻人对我青眼有加。我要不给他再送去两张亲笔签名照，他就不给我的护照签证。

轮船公司的乔治·W·格鲁姆（George W. Gloom，这姓氏的意思是「阴沉」）说，星期六有船开。

「过了危险区，有护航吗？」我问。

「这个我们不担保，」他说，「本航线从来没有出过事。」他又补了一句。

「我惦记的那种事，」我说，「恐怕算不得『事故』。」再往下盘问，倒问出一条令人宽心的事实：我要搭的这条船，从来没有被击沉过。

===Original===
I told him I wanted a cabin to myself, as I expected to work.

“You will be in with two others,” he said.

“I would pay a little more to be alone,” said I.

This evidently was not worth answering, so I asked him how long the trip would take.

“I know nothing about it,” said he.

“I believe that,” said I when I was well out of his earshot.

===Chinese===
我说我想要一间独住的舱房，因为我指望干点活。

「你跟另外两位合住。」他说。

「多付点钱让我一个人住也行。」我说。

这话显然不值得一答，于是我改问他航程得走多久。

「这我一概不知。」他说。

「这我信。」等走到他断断听不见的地方，我说。

===Original===
Wednesday, August 8. At Sea.

We left port at ten last night, a mere three and a half days behind schedule. The ship and I should be very congenial, as we are about the same age.

My roommates are a young man from Harvard and a young man from Yale, but so far I have managed to keep the conversation neutral. We suspect that they made ours a first-class cabin by substituting the word *1ère* for *2ème* on the sign, and I am very certain that my berth was designed for Rabbit Maranville.

Our passenger list includes a general, a congressman, a lady novelist and her artist husband, French; a songbird, also French; two or three majors, a Thaw, and numerous gentlemen of the consular service. The large majority on board are young men going into American Ambulance and Y.M.C.A. work.

===Chinese===
8 月 8 日，星期三。海上。

昨晚十点我们离了港，比时刻表不过晚了区区三天半。这条船和我是同辈，年齿相仿，理应很投缘。

与我同舱的一位是哈佛小子，一位是耶鲁小子，不过到目前为止，我总算把谈话维持在中立状态。我们疑心是有人把舱门牌上的「2ème（二等）」改成了「1ère（一等）」，把我们这间升成了一等舱；而我可以拿得十分肯定，我的铺位当初是照「兔子」马拉维尔（Rabbit Maranville）的身材设计的。

乘客名单里有位将军、位国会议员、位法国女小说家和她的画家丈夫；还有一只法国歌鸟；两三位少校，一位索家阔少（a Thaw），外加一大票领事界的先生们。船上绝大多数是去干美国救护队和基督教青年会工作的年轻人。

===Original===
After breakfast this morning there was lifeboat drill, directed by our purser, who is permanently made up as Svengali. He sent us down to our cabins to get our life-belts and then assigned us to our boats. Mine, No. 12, is as far from my cabin as they could put it without cutting it loose from the ship, and if I happen to be on deck when that old torpedo strikes, believe me, I’m not going to do a Marathon for a life-belt. Shoes off, and a running hop, step and jump looks like the best system. Moreover, I’m going to disobey another of the rules, which is that each passenger must remain calm.

Next we had to fill out a form for the enlightenment of Svengali as to our destination, business, home address, foreign address, literary tastes, etc. One item was “the names of relatives or friends you lofh.” This was unanswered, as nobody aboard seemed to know the meaning of the verb.

In the *fumoir* this afternoon a young American wanted a match. He consulted his dictionary and dug out “*allumette*.” But he thought the *t*’s were silent and asked Auguste for “allumay.” Auguste disappeared and returned in five minutes with a large glass of lemonade. The cost of that little French lesson was two francs.

I am elected to eat at the “second table.” Our bunch has luncheon at twelve-thirty and dinner at seven. The first table crowd’s hours are eleven and five-thirty. Breakfast is a free-for-all and we sit where we choose. My trough mates at the meals are two Americans, a Brazilian, and four Frenchmen. Ours is a stag table, which unfortunate circumstance is due to the paucity of women, or, as they are sometimes called, members of the fair sex. The Brazilian speaks nine or ten languages, but seems to prefer French. The two Americans are always engaged in sotto voce dialogue, and the four Frenchmen race with the Brazilian for the conversational speed championship of the high seas. This leaves me free to devote all my time to the proper mastication of food.

===Chinese===
今天早饭后是救生演习，由我们那位事务长指挥——此人的长相永久定妆为斯文加利（Svengali，小说《特丽尔比》里的催眠师）。他打发我们下舱取救生带，再把各人分派到各艇。我那条，12 号，安得离我舱房要多远有多远，再远一点就得从船上锯下去了。倘若那颗老鱼雷打来时我恰好人在甲板上，请相信，我绝不为一条救生带去跑马拉松。脱了鞋，来个助跑加单脚起跳，看着才像最佳方案。另外，我还打算违抗另一条规矩——乘客务必保持镇静。

接着要填一张表，好让斯文加利弄清各人的目的地、职业、家庭住址、在外住址、文学品味等等。其中一栏是「你所 lofh（爱）的亲戚朋友姓名」。此栏无人填写，因为船上似乎没人认识这个动词。

今天下午在吸烟室（fumoir）里，一位美国小伙子想要根火柴。他查了词典，挖出「allumette（火柴）」。可他以为那两个 t 不发音，就跟奥古斯特（Auguste）要「阿吕梅（allumay）」。奥古斯特退下去，五分钟后端回来一大杯柠檬水。这堂法语小课的学费是两个法郎。

我被分派在「第二席」用膳。我们这一拨十二点半进午餐，七点进晚餐。头席那拨是十一点和五点半。早饭自由入场，座位随挑。与我同槽进食的是两位美国人、一位巴西人和四位法国人。我们那桌是纯爷们桌，这一不幸事态应归咎于女性匮乏——或者按有些人的叫法，「阴性成员」短缺。那位巴西人通晓九到十门语言，但似乎偏爱法语。两位美国人永远忙着低声对谈，四位法国人则同巴西人争夺公海对话速度锦标赛的锦标。这倒让我得以腾出全部时间，专心致志地咀嚼食物。

===Original===
Thursday, August 9. Completely at Sea.

A gentleman on board is supplied with one of these newfangled one hundred dollar safety suits. The wearer is supposed to be able to float indefinitely. It is also a sort of thermos bottle, keeping one warm in cold water and cool in hot. I do not envy the gent. I have no ambition to float indefinitely. And if I didn’t happen to have it on when the crash came, I doubt whether I could spare the time to change. And besides, if I ever do feel that I can afford one hundred dollars for a suit, I won’t want to wear it for the edification of mere fish.

When Svengali isn’t busy pursing, he is usually engaged in chess matches with another of the officers. The rest of the idle portion of the crew stand round the table and look on. Sometimes they look on for an hour without seeing a move made, but they never seem to lose interest. Every little movement brings forth a veritable torrent of *français* from the spectators. I can understand the fascination of chess from the player’s end, but could get few thrills from watching, especially when there was standing room only.

Far more fascinating to look at is the game two of my French trough mates play at breakfast. The rules are simple. You take a muffin about the size of a golf ball. You drop it into your cup of chocolate. Then you fish for it, sometimes with a spoon, but more often with your fingers. The object is to convey it to your mouth without discoloring your necktie. Success comes three times in five.

The players are about evenly matched. One of them I suspect, is not in the game for sport’s sake, but has a worthier object. Nature supplied him with a light gray mustache, and a chocolate brown would blend better with his complexion. If the muffins hold out, his color scheme will be perfect before we reach port.

The discovery has been made that there’s a man on board who plays the cornet, so if we are subbed it will not be an unmitigated evil.

===Chinese===
8 月 9 日，星期四。彻底在海上。

船上有位绅士，置办了一身新出的一百美元安全服。据说穿上它的人能无限期漂浮。它还是种热水瓶，冷水里为你保暖，热水里为你保凉。我不眼红这位爷。我没有无限期漂浮的抱负。何况就算有，撞船那一刻我若恰好没穿着它，我怀疑自己也匀不出换装的工夫。再说，真有朝一日我出得起一百美元买一身衣裳，我也犯不着穿给区区几条鱼开眼。

斯文加利不忙着当事务长的时候，通常在和另一位军官下国际象棋。船上其余的闲人围在桌边观战。有时他们看上个把钟头也没见走一步棋，兴致却从不稍减。每动一子，观战诸公便迸出一股名副其实的法语（français）激流。下棋的人迷象棋，这个我懂；观战能得什么刺激，我就不明白了，尤其还只有站票。

远为耐看的，是我两位法国槽友早饭时玩的游戏。规则简单：取一枚高尔夫球大小的松饼，投进你那杯巧克力里，然后垂钓——有时用勺，多数时候用手指。目标是不染污领带，把它送进嘴里。五次能成三回。

两位选手实力相当。我疑心其中一位参与此道并非为了运动，而是抱有更高尚的目标：造化赐了他一撇浅灰小胡子，而巧克力棕和他的肤色更相配。只要松饼供应不断，船抵港之前，他的配色方案就十全十美了。

有人发现船上有一位会吹短号的，所以万一挨了潜艇，也不算十足的坏事。

===Original===
Friday, August 10.

Every morning one sees on the deck people one never saw before, and as we have not stopped at any stations since we started, the inference is that certain parties have not found the trip a continuous joy ride.

A news bulletin, published every morning, sometimes in English and sometimes in French, keeps us right up to date on thrilling events, thrillingly spelled. I have copied a sample:

> It is now the tim for the final invaseon of the west by the eastren american league teams and before this clash is over it will be definitively known wether the two sox teams are to fight it out in a nip and tuk finish or wether the Chicago sox will have a comfortable margen to insure a world series betwean the two largest American citys Chicago and New York.

The French news deals exclusively with the developments in the world series Over There, which is, perhaps, almost as important.

===Chinese===
8 月 10 日，星期五。

每天早上，甲板上都会冒出些前所未见的人。而我们出发以来没靠过一站，由此可以推断：某些人并不觉得这趟旅行是一路逍遥。

每天早晨出一份新闻简报，有时英文有时法文，让我们随时掌握惊心动魄的大事——拼写同样惊心动魄。我抄录了一则样本：

> 现在已到东布美联球队最后侵掠西布的时客；这场厮杀见分晓之前，究竞是两支「袜」队要叮咬到最后一口才分高下，还是芝加哥袜队握有宽裕的胜卷、保征世界大赛在两坐最大的美国成市——芝加哥与纽约——之间举行，届时自会见分哓。

法文版新闻则专登大洋彼岸世界大赛的赛况，这或许也差不多要紧。

===Original===
A new acquaintance made today was that of the Gentleman from Louisiana. He introduced himself to scold me and another guy for not taking sufficient exercise. We told him we found little pleasure in promenading the deck.

“That’s unnecessary,” he said. “Get yourselves a pair of three-pound dumbbells and use them a certain length of time every day.”

So we are constantly on the lookout for a dumbbell shop, but there seems to be a regrettable lack of such establishments in mid-ocean.

The Gentleman from Louisiana says he is going to join the Foreign Legion if they’ll take him. He is only seventy years old.

“But age makes no difference to a man like I,” says he. “I exercise and keep hard. All my friends are hard and tough. Why, one of my friends, an undertaker, always carries a razor in his boot.”

Presumably this bird never allows psychological depression in his business.

===Chinese===
今天新结识的一位，是路易斯安那绅士（the Gentleman from Louisiana）。他自报家门，为的是责备我和另一个家伙运动量不足。我们答道，在甲板上遛弯，我们体会不到多少乐趣。

「那倒不必，」他说，「去弄一对三磅的哑铃，每天定时练上一阵。」

于是我们便时刻留神物色哑铃铺，可惜大洋正中似乎遗憾地缺乏这类商号。

路易斯安那绅士说，只要人家肯收，他就要参加外籍军团（Foreign Legion）。他不过七十岁。

「可年纪对我这样的人毫无影响，」他说，「我坚持锻炼，保持坚硬。我的朋友个个又硬又韧。嗐，我有位朋友是开殡仪馆的，长年在靴筒里揣一把剃刀。」

想必备这位爷的买卖里，从不容许心理性消沉。

===Original===
The Gentleman from Louisiana continues:

“I’ve got a reputation for hardness, but I’m only hard when I know I’m right. I used such hard language once that they injected me from a committee. I was state senator then. But in all the time I held office I never talked more than two minutes.”

We expressed polite regret that he was not a state senator still. And we asked him to have a lemonade.

“No, thank you. Even the softest drinks have a peculiar effect on me. They make my toes stick together.”

We guaranteed to pry those members apart again after he had quenched his thirst, but he would not take a chance.

On the way cabinward from this fascinating presence, I was invited into a crap game on the *salle à manger* floor. The gentleman with the dice tossed a hundred-franc note into the ring and said: “Shoot it all.” And the amount was promptly oversubscribed. So I kept on going cabinward.

===Chinese===
路易斯安那绅士继续道：

「我是出了名的硬，可我只有在确信自己占理时才硬。有一回我措辞太硬，硬到他们把我从一个委员会里『注射』（injected，『逐出』之误）了出去。那会儿我是州参议员。不过我在任的全部年月里，讲话从没超过两分钟。」

我们彬彬有礼地表示遗憾，遗憾他如今不再是州参议员了。接着我们请他喝杯柠檬水。

「不了，谢谢。再柔和的饮料到我身上也有奇效——它们会让我的脚趾头粘到一块儿。」

我们打包票，等他解完渴，定能把那几位成员重新撬开，可他不肯冒这个险。

从这位魅力四射的人物身边回舱的路上，我在餐厅（salle à manger）的地板上被人邀进一场双骰赌局。掷骰的那位绅士往圈里甩进一张一百法郎的票子，说了声：「全押。」赌注顷刻超额认购。于是我继续回舱去。

===Original===
Samedi, 11 Août.

The man back there in the steamship office can no more truthfully say: “There has never been an accident on this line.”

I awoke at three-thirty this morning to find the cabin insufferably hot and opened the porthole which is directly above my berth. The majority of the ocean immediately left its usual haunts and came indoors. Yale and Harvard were given a shower bath and I had a choice of putting on the driest things I could find and going on deck or drowning where I lay. The former seemed the preferable course.

Out there I found several fellow voyagers asleep in their chairs and a watchman in a red-and-white tam-o’-shanter scanning the bounding main for old Hans W. Periscope.

I wanted sympathy, but the watchman informed me that he *ne comprended pas anglais*, monsieur. So we stood there together and scanned, each in his own language.

My *garçon de cabine* promises he will have me thoroughly bailed out by bedtime tonight.

===Chinese===
8 月 11 日，星期六（Samedi, 11 Août）。

轮船公司办公室里那位先生，往后可再不能实话实说「本航线从来没有出过事」了。

今天凌晨三点半我醒来，觉得舱里热得难当，便打开了正开在我铺位上方的舷窗。大洋的大部队当即撤离它惯常的巢穴，进了屋。耶鲁小子和哈佛小子领受了一场淋浴，我呢，要么穿上找得到的最干爽衣裳上甲板，要么原地淹死。前者似乎更为可取。

外面，我见到几位同船旅人在椅子里酣睡，还有个戴红白双色扁圆帽的守望员，正扫视翻腾的洋面，搜寻老汉斯·W·潜望镜（Hans W. Periscope）。

我想要点同情，可守望员通知我，他「听不懂英语，先生（ne comprended pas anglais）」。于是我们并肩站在那里各自扫视，一人操一种语言。

我的舱房侍者（garçon de cabine）保证，今晚就寝前一定把我彻底舀干。

===Original===
I sat at a different breakfast table, but there was no want of entertainment. At my side was a master of both *anglais* and *français*, and opposite him an American young lady who thinks French is simply just impossible to learn.

“Mademoiselle,” says he, “must find it difficult to get what she likes to eat.”

“I certainly do,” says she. “I don’t understand a word of what’s on the menu card.”

“Perhaps I can help mademoiselle,” says he. “Would she like perhaps a grapefruit?”

She would and she’d also like oatmeal and eggs and coffee. So he steered her straight through the meal with almost painful politeness, but in the intervals when he wasn’t using his hands as an aid to gallant discourse, he was manicuring himself with a fork.

This afternoon they drug me into a bridge game. My partner was our congressman’s secretary. Our opponents were a Standard Oil official and a vice-consul bound for Italy. My partner’s middle name was Bid and Mr. Oil’s was Double. And I was too shy to object when they said we’d play for a cent a point.

At the hour of going to press, Standard Oil had practically all the money in the world. And my partner has learned that a holding of five clubs doesn’t demand a bid of the same amount.

===Chinese===
早饭我换了一张桌子坐，照样不缺消遣。我旁边坐着一位精通英语（anglais）和法语（français）的大师，对面是位认定法语压根没法学的美国小姐。

「小姐，」他说，「想吃到合口味的东西，一定很不容易吧。」

「可不是嘛，」她说，「菜单上的字我一个都不认得。」

「也许我能帮小姐一点忙，」他说，「来一只葡萄柚如何？」

她要了，另外还想要麦片粥、鸡蛋和咖啡。于是他领着她一路点完了这顿饭，殷勤得近乎费力；而在不用双手辅助献殷勤的间隙，他便用叉子给自己修指甲。

今天下午他们把我拖进一局桥牌。我的搭档是我们那位国会议员的秘书。对手是标准石油公司（Standard Oil）的一位官员和一位赴意大利上任的副领事。我搭档的中间名是「叫牌」，石油先生的中间名是「加倍」。等他们宣布按每分一美分计注时，我脸皮薄，没好意思反对。

截至发稿，标准石油公司实际上已经把全世界的钱赢到手了。我搭档也学到了：手里攥着五张梅花，并不等于非得照数叫牌。

===Original===
Sunday, August 12.

The boat seems to be well supplied with the necessities of life, such as cocktails and cards and chips, but it is next to impossible to obtain luxuries like matches, ice-water and soap.

Yale and Harvard both knew enough to bring their own soap, but my previous ocean experiences were mostly with the Old Fall River Line, on which there wasn’t time to wash. Neither Yale nor Harvard ever takes a hint. And “*Apportez-moi du savon, s’il vous plaît*,” to the cabin steward is just as ineffectual.

All good people attended service this morning, and some bad ones played poker this afternoon.

===Chinese===
8 月 12 日，星期日。

船上似乎把生活必需品置办得充裕周到——鸡尾酒、纸牌、筹码之类；可火柴、冰水、肥皂这样的奢侈品，却近乎弄不到手。

耶鲁小子和哈佛小子都懂得自带肥皂，而我以往的海上经历多半是在老Fall River航线（the Old Fall River Line，夜航短程班船）上攒下的，那条线路上根本来不及洗漱。耶鲁和哈佛谁也不接暗示。对着舱房侍者来一句「请给我拿块肥皂来（Apportez-moi du savon, s’il vous plaît）」，同样无济于事。

今天早上，好人们都去做了礼拜；下午，部分坏人打起了扑克。

===Original===
In a burst of generosity I invited a second-class French young lady of five summers to have some candy. She accepted, and her acceptance led to the discovery that the ship’s barber is also its candy salesman.

This barber understands not a syllable of English, which fact has added much to young America’s enjoyment. The boys, in the midst of a hair cut, say to him politely: “You realize that you’re a damn rotten barber?” And he answers smilingly: “*Oui, oui*, monsieur.” Yesterday, I am told, a young shavee remarked: “You make me sick.” The barber replied as usual, and the customer was sick all last night.

Tomorrow afternoon there is to be a “concert” and I’m to speak a piece, O Diary!

===Chinese===
一时大发善心，我请一位二等舱的、已度过五个夏天的法国「小姐」吃糖果。她接受了，而她的接受带来了一个发现：船上的理发师兼任本船糖果售货员。

这位理发师一个音节的英文都不懂，这一事实大大增进了年轻美国的游兴。小伙子们正剪着头发，客客气气地问他：「你可知自己是个烂透了的理发师？」他便笑吟吟地答：「好好，先生（Oui, oui）。」听说昨天有位刮脸的主顾说：「你真让我恶心。」理发师照例作答，于是这位顾客恶心了一整夜。

明天下午有一场「音乐会」，我还要表演一段节目，哦，日记！

===Original===
Monday, August 13.

The concert was “*au profit du Secours National de France. Œuvre fondée pour répartir les Secours aux Victimes de la Guerre.*”

Ten minutes before starting time they informed me that I was to talk on “The American National Game,” and I don’t even know how the White Sox came out a week ago tomorrow.

The afternoon’s entertainment opened with a few well-chosen remarks by our congressman. The general, designated on the program as “chairman,” though his real job was toastmaster, talked a while about this, that and the other thing, and then introduced the cornet player, using his real name. This gentleman and I blew at the same time, so I have no idea what he played. I got back in time for some pretty good harmonizing by three young Americans and a boy from Cincinnati. Then there was a Humorous Recitation (the program said so) by a gent with a funny name, and some really delightful French folk songs by the lady novelist. After which came a Humorous Speech (the program forgot to say so) by myself, necessarily brief, as I gave it in French. The French songbird followed with one of those things that jump back and forth between Pike’s Peak and the Grand Canyon, and a brave boy played a ukelele, and the quartet repeated. In conclusion, we all rose and attempted “La Marseillaise.”

Some of the programs had been illustrated by the lady novelist’s artist husband, and these were auctioned off after the show. I made my financial contribution indirectly, through better card players than myself. My bridge partner, I noticed, had recovered from his attack of the Bids.

===Chinese===
8 月 13 日，星期一。

这场音乐会「为法国国家救济会（au profit du Secours National de France）募集善款。本事业创立宗旨：将救济分发给战争受害者（Œuvre fondée pour répartir les Secours aux Victimes de la Guerre）」。

开演前十分钟，他们通知我讲的题目是「美国国球」，可我连白袜队「从明天倒数一星期」那天赛果如何都一无所知。

下午的余兴节目由我们那位国会议员几句择词得当的讲话开场。将军——节目单上给他定的头衔是「主席」，虽然他真正的差事是宴席司仪——就这个、那个以及别的事谈了一阵，然后用真名引荐了那位短号手。这位爷和我是同时开吹的，所以他吹了什么我毫无所知。我赶回来时，正逢三位美国小伙子和一个辛辛那提来的男孩唱几段挺不错的和声。随后是一位名字可笑的先生来了一段幽默朗诵（节目单上这么印的），接着女小说家唱了几支确实迷人的法国民歌。再往后是我的一段幽默演讲（节目单忘了这么印），难免简短，因为我用的是法语。随后那只法国歌鸟唱了那种在派克斯峰和大峡谷之间来回跳跃的东西，一位勇敢的男孩弹了尤克里里，四重唱又返了一场。最后，我们全体起立，试着唱了《马赛曲》。

有几份节目单由女小说家的画家丈夫配了插图，演出结束后当场拍卖。我的捐款是间接完成的，经由几位牌技高过我的牌友之手。我还注意到，我那位桥牌搭档的叫牌病已经痊愈了。

===Original===
Tuesday, August 14.

The concert, by the way, was given in the salon de conversation, which, I think, should be reserved for the Gentleman from Louisiana. He has now told me two hundred times that he won his election to the State Senate by giving one dollar and a half to “a nigger.”

One of our young field-service men spoiled the forenoon poker game with a lecture on how to catch sharks. His remarkable idea is to put beefsteak on a stout copper wire and troll with it. He has evidently been very intimate with this family of fish, and he says they are simply crazy about beefsteak. Personally, I have no desire to catch sharks. There are plenty aboard. But I do wish he had not got to the most interesting part of his theory at the moment the dealer slipped me four sixes before the draw. Everybody was too busy listening to stay.

We have discovered that the man behind the gun in the *fumoir* bears a striking resemblance to Von Hindenburg, but no one has been found who will tell him so.

There was a track meet this afternoon, and the author of this diary was appointed referee. But the first event, a wheelbarrow race, was so exciting that he feared for his weak heart and resigned in favor of our general. There didn’t seem to be much else to the meet but jujutsu, the sport in which skill is supposed to triumph over brawn. I noticed that a two-hundred-and-thirty-pound man was the winner.

We are in that old zone, and the second table’s dinner hour has been advanced to half past six so that there need be no lights in the dining-room. Also, we are ordered not to smoke, not even to light a match, on deck after dark. The *fumoir* will be running for the last time, but the portholes in it will all be sealed, meaning that after thirty-five smokers have done their best for a few hours the atmosphere will be intolerable. We can stay on deck smokeless, or we can try to exist in the airless *fumoir*, or we can go to bed in the dark and wish we were sleepy. And the worst is yet to come.

===Chinese===
8 月 14 日，星期二。

顺便一提，音乐会是在会客厅（salon de conversation）里举行的，我看那地方该留给路易斯安那绅士专用。到如今他已对我讲过两百遍：他当年之所以当选州参议员，靠的是施舍给「一个黑鬼」一块半钱。

我们一位年轻的战地服务队小伙用一场捕鲨讲座，毁了上午的扑克局。他的妙论是把牛排穿在粗铜丝上拖钓。他显然跟鲨鱼这一门鱼类过从甚密，说它们简直为牛排痴狂。就我个人而言，我毫无捕鲨的愿望——船上现成的已经够多。可我唯一惋惜的是，他偏偏赶在发牌人正好在换牌前塞给我四张六的当口，讲到了他理论最精彩的部分，人人忙着听讲，谁也不肯留守下注。

我们发现，吸烟室里炮位后头那位，长相同冯·兴登堡（Von Hindenburg）像得惊人，只是至今还没找到肯当面告诉他的人。

今天下午开了场运动会，本日记作者被任命为裁判。可第一项独轮车赛就扣人心弦得让他为自己的心脏捏一把汗，遂宣布退位，让贤给我们的将军。运动会上除柔术之外似乎再无别项——柔术嘛，按说是技巧制胜蛮力的运动。我注意到，摘冠者是位体重二百三十磅的先生。

我们已驶入那个老区域，第二席的晚饭时刻提前到六点半，餐厅便可不必点灯。同时奉命天黑后不得在甲板上吸烟，连火柴也不许划。吸烟室将最后开放一晚，但舱窗将尽数封死——这意味着三十五位烟民奋力熏陶几个小时之后，室内空气将无法消受。我们可以待在甲板上不吸烟，可以尝试在无空气的吸烟室里存活，也可以摸黑上床，盼着自己犯困。而最糟的还在后头。

===Original===
Wednesday, August 15.

The rules for tonight and tomorrow night provide for the closing of our old friend, the *fumoir*, at seven o’clock, and that witching hour is on you long before you expect it, for they jump the clock fifteen minutes ahead every time it’s noon or midnight. The ship will not be lit up. The passengers may, if they do their shopping early.

There was another lifeboat “drill” this afternoon. Everyone was required to stand in front of his canoe and await the arrival of Svengali. When that gent appeared, he called the roll. As soon as you said “Here” or “Present,” your part of the “drill” was over. When the time comes I must do my drifting under an alias, as Svengali insists on designating me as Monsieur Gardnierre. But No. 12 is at least honored with two second-class ladies. Many a poor devil on the ship is assigned to a lifeboat that is strictly stag.

===Chinese===
8 月 15 日，星期三。

今晚和明晚的章程规定，我们的老朋友吸烟室七点打烊。这个瘆人的时辰总在你料到之前早早降临，因为每逢正午或子夜，他们就把时钟往前拨一刻钟。船不会亮灯。乘客们倒未尝不可亮着——只要赶早置办。

今天下午又搞了一次救生艇「演习」。人人都要站在自己的独木舟前，恭候斯文加利驾到。那位爷一露面就点名。你刚答一声「到」或「有」，你的「演习」任务便告完成。真到那会儿，我只得顶着化名漂流了，因为斯文加利执意称呼我「加德尼耶尔先生」（Monsieur Gardnierre）。不过 12 号艇至少荣幸地配了两位二等舱女士。船上不少倒霉蛋分到的救生艇，可是清一色的光棍。

===Original===
The Gentleman from Louisiana today sprang this one:

“You know when I part my hair in the middle I look just like a girl. Well, sir, during the Mardi Gras, two years ago, I put on a page’s costume and parted my hair in the middle. And you know girls under a certain age must go home at nine o’clock in the evening. Well, sir, a policeman accosted me and told me I had to go home. I gave him the bawling out of his life. And maybe you think he wasn’t surprised!”

Maybe I do think so.

The Gentleman strayed to the subject of Patti and wound up with a vocal imitation of that lady. He stopped suddenly when his voice parted in the middle.

We have seen no periscopes, but when I opened my suitcase this morning I met face to face one of those birds that are house pets with inmates of seven-room flats at twenty-five dollars per month. I missed fire with a clothes brush, and before I could aim again he had submerged under a vest. Looks as if the little fellow were destined to go with me to Paris, but when I get him there I’ll get him good.

===Chinese===
路易斯安那绅士今天又抖出这么一段：

「你知道，我把头发从中间分开，看上去活脱脱一个大姑娘。嘿，先生，两年前过狂欢节（Mardi Gras）那阵子，我穿上一身侍童装，把头发中分。你也知道，没到岁数的姑娘晚上九点就得回家。嘿，先生，一个警察过来搭话，说我必须回家。我把他骂了个狗血淋头——这辈子数那回骂得狠。只怕你还当他没吓一跳吧！」

没准儿我正是这么想的。

绅士先生又把话头溜到了帕蒂（Patti）身上，末了还嗓子一抖，学起那位女士来。他戛然而止——嗓音在当中分了岔。

潜望镜我们一根也没见着，可今天早上打开衣箱，我却和这样一只鸟儿打了照面——就是七间房、月租二十五美元的公寓住户家里养的那种宠物。我用衣刷开了一枪没打着，还没等我重新瞄准，它已潜入一件背心底下。看样子这位小兄弟注定要随我奔赴巴黎了，不过等到了那儿，我准能好好收拾它。

===Original===
Thursday, August 16.

Great excitement last night when a small unlighted boat was sighted half a mile or so off our port. Our gunners, who are said to receive a bonus for every effective shot, had the range all figured out when the pesky thing gave us a signal of friendship. It may have been part of the entertainment.

Today we persuaded the Gentleman from Louisiana to part his hair in the middle. The New Orleans policeman is not guilty.

It develops that while first- and second-class passengers were unable to read or smoke after dark, the third-class *fumoir* is running wide open and the Greeks have their cigarettes, libations and card games, while the idle rich bore one another to death with conversation.

*Un Américain* aboard is now boasting of the world’s championship as a load carrier. It was too much trouble for him to pay Auguste for each beverage as it was served, so he ran a two days’ charge account. His bill was one hundred and seventy-eight francs, or thirty-five dollars and sixty cents.

===Chinese===
8 月 16 日，星期四。

昨晚大为骚动：左舷外约半英里处，望见一条不点灯的小船。我们的炮手们——据说每打中一发有赏钱——已把射程算得妥妥帖帖，那讨厌的东西却偏偏朝我们发了个友好信号。说不定这也是余兴节目的一部分。

今天我们说服了路易斯安那绅士把头发中分。新奥尔良那位警察是无辜的。

这才查明：头等、二等舱乘客天黑后不能看书吸烟，三等舱的吸烟室却开得热火朝天，希腊人们香烟、美酒、纸牌一样不缺；而无所事事的有钱人们，正靠交谈把彼此烦死。

船上「一位美国人」（Un Américain）眼下正自夸是负重项目的世界冠军。每杯饮料端上来就付钱太费事，他索性赊了两天账。账单共计178 法郎，合 35 美元 60 美分。

===Original===
“Who got all the drinks?” he asked Auguste.

“You, monsieur,” that gent replied.

“And what do you charge for a highball?”

“One franc, monsieur,” said Auguste.

Which means, if Auguste is to be believed, that one hundred and seventy-eight highballs went down one throat in two days. And the owner of the throat is still alive and well. Also, he says he will hereafter pay as you enter.

As an appetizer for dinner tonight the captain told everybody to remain on deck, fully dressed and armed with a life-belt, this evening, until he gave permission to retire.

We’re all on deck, and in another minute it will be too dark to write.

Tomorrow night, Boche willing, we will be out of the jurisdiction of this Imp of Darkness.

===Chinese===
「这些酒都是谁喝的？」他问奥古斯特。

「是您，先生。」那位爷答道。

「威士忌兑水你们卖多少钱一杯？」

「一法郎，先生。」奥古斯特说。

这就是说，倘若奥古斯特可信，两天之内有一百七十八杯威士忌下了同一条喉咙，而这条喉咙的主人至今活蹦乱跳。此外他还宣布，今后一律进门先付钱。

作为今晚的开胃小菜，船长吩咐诸位今晚衣装整齐、身佩救生带，留在甲板上，直至他恩准退席就寝。

我们全在甲板上，再过一分钟，天就要黑得写不成字了。

明晚，只要德国佬（Boche）肯开恩，我们就出了这位黑暗小魔王的辖境。

## II I Get to Paris and Encounter Some Strange Sights / 二 我到了巴黎，见识了几桩怪事

===Original===
Friday, August 17. A French Port.

In obedience to the captain’s orders we remained on deck last night, fully dressed, till our ship was past the danger zone and in harbor. There was a rule against smoking or lighting matches, but none against conversation.

===Chinese===
8 月 17 日，星期五。法国某港口。

遵照船长的命令，我们昨夜和衣待在甲板上，直到轮船驶出危险地带、进了港口。船上有条规定：不许抽烟，不许划火柴；至于聊天，倒没禁。

===Original===
The Gentleman from Louisiana and a young American Field Service candidate had the floor. The former’s best was a report of what he saw once while riding along beside the Columbia River. An enormous salmon jumped out of the water and raced six miles with the train before being worn out. Whether the piscatorial athlete flew or rode a motorcycle, we were unable to learn.

The Gentleman from Louisiana yielded to his younger and stronger countryman. Someone had spoken of the lack of convoy. “Don’t you think we haven’t a convoy,” the kid remarked.

I scanned the sea in all directions and saw nothing but the dark waters. “Where is it?” I inquired.

“There’s one on each side of us,” said Young America. “They’re about twenty miles from the ship.”

===Chinese===
发言权落到了路易斯安那绅士和一位美国战地服务队（American Field Service）的年轻候选生手里。前者的看家绝活，是讲他有一回坐火车沿哥伦比亚河而行亲眼所见的一幕：一条大得吓人的鲑鱼跃出水面，跟火车并排赛跑了六英里，方才力竭。至于这位鱼类运动员是靠飞的还是骑了摩托车，我们无从查证。

路易斯安那绅士把讲台让给了这位更年轻、更强壮的同胞。刚才有人提到船上没有护航。那小子评论道：「你们可别以为咱们没护航。」

我朝四面八方的海面扫了一遍，除了黑黢黢的海水什么也没瞧见。「护航舰在哪儿呢？」我问。

「咱们两边各有一条，」这位美国后生说，「离船差不多二十英里。」

===Original===
“I should think,” said somebody, “that a very slender submarine might slip in between our side kicks and us and do its regular job.”

“No chance,” the youth replied. “The convoy boats are used as decoys. The sub would see them first and spend all its ammunition.”

A little later he confided in me that the new American warships were two hundred and forty-five thousand horsepower. I had no idea there were that many horses left to measure by.

We spotted a shooting star. “That was a big one,” I said.

“Big! Do you know the actual size of those things? I got it straight from a professor of astronomy. Listen. They’re as small as a grain of sand.”

“Why do they look so big?”

“Because they’re so far away and they travel so fast.”

Round ten o’clock, beckoning lights ashore told us we were close to safety. But the French gunners remained at their posts two hours longer. The captain’s shouted order, relieving them from duty, was music to our ears.

===Chinese===
「要我说，」有人开口，「一条足够苗条的潜艇，完全可以从咱们的护航舰和咱们中间溜进来，干它的老本行。」

「门儿都没有，」后生答道，「护航船是拿来当诱饵的。潜艇会先看见它们，把弹药一股脑儿全打光。」

过了一会儿，他又凑过来向我透露：美国的新式军舰有二十四万五千马力。我原先真不知道世上还剩下那么多匹马可供计数。

我们看见一颗流星。「这颗个头不小。」我说。

「个头！你知道那玩意儿实际多大吗？我从一个天文学教授那儿得来的第一手消息。听着——它们只有一粒沙子那么点儿大。」

「那它们怎么看着那么大？」

「因为它们离得远，跑得又快。」

十点钟上下，岸上招手的灯火告诉我们，安全在望了。可法国炮手们又在炮位上多守了两个钟头。船长高声下达解除任务的口令，在我们听来不啻仙乐。

===Original===
After midnight, however, we turned a complete circle, and at once the deck was alive with rumors. We had been hit, we were going to be hit, we were afraid we would be hit, and so on. The fact was that our pilot from ashore was behind time and we circled round rather than stand still and be an easy target while awaiting him. We were in harbor and anchored at three. Many of us stayed up to see the sun rise over France. It was worth the sleep it cost.

They told us we would not dock until six tonight. Before retiring to my cabin for a nap, I heard we had run over a submarine and also that we had not. The latter story lacked heart interest, but had the merit, probably, of truth. Submarines have little regard for traffic laws, but are careful not to stall their engines in the middle of a boulevard.

===Chinese===
然而过了午夜，船兜了一个整圈，甲板上顿时谣言四起：我们已经中鱼雷了；我们就要中鱼雷了；我们怕是要中鱼雷了；等等等等。事实是，岸上来的引水员迟到了，我们与其停在水里干等、当现成的靶子，不如兜圈子玩儿。凌晨三点，我们进了港，抛了锚。我们当中不少人熬着不睡，就为看太阳从法兰西地平线上升起。搭上这点觉，值。

人家告诉我们，今晚六点之前别想靠岸。回舱打盹之前，我听说我们压过了一艘潜艇，又听说没压过。后一个说法缺乏人情味，但大概占着一个优点：真。潜艇这东西不大把交通规则放在眼里，不过倒还懂得别把引擎熄在大马路当间儿。

===Original===
I was peacefully asleep when the French officers came aboard to give us and our passports the Double O. They had to send to my cabin for me. I was ordered to appear at once in the salon de conversation. A barber hater addressed me through his beard and his interpreter: “What is Monsieur Laudanum’s business in France?”

I told him I was a correspondent.

“For who?”

“Mark Sullivan.”

“Have you credentials from him?”

“No, sir.”

“Your passport says you are going to Belgium. Do you know there are no trains to Belgium?”

“I know nothing about it.”

“Well, there are no trains. How will you go there?”

“I’ll try to get a taxi,” I said.

“Are you going from here to Paris?”

“Yes.”

“And where are you going from Paris?”

“I don’t know.”

“Please explain that answer.”

“I will go wherever the authorities permit me to go.”

“That is not a satisfactory answer.”

“I’m sorry.”

===Chinese===
法国军官们上船来，要对我们本人和我们的护照做「双 O 式」彻查（Double O）的时候，我正睡得安稳。他们只好派人到舱里来提我。我奉命即刻前往会客室（salon de conversation）。一位憎恨理发师的家伙，隔着他的大胡子和他的译员向我发话：「洛丹纳姆先生（Monsieur Laudanum，法国军官对 Lardner 的讹读）来法国有何贵干？」

我告诉他，我是记者。

「替谁跑腿？」

「马克·沙利文（Mark Sullivan）。」

「有他开的证明吗？」

「没有，长官。」

「你护照上写着要去比利时。你知道现在没有去比利时的火车吗？」

「这我倒不清楚。」

「反正没有火车。你打算怎么去？」

「我试试叫辆出租车。」我说。

「你从这儿去巴黎吗？」

「是。」

「从巴黎再上哪儿去？」

「不知道。」

「请解释一下这个回答。」

「当局准我去哪儿，我就去哪儿。」

「这个回答不能令人满意。」

「很遗憾。」

===Original===
“What is your real business in France?”

“To write.”

“I’m afraid we’ll have to keep your passport. You will appear tomorrow morning at nine o’clock at this address.”

And they handed me a scary-looking card.

On the deck I met our congressman and told him my troubles.

“I know these fellows very well,” he said. “If you like, I can fix it for you.”

“No,” I replied proudly. “I’d rather do my own fixing.”

At the dock I got into a taxi and asked to be taken to the —﻿—﻿— Hotel. Not to my dying day will I forget that first ride in a French taxi. Part of the time we were on the right side of the street, part of the time on the left, and never once were we traveling under a hundred and fifty miles an hour. We turned twenty corners and always on one ear. We grazed dozens of frightened pedestrians, many of them men crippled in the war, or by taxis, and women too old to dodge quickly. We aimed at a score of rickety horse-drawn vehicles, but our control was bad and we bumped only one. In front of the hostelry we stopped with a jerk.

“*Comme beaucoup?*” I asked the assassin.

“*Un franc cinquante*,” he said.

===Chinese===
「你在法国的真实差事到底是什么？」

「写作。」

「恐怕我们得扣下你的护照。明天上午九点，你到这个地址来报到。」

说着他们递给我一张看着就瘆人的卡片。

甲板上我遇见了我们那位国会议员，向他倒了我的苦水。

「这帮家伙我熟得很，」他说，「你要愿意，我可以替你摆平。」

「不必，」我骄傲地答道，「我宁可自己摆平自己。」

码头上我钻进一辆出租车，报了目的地：——饭店。这头一遭法国出租车之旅，我到死也忘不了。一路上，我们有时在街右边走，有时在街左边走，车速没有一刻在一百五十英里以下。我们拐了二十个弯，每个弯都是单边着地擦过去的。我们擦着了几十个魂飞魄散的行人——其中不少是战争致残的（也可能是被出租车致残的）汉子，还有躲闪不及的老太太。我们瞄准了二十来辆摇摇欲坠的马车冲过去，可惜车技欠佳，只撞上了一辆。到了饭店门口，我们一个急刹，收工。

「*Comme beaucoup*？（多少钱？——作者把 Combien 念岔了）」我问这位刺客。

「一个半法郎。」他说。

===Original===
Only thirty cents, and I thought I knew why. When they get through a trip without killing anyone, they feel they have not done themselves justice nor given you a square deal.

I found myself a seat at a sidewalk table and ordered sustenance. The vial they brought it in was labeled “*Bière Ritten*,” but I suspect the adjective was misspelled.

Till darkness fell I watched the passing show﻿—streetcars with lady motormen and conductors; hundreds of old carts driven by old women, each cart acting as a traveling roof for an old dog; wounded soldiers walking or hobbling along, some of them accompanied by sad-faced girls; an appalling number of women in black; a lesser number of gayly garbed and extremely cordial ones, and whole flocks of mad taxis, seeking whom they might devour.

===Chinese===
才三十美分，我想我知道为什么。但凡一趟跑下来没轧死人，他们就觉得自己没尽了本分，也亏待了你。

我在人行道边一张桌前落座，点了点续命的东西。端上来的小药瓶上贴着标签：「*Bière Ritten*（里滕啤酒）」，不过我疑心那个形容词拼错了（疑为 pourrie，「馊」的误拼）。

直到天黑，我一直在看街上的流动表演——电车司机和售票员全是女士；几百辆破旧板车由老妇人赶着，每辆车顶上都趴着一条老狗，随车旅行；伤兵们有的走、有的跛行，有的身边陪着愁容满面的姑娘；穿黑衣的女人多得吓人；穿得花枝招展、热络得过分的女人少一些；还有成群的疯出租车，四处逡巡，寻找可吞吃的人。

===Original===
By using great caution at the street crossings, I succeeded in reaching the telegraph office where I wrote a message informing Paris friends of my arrival. I presented it to the lady in the cage, who handed it back with the advice that it must be rewritten in French. I turned away discouraged and was starting out again into the gloom when I beheld at a desk the songbird of the ship. Would she be kind enough to do my translating? She would.

The clerk approved the new document, and asked for my passport. I told her it had been taken away. She was deeply grieved, then, but without it monsieur could send no message. *Bonne nuit!*

Back at the hotel I encountered the Yankee vice-consul, a gentleman from Bedford, Indiana. I told him my sad plight, and he said if matters got too serious his office would undertake to help.

With his assurances to comfort me, I have retired to my room to write, to my room as big as Texas and furnished with all the modern inconveniences.

===Chinese===
靠着在路口过街时十二万分的小心，我成功抵达电报局，写了一封电文，通知巴黎的朋友们我到了。我把电文递进窗口，里头的女士把它递了回来，并附忠告：必须改用法文书写。我灰溜溜地转身，正要重新扎进夜色，忽见一张桌前坐着船上那位女歌唱家。可否劳驾她替我翻译？可以。

文书的新版本获得了认可，她又要我的护照。我告诉她护照被扣了。她为此深感痛心——不过没有护照，先生就发不了电报。*Bonne nuit!*（晚安！）

回到饭店，我碰上了美国副领事，一位印第安纳州贝德福德（Bedford）来的绅士。我把自己的悲惨处境讲给他听，他说，事态若真闹得太严重，领事馆可以出面帮忙。

揣着他这句定心丸，我回房写日记来了——我的房间有得克萨斯州那么大，各类现代化不便设施一应俱全。

===Original===
Saturday, August 18. Paris.

It is Saturday night and they have hot water, but before I take advantage of it I must recount the thrilling experiences of the day.

After a sidewalk breakfast of “oofs” and so-called café in Bordeaux, I went to keep my engagement at court. It was apparent that I was not the only suspect. The walk outside and the room within were crowded with shipmates, most of them from the second cabin, all looking scared to death.

I stood in line till I realized that I must make it snappy if I wanted to catch the eleven-five for Paris; then I butted my way into the august presence of Him of the Beard.

===Chinese===
8 月 18 日，星期六。巴黎。

今晚是星期六，旅馆有热水了。不过在享受之前，我得先把今天这段惊心动魄的经历记下来。

在波尔多的人行道边用过早餐——「鸡蛋」（oofs，oeufs 的讹音）外加所谓的咖啡（café）——之后，我便去赴法庭之约了。显然，嫌疑人不止我一个。屋外的过道和屋里的房间都挤满了同船的旅伴，多数是二等舱的，个个吓得半死。

我排队排到一半，猛然醒悟：想赶上十一点零五分去巴黎的火车，就得抓紧。于是我横冲直撞，挤进了那位大胡子至尊的圣驾跟前。

===Original===
He recognized me at once and told me with his hands to go upstairs. In a room above I found the English-speaking cross-examiner, with the accent on the cross.

He waved me to a chair and began his offensive.

“Monsieur Laudanum,” he said, “when I asked you yesterday how you expected to get to Belgium, you said something about a taxi. That answer was not satisfactory. You have not explained anything to us. I do not believe we can allow you to leave Bordeaux.”

“All right, sir.” I arose.

“Sit down!” he barked. “Now tell me if you have any explanations to make.”

“Nothing beyond what I said yesterday. I have come here to write. I want to go to Paris, and when I arrive there I will find out where else I will be permitted to go.”

“It seems very strange to me that you have no papers.”

“Yes, sir.”

“Have you any?”

===Chinese===
他一眼认出了我，用手比划着叫我上楼。楼上一间屋里，我见到了那位说英语的盘问官——重音全落在「盘问」二字上。

他挥手让我落座，随即发动攻势。

「洛丹纳姆先生，」他说，「昨天我问你打算怎么去比利时，你扯了什么出租车。那个回答不能令人满意。你什么都没向我们交代清楚。我看，我们不能放你离开波尔多。」

「好的，长官。」我站起身。

「坐下！」他喝道，「现在说吧，你还有什么要解释的。」

「除了昨天说的，没什么了。我来这儿是为了写作。我要去巴黎，到了那儿，自然会打听清楚还准我去别处。」

「你一点证明文件都没有，这在我看来非常蹊跷。」

「是的，长官。」

「到底有没有？」

===Original===
I searched my pockets and produced a used-up check book on a Chicago bank. The ogre read every little stub and I felt flattered by his absorbed interest. When he had spent some five minutes on the last one, which recorded a certain painful transaction between me and a man-eating garage, he returned my book and said: “You don’t satisfy me at all. You will have to stay here.”

“Suppose,” said I, “that the American consul vouches for me.”

“That will make no difference. You do not seem to realize that we are at war.”

“Not with America.”

“I don’t know your nationality.”

“I thought,” said I, “that my passport hinted at it.”

“You will have to stay in Bordeaux,” was his pertinent reply.

“Thank you, sir,” I said, and arose again.

“Sit down,” said he, “and wait a minute.”

He was out of the room five years.

“If he ever does come back,” I thought, “it will be in the company of five or six large gendarmes.”

But when he came back he came alone.

“Here,” he said abruptly, “is your passport. You will be permitted to go to Paris. We will keep track of you there.” And he bowed me out of the joint.

===Chinese===
我摸遍口袋，掏出一本芝加哥某银行用罄的支票簿。这位吃人的妖魔把每张存根都读了一遍，他读得那样入神，我不禁有些受宠若惊。最后一张存根记的是我和一家吃人的车库之间某笔惨痛交易，他在这上面足足花了五分钟，然后把支票簿还给我，说：「你完全不能让我满意。你得留在这儿。」

「假如，」我说，「美国领事愿意为我担保呢？」

「那也不会有什么两样。你似乎没有意识到，我们在打仗。」

「不是跟美国打。」

「我并不知道你的国籍。」

「我原以为，」我说，「我的护照已经暗示过了。」

「你必须留在波尔多。」这是他切题的回答。

「谢谢您，长官。」我说着，再次起身。

「坐下，」他说，「再等一会儿。」

他出了那间屋，一去五年。

「他要真回来，」我心想，「准是带着五六个五大三粗的宪兵。」

可他回来时，是独自一人。

「喏，」他没头没脑地说，「你的护照。准你去巴黎。到了那边，我们会盯着你的。」说罢，他把我恭恭敬敬送出了那个衙门。

===Original===
The crowd downstairs seemed as great as ever, and as scared. I picked my way through it with my head held high, a free man.

I decided on a fiacre for my trip from hotel to station. It would be safer, I thought. But I learned, on our interminable way, that defensive fighting in the streets of Bordeaux is far more terrifying, far more dangerous than the aggressive taxi kind. We were run into twice and just missed more times than I could count, and besides my conveyance was always on the verge of a nervous breakdown. ’Spite all the talk of periscopes and subs, the journey across the ocean was parlor croquet compared to my fiacre ride in Bordeaux.

While awaiting my turn at the ticket window I observed at the gate a French soldier wearing a large businesslike bayonet. “Probably to punch tickets with,” I thought, but was mistaken. Another gentleman attended to that duty, and the soldier did not give me so much as the honor of a glance.

Outside on the platform were a few of the Red Cross and Y.M.C.A. men of our ship, and I learned from them that one of their number had suffered a sadder fate than I. He had tried to get by on a Holland passport, viséed at the French consulate in New York, and been quietly but firmly persuaded to take the next boat back home.

I shared a compartment on the train with a native of the Bronx, and a French lady who just couldn’t make her eyes behave, and two bored-looking French gentlemen of past middle age, not to mention in detail much more baggage than there was room for. The lady and the two gentlemen wore gloves, which made the Bronxite and me feel very bourgeois.

Our train crew, with the possible exception of the engineer and fireman whom I didn’t see, was female, and, thinking I might some time require the services of the porter, I looked in my dictionary for the feminine of George.

===Chinese===
楼下的人群还是那么多，还是那么吓。我昂首挺胸、侧身其间挤了出去——一个自由人。

从饭店去车站，我决定改乘出租马车（fiacre）。我想总稳妥些吧。可在这段没完没了的路途上我才领教：波尔多街头的防御性作战，比出租车那种进攻性打法吓人得多，也凶险得多。我们被人撞了两次，险些被撞的次数多到我数不过来，外加我这辆车驾随时处在精神崩溃的边缘。任凭人家把潜望镜和潜艇吹得天花乱坠，比起我在波尔多坐的这趟马车，横渡大洋充其量算是客厅里玩门球。

在售票窗口排队时，我瞧见检票口立着一名法国兵，挎着一柄颇具专业范儿的大刺刀。「大概是拿来剪票的。」我想。可我想错了。剪票另有专人负责，那位大兵连赏我一瞥的荣幸都没施舍。

站台上站着几位同船的红十字会和基督教青年会（Y.M.C.A.）先生，我从他们口中得知，他们当中有一位比我命还苦。他拿着一本荷兰护照想蒙混过关——护照上倒是有纽约法国领事馆的签证——结果被人客客气气但毫不含糊地劝上了下一班回美国的船。

火车包厢里，跟我同处一室的有：一位布朗克斯土著、一位眼珠子怎么也不肯守规矩的法国女士、两位一脸百无聊赖的过中年法国绅士，此外还有多得没处塞的行李，就不一一细表了。女士和那两位绅士都戴着手套，弄得布朗克斯土著和我自觉布尔乔亚得很。

我们这列车的工作人员，除了我没见着的司机和司炉可能例外，清一色是女性。我寻思自己兴许哪天用得上搬运工的服务，便翻开词典，查「乔治」一词的阴性形式。

===Original===
To try my knowledge of *française*, I had purchased at the station a copy of *Le Cri de Paris*. I found that I could read it very easily by consulting the dictionary every time I came to a word.

But the scenery and the people were more interesting than *Le Cri*, the former especially. Perfect automobile roads, lined with trees; fields, and truck gardens in which aged men and women, young girls and little boys were at work; green hills and valleys; winding rivers and brooks, and an occasional château or a town of fascinating architecture﻿—these helped to make us forget the heat and dust of the trip and the earsplitting shrieks of our engines. No wonder the boche coveted his neighbor’s house.

We stopped for some time at one particularly beautiful town and went out for air. I wondered audibly concerning the name of the place. An American companion looked at the signs round the station.

“It’s *Sortie*,” he said.

But it wasn’t. It was Angoulême, and I wouldn’t mind moving thither. My American friend was probably from Exit, Michigan.

===Chinese===
为了检验我的法语（française）水平，我在车站买了一本《巴黎呐喊》（*Le Cri de Paris*）。我发现读起来毫无难度——只需每碰上一个词就查一遍词典。

可风景和人比《巴黎呐喊》有意思多了，风景尤甚。平整的公路两旁绿树成行；田野和菜园里，老头老太、大姑娘小小子都在劳作；青山幽谷；蜿蜒的河流与小溪；间或一座城堡，或一座建筑迷人的小镇——这一切帮我们忘掉了旅途的酷热与尘土，忘掉了机车那撕耳朵的尖叫。难怪德国佬（boche）要觊觎邻舍的房屋了。

我们在一座格外漂亮的小镇停了些时候，下车透气。我出声嘀咕这地方叫什么名目。一位美国同伴端详了一圈车站周围的招牌。

「叫 *Sortie*（法语「出口」）。」他说。

可惜不是。那是昂古莱姆（Angoulême），让我搬过去住我也乐意。我这位美国朋友，八成是从密歇根州艾克西特（Exit，英语「出口」）来的。

===Original===
The discovery was made and reported that one might go into the dining-car and smoke as much as one liked without asking permission from the maiden with the dreamy eyes. This car was filled with French soldiers and officers going back to the front after their holiday. There seemed to be as many different uniforms as there were men, and the scenery indoors was almost as brilliant as that outside.

It was about eight-thirty in the evening when we reached Paris. The sophisticated soldiers engaged their “redcaps” before they left the train, calling to them through the open windows. The demand was much greater than the supply, and I was among the unfortunates who had to carry their own baggage. I staggered to a street where a whole flotilla of taxis was anchored, but when I asked for one the person in charge said “No, no, no, no, no,” meaning “No,” and pointed around the corner. I followed his directions and landed on a boulevard along which there was a steady procession of machines, but it was fully twenty minutes before one came that was going slow enough to stop.

Our city is not all lit up like a church these nights, and it was impossible to see much of what we passed on the way to the hotel.

At the desk an English clerk, dressed for a noon wedding, gave me a blank to fill out. All the blank wanted to know was my past family history. It is to be sent, said the clerk, to the prefect of police. I had no idea he was interested in me.

===Chinese===
有人发现并传播了一条消息：餐车可以随便进，烟可以随便抽，无须请示那位眼神迷离的姑娘。这节车厢里坐满了休完假重返前线的法国官兵。军装的款式似乎一人一个样，车内的景致差不多跟窗外一样绚丽。

到巴黎时是晚上八点半光景。老练的士兵们人还没下车，就隔着敞开的车窗喊话，预订好了各自的「红帽子」搬运工。狼多肉少，我便沦入了只能自己扛行李的不幸者之列。我跌跌撞撞挪到一条街上，那儿停泊着一整支出租车舰队，可我一开口要车，管事的说「不、不、不、不、不」，意思是「不」，然后朝街角一指。我按他的指引走下去，落身在一条大道上，车流滚滚络绎不绝，足足过了二十分钟，才等来一辆慢得足以停下的。

咱们这座城市近来夜里可不像教堂那样灯火通明，去饭店的一路看不清什么东西。

柜台前，一位英国职员——穿得像要去参加正午的婚礼——递给我一张表格填。表格想知道的，无非是我的家族史。职员说，这表要呈交警察总监（préfet）。我原先竟不知道他对本人感兴趣。

===Original===
Sunday, August 19. Paris.

When I get back to Chicago I shall insist that my favorite restaurant place tables out on the walk. It is more hygienic and much more interesting.

But Chicago, I’m afraid, can’t provide half as much sidewalk entertainment as Paris. As I remember the metropolis of Illinois, there is a sad lack there of demonstrative affection on the streets. In fact, I fear that a lady and gentleman who kissed each other repeatedly at the corner of Madison and Dearborn would be given a free ride to Central Station and a few days in which to cool off. Such an osculatory duel on Paris’s Grand Boulevard﻿—also known by a dozen other names﻿—goes practically unnoticed except by us Illinois hicks.

An American officer and I﻿—at the former’s expense﻿—lunched sur curb today. The food was nothing to boast about, but we got an eyeful of scenery. Soldiers﻿—French, British and American﻿—strolled by constantly, accompanied by more or less beautiful brunettes, and only a few were thoughtless enough not to stop and kiss a few times in full view of our table. We also observed the inmates of passing taxis. No matter how wide the back seat, the lady occupant invariably sat on her escort’s lap. A five-passenger car in America is a ten-passenger car in Paris, provided the chauffeur has a girl of his own.

When the American officer was tired of buying, I left him and sought out the *Chicago Tribune* office, conveniently located above *Maxim*’s. The editor was there, but he was also broke, so I went back to the Ritz and got ready for bed.

The express office will be open tomorrow and I will be a rich man.

===Chinese===
8 月 19 日，星期日。巴黎。

等我回到芝加哥，一定力劝我最青睐的那家饭馆把桌子摆到人行道上去。更卫生，也有趣得多。

可惜芝加哥能提供的人行道余兴节目，恐怕连巴黎的一半都不到。凭我对这座伊利诺伊大都会的记忆，街头公开表露爱意的情况少得可怜。老实说，我担心一对男女要是在麦迪逊街和迪尔伯恩街的街角反复亲嘴，就会被免费专车送到中央警察站，再关上几天冷静冷静。这样一场接吻拉锯战，若搬到巴黎的大林荫大道上——此道另有十来个别名——简直无人留意，只有我们伊利诺伊乡下佬才大惊小怪。

今天我和一位美国军官——由前者买单——在路牙子上（sur curb）用了午餐。饭菜没什么可吹的，风景倒是看饱了。士兵们——法国的、英国的、美国的——络绎不绝地打眼前遛达过去，身边陪着美貌程度不等的黑发女郎；只有寥寥几位没心没肺，忘了在咱们餐桌全视野之内停下来亲上几口。我们还观察了过往出租车里的乘员。后座无论多宽，女乘客一概坐在男伴的腿上。美国坐五人的车，在巴黎能坐十人——前提是司机自己也有一位姑娘。

等那位美国军官买累了单，我便离开他，去寻《芝加哥论坛报》（*Chicago Tribune*）的办公处，位置便利，就在马克西姆（*Maxim*）饭店楼上。编辑在是在了，可他跟我一样身无分文，于是我折回里兹饭店，准备就寝。

快递汇兑处明天开门，届时我就是一个有钱人了。

===Original===
Lundi, 20 Août. Paris.

Went down to the express office and cashed a large part of my order. Friends were with me, and they immediately relieved me of most of the burden. I was hungry for lunch, having had no breakfast. Meat was what I wanted, and meat was what I couldn’t get. Which led me to inquire into the Rules *de la vie* of Paris.

1. Monday and Tuesday are meatless days.
2. All except Saturday and Sunday are heatless days. Hot baths are impossible on Mondays, Tuesdays, Wednesdays, Thursdays and Fridays.
3. Strong liquor is procurable between noon and two p.m. and seven-thirty and nine-thirty at night. At other times ye toper must be content with light wines.
4. All public places except the theaters must close and douse lights at nine-thirty in the evening.

===Chinese===
8 月 20 日，星期一（Lundi, 20 Août）。巴黎。

下楼去了汇兑处，把汇款单兑现了大半。朋友们陪我同志，旋即替我卸下了大半负担。我没吃早饭，饿着肚子巴望午饭。我想吃的是肉，偏吃不着的就是肉。这就促使我研究起巴黎的生活规则（Rules *de la vie*）来。

1. 星期一、星期二是无肉日。
2. 除星期六、星期日外均为无暖日。星期一、二、三、四、五洗不成热水澡。
3. 烈酒可购时段为正午至下午两点、晚七点半至九点半。其余时辰，酒客诸君只好屈就低度葡萄酒。
4. 除剧院外，一切公共场所晚九点半须闭门熄灯。

===Original===
5. There is no speed limit for taxis or privately owned cars. A pedestrian run over and killed is liable to imprisonment. The driver is not only innocent, but free to hurl as many French curses as he likes at his victims. If the pedestrian is not killed, he must explain why not to the judge.
6. It is not only permissible but compulsory to speak to any girl who speaks to you, and a girl who won’t speak to you should be reported to the police.
7. No watch or clock is wrong. Whatever time you have is right and you may act accordingly.
8. Matches never ignite. A smoker must purchase a cigar or cigarette lighter and keep it filled with essence, the *française* term for gas. Sometimes the lighters work.
9. American cigarettes are not procurable. Bum ones may be bought at any tabac store or café for only five times what they are worth.
10. Water must never be used as a thirst quencher, and seldom for any other purpose. It’s worse than bourgeois; it’s unheard-of.

===Chinese===
5. 出租车及私用车不限速。被轧身亡的行人须负监禁之责。司机不但无罪，还可随意朝受害者倾倒任意数量的法语咒骂。行人若未被轧死，须向法官解释为何不死。
6. 凡有姑娘跟你搭话，你不但可以而且必须回话；不肯跟你搭话的姑娘，应报告警察。
7. 天下没有走错的表和钟。你手头是什么时间，什么时间就是对的，尽管照此行事。
8. 火柴永远划不着。吸烟人士须自购雪茄或香烟打火机，并持续灌满 essence——即法语的「汽油」。打火机有时能打着。
9. 美国香烟无处可购。劣质烟倒可在任意一家烟草店或咖啡馆买到，价格仅为其实际价值的五倍。
10. 水绝不可用作解渴之物，旁的用途也少。这比资产阶级还糟；简直是闻所未闻。

===Original===
The lack of water, hot or cold, drove me to a barber shop this morning. The barber first made me put on a shroud, and I was afraid he was either going to cut me to pieces or talk me to death. But his operation was absolutely painless and his incessant conversation harmless, because I couldn’t understand a word of it.

From the barber shop I went to the information department of American Army Headquarters. That’s where you get permits to visit our camps. But of course, if you’ve run over here from America, you have lots of spare time on your hands, so they’re doing you a favor if they hold you up a few days. What is a week or so when a man’s here for a whole month?

They have queer ideas at the Maison de la Presse, which is the French equivalent for our publicity bureau. They receive you cordially there and treat you just as if you were not dregs.

I jumped thither after a futile visit to our own headquarters. I said I would like to go to the French front.

“Certainly,” replied the man in charge. “Whenever is convenient for you, we’ll see that you get a trip.”

So I told him when it would be convenient and he’s going to see me through. I hear that the British are similarly peculiar. They are polite even to newspaper men and magazine writers. They might even speak to a cartoonist.

===Chinese===
正是热水的缺失——冷水的亦然——今早把我赶进了理发店。理发师先给我围上一件裹尸布，我一度担心他要把我零剐碎切，或者用话把我活活说死。好在他的手术全然无痛，他滔滔不绝的谈话也全然无害，因为我一个字也听不懂。

出了理发店，我去了美国陆军总部的问讯处。参观咱们兵营的许可就是在那儿办。不过当然啦，你既然大老远从美国跑来，手头有的是闲工夫，人家留你几天，那是赏你的脸。一个人来整整一个月嘛，耗上一星期算个什么？

新闻部（Maison de la Presse）那边的想法就很古怪了——那是法国版的我们的宣传局。人家在那儿热忱接待你，待你就仿佛你不是社会渣滓似的。

在自家总部白跑一趟之后，我便一头扎到了那边。我说我想去法国前线。

「当然可以，」主管人答道，「您什么时候方便，我们保证给您安排一趟。」

于是我告诉了他我什么时候方便，他就要一路送我成行了。听说英国人也有这种怪毛病。他们对报纸记者和杂志撰稿人居然也讲礼貌。没准儿连漫画家都肯搭话。

===Original===
Returning to our side of the Seine, I bumped into some Australians, here on leave. One had been in Germany before the war and could speak and understand the “*schoenste* language.”

“They use me as an interpreter,” he said. “When they bring in a bloody boche prisoner, I talk to him. First we give him a real meal, maybe bacon and eggs and coffee, something he hasn’t seen for months. Then I ask him where he came from and how he got here. Most of them are glad to tell me the truth. Those that do, I mark them down as ‘Very intelligent.’ Those that volunteer information I record as ‘Extremely intelligent.’ Those that say ‘*Nicht verstehe*’ go down in the record as ‘Not intelligent.’ But the majority are so bloody well glad to be out of the war that they talk freely.

“I asked one Heinie if he was going to try to escape. ‘Not me,’ he said, ‘I’m tickled to be here.’ They’re all fed up on the war. You’d be too with three years of it.”

This young man admitted that he was one of the best football players in Australia. “Maybe I’ve forgotten how now,” he said. “I’ve been over here three years. Just think of it﻿—I traveled twelve thousand miles, or maybe it’s kilos, to mix up in this.”

Baseball, he told me, had taken a strong hold on Australia.

“I don’t hit well,” he said, “but I can catch what you call flies! I can catch the widest flies that are knocked.”

Which gift would probably be useless in America, where most of the flies knocked are bloody narrow.

===Chinese===
回到塞纳河我们这一岸，我撞见了几个来休假的澳大利亚人。其中一位战前去过德国，会说也听得懂那种「*schoenste*（德语「最美」）的语言」。

「他们拿我当翻译使，」他说，「押来一个该死的德国佬俘虏，就由我来问。先给他来一顿正经饭，兴许是培根鸡蛋加咖啡——他几个月没见过的东西。然后我问他是哪儿人、怎么到这儿来的。大多数人乐意跟我说实话。说实话的，我登记为『很聪明』。主动交代的，我记作『极其聪明』。只会说『*Nicht verstehe*（听不懂）』的，记录在案为『不聪明』。不过大多数人他妈的高兴坏了，总算脱离了那场战争，所以问什么说什么。

「我问过一个海尼（Heinie，对德国兵的称呼）想不想逃跑。『我才不呢，』他说，『待在这儿我乐死了。』他们都吃腻了这场战争。换你打上三年，也一样。」

这位年轻人承认，自己曾是澳大利亚最顶尖的足球运动员之一。「如今兴许手生了，」他说，「我到这边三年了。你想想——我跑了一万二千英里，还是一万二千公里来着，就为了掺和进这档子事。」

他还告诉我，棒球在澳大利亚相当风行。

「我击球不行，」他说，「可你们说的那种『高飞球』（flies，亦指苍蝇）我能接！再宽的高飞球打出来我都能接住。」

可惜这本事到了美国多半用不上——那边被打出去的高飞球，大都不他妈的够宽。

===Original===
Before I left him I learned also that Les Darcy was all right at heart, but that the professional “sports” spoiled him, and that he could have “knocked Jack Johnson, Stanley Ketchel, Billy Papke or Jess Willard clean out of the ring.”

He is going back to the trenches tonight, and I hope there are plenty of extremely intelligent Heinies there to keep him busy interpreting till his next leave. Interpreting, I should think, would be much pleasanter than going over the top.

Tuesday, August 21.

This time it was an American of the French Ambulance Service.

“Say, listen,” he said. “I can give you some mighty good stories. Real stuff, do you get me? Listen: One night there was a boche wounded out there and I brought him in. He had one leg all shot to pieces and we had to operate. I was going to give him the ether when he turned over and looked me in the face. ‘Why, Dan,’ he said, ‘aren’t you going to speak to me?’ It was a chap I’d gone to school with in America. I could give you lots of stuff like that; do you get me? I used to be in New York, and Rube Goldberg used to call me up out of bed at six in the morning. ‘Dan,’ he’d say to me, ‘I’m up against it for an idea. Will you give me an idea?’ Do you get me? And there’s a dramatic critic in New York﻿—I won’t tell you his name﻿—but he used to tag around me after a first night and ask me what I thought of the show. Do you get me? I can give you a lot of good stuff.”

I told him I was afraid that if he gave it to me all at once I wouldn’t remember any of it. So he is coming to my hotel every day during his leave, to give me a little at a time﻿—if he can find me.

===Chinese===
跟他分手之前，我又获悉：莱斯·达西（Les Darcy）心眼儿不坏，可惜让职业「体育圈」那帮人带坏了；还获悉他本来能「把杰克·约翰逊（Jack Johnson）、斯坦利·凯切尔（Stanley Ketchel）、比利·帕普克（Billy Papke）或者杰斯·威拉德统统打出拳台」。

他今晚就要回战壕去了。我祝愿那边「极其聪明」的海尼多得是，够他从早忙到晚地翻译，一直忙到下次休假。依我看，做翻译总比爬出战壕冲锋舒服多了。

8 月 21 日，星期二。

这回轮到一位法国救护队的美国人。

「哎，听着，」他说，「我能给你一些特棒的素材。真事儿，懂我意思吧？听着：有天夜里，外头一个德国佬挂了彩，是我把他抬回来的。他一条腿被打得稀烂，得动手术。我正要给他上乙醚，他翻过身来盯着我的脸。『哎呀，丹，』他说，『你不跟我打个招呼吗？』那是我在美国一起上学的老同学。这样的料我还能给你一大堆；懂我意思吧？我从前在纽约，鲁布·戈德堡（Rube Goldberg）老是大清早六点把我从床上电话薅起来。『丹，』他会说，『我憋不出点子了。赏我一个点子行不行？』懂我意思吧？纽约还有位剧评家——名字我就不告诉你了——每回首演夜，他总跟在我屁股后头，问我那戏怎么样。懂我意思吧？我好料多得很。」

我对他说，这些好料要是一股脑全给我，只怕我一样也记不住。于是他打算休假期间每天来饭店一趟，每次喂我一点儿——前提是他找得着我。

===Original===
Last night a good-hearted American officer took me to dinner at La Tour d’Argent, which is said to be the oldest restaurant in Paris and which, they say, is the place the Kaiser was going to have his banquet on a certain night three years ago if Gott hadn’t gone back on him at the last moment.

We ordered duck, the restaurant’s specialty. They cook it in your presence, slice off whatever is sliceable, and then put the bird in a press and give you the result as gravy. After the meal they hand you a post card on which is inscribed *le numéro de votre canard*. I looked up “*canard*” in my dictionary and found that it meant a drake, or false news, or a worthless newspaper. I have heard lots of false news, but I know no one took the trouble to count the items. Also I know that my newspaper is neither worthless nor numbered. So *canard* in this case must mean drake. The number of mine was 41654. If he had happened to disagree with me, I could have taken his number and traced him to the source. It’s a very good idea and might be used in America on eggs or drinks.

I made another trip to the office which is supposed to be in charge of American correspondents and accommodations for them. I will go there again tomorrow and again the next day. I will bother them to death. Meantime I have applied to a person in London for permission to go to the British front, and have been assured a visit to the French lines late next week. I have wonderful vision and can see things twelve miles away.

P.S. It was revealed to me tonight that my detention and trial in Bordeaux was a frame-up conceived by loving friends aboard ship and carried out by that English-speaking cross-examiner, who, believe me, is a convincing actor.

Thanks, gents. It was good for about two thousand words.

===Chinese===
昨晚，一位好心的美国军官带我去了银塔饭店（La Tour d’Argent）晚餐。据说这是巴黎最老的馆子；据说三年前的某个夜晚，德皇本要在此设庆功宴，多亏上帝（Gott）在最后关头食了言。

我们点了这家的招牌鸭子。他们当着你的面烹制，把能片下来的都片下来，然后把整只鸟儿放进压榨机里榨，榨出的汁当作肉汁奉客。饭后递给你一张明信片，上面印着 *le numéro de votre canard*（你的鸭子的编号）。我翻了词典，查得 canard 一词意为公鸭，或假新闻，或一文不值的小报。假新闻我听过一大堆，但我知道没人费心去数过条数。我也知道我供职的报纸既非一文不值，也未曾编号。所以此处的 canard 必是公鸭无疑。我那只的编号是 41654。他要是碰巧跟我合不来，我可以记下他的编号，顺藤摸瓜追到源头。这主意委实不坏，美国不妨用来编鸡蛋或酒水。

我又跑了一趟那个据说主管美国记者及其食宿事宜的办公处。明天还去，后天也去。我烦也要把他们烦死。与此同时，我已向伦敦某君申请前往英国前线的许可，并且得了准信儿：下周后半段可访问法军防线。我视力好极了，十二英里外的东西都看得见。

又及：今晚有人向我揭底，我在波尔多被扣押受审一事，乃船上亲爱的朋友们共同策划、由那位说英语的盘问官一手执行的圈套。信我一句，那位的演技，说服力十足。

多谢各位仁兄。这一段够写两千来个词了。

## III I Try to Get to the American Camp﻿—But Meet Disaster / III 我想去美军营地——却遭了难

===Original===
Wednesday, August 22. Paris.

The gentlemen authorized to issue visitors’ passes to the American camp and the various fronts don’t seem to realize that a person may be in a hurry. They fail to appreciate the facts that hanging round Paris is financial ruin and that the world series, which one positively must attend, is drawing nearer every hour.

Permission to go to the British front was requested over a week ago. No reply. Daily calls at our own press bureau produce nothing but promises of a trip somewhere, some time. Monsieur Boss of the French Maison de la Presse says I may be taken through the devastated territory﻿—in a week or so.

Meanwhile the Battle of Paris goes on, with Death always staring one in the face﻿—Death from taxis, from starvation, from water thirst, from hand-to-hand encounters with the language.

===Chinese===
8 月 22 日，星期三。巴黎。

那些有权签发美军营地和各条前线采访证的先生们，似乎意识不到一个人可能是赶时间的。他们体会不到这样两个事实：一是泡在巴黎闲逛等于财政上的慢性自毁，二是那场绝对不容错过的世界大赛，正一个钟头一个钟头地逼近。

去英国前线的申请是一周多以前递上去的。没有回音。天天往我们自己的新闻处跑，跑出来的只有许诺：什么时候带你去个什么地方。法国新闻部（Maison de la Presse）的那位老板大人说，兴许能带我走一趟饱受战火摧残的地区——大约一星期之后吧。

与此同时，巴黎战役继续进行，死神总在瞪着你——出租车的死法，饿死的死法，渴死的死法，还有跟法语白刃肉搏的死法。

===Original===
Death from a taxi is the most likely form and the most distressing, for under the Parisian law the person run down and killed is the one at fault and the corpus delicti is liable to life imprisonment or worse. A pedestrian has no more rights here than the Kaiser, and it’s almost impossible to cross the street unless you’ve gone through a course of intensive training in Detroit.

There would be little danger if all the crossings were on the upgrade, for the French cars﻿—those which aren’t in the military service﻿—have a desperate time climbing. They have to shift speeds even to run up on the sidewalk, which is one of their favorite sports. But the Loop District of Paris is topographically on the level, and taxis can tear along like an eastbound Russian.

On occasions when you are run into and knocked down a gendarme appears on the scene with pencil and notebook. He takes the name and address of the driver and escorts you to jail. If you die there, the driver is sent a medal for marksmanship.

Taxi fares are cheaper, probably, than anywhere else in the world. They amount to practically nothing if you have an accident﻿—that is, a trip without a collision with something or somebody. But even if you enjoy an average tour and hit a building or another vehicle or a dog or a person, they soak you only about half as much as they would in New York or Chicago, where there are far fewer thrills per drive.

The tariff from the hotel where I put up (I haven’t found out how much) to American General Headquarters, where I go every morning to be refused a pass to the camps, is one *franc cinquante* if you miss all targets. This forenoon it was two *francs cinquante* because we knocked the rear wheel off a young boy’s bicycle.

The boy, after a hearty bawling out by the driver and two gendarmes, was carted to a police station. They’ll hardly keep him in jail, though. Matteawan is the proper place for a boy who attempts bicycling on the streets of Paris.

===Chinese===
死于出租车是最可能的死法，也最令人痛心，因为按巴黎的法律，被撞倒撞死的那个人才是有过错的一方，那具尸体（corpus delicti）还得被判无期徒刑甚至更重。行人在此地享有的权利不比德皇多，想过马路几乎不可能——除非你在底特律受过强化训练。

要是所有路口都设在坡上，危险就微乎其微了，因为法国的汽车——那些不在军中服役的——爬坡爬得死去活来。它们连上人行道都得换挡，而上人行道正是它们最喜爱的运动之一。可惜巴黎的闹市区（相当于芝加哥的卢普区）地势偏偏平坦，出租车可以像东行的俄国人一样一路狂奔。

偶尔你被撞倒了，一名警察会带着铅笔和小本子赶到现场。他记下司机的大名和住址，然后把你押送进监狱。你要是死在里头，司机就会领到一枚神射手奖章。

出租车费大概比世界上任何地方都便宜。如果你一路太平——也就是这趟车没撞上任何东西或任何人——车费差不多等于白给。但即便你享受了一趟平均水准的车程，撞上一栋楼、另一辆车、一条狗或一个人，他们收你的钱也不过纽约或芝加哥的一半，而那两个城市每一趟车能享受的刺激要少得多。

从我下榻的饭店（房钱多少我还没弄清）到美军总司令部的车费——我每天早上去那儿吃一回闭门羹，申请去营地的通行证——要是一枪未中、什么目标都没撞着，是一个法郎五十（*franc cinquante*）。今天上午是两个法郎五十（*francs cinquante*），因为我们撞掉了一个小男孩自行车的后轮。

那男孩挨了司机和两名警察的一顿臭骂之后，被拉到了警察局。不过他们多半不会关他。想在巴黎街头上骑自行车的男孩，正经该送去的地方是马蒂万（Matteawan，纽约州收疯子的去处）。

===Original===
Thursday, August 23. Paris.

One of several differences between an American and a Frenchman is that an American tries to understand a Frenchman’s English and a Frenchman tries not to understand an American’s French.

Today I wanted to go from somewhere to the Hotel Continental.

“Hotel Con-tin-ent-al,” I said to the driver.

He shook his head. I repeated. He shook his head again. This went on till I had pronounced the name five times and he had shaken his head that often. I said it the sixth time just as I had said it the other five.

“Oh-h-h!” shouted the driver, his face lighting up. “Hotel Con-tin-ent-al!”

And there wasn’t a particle of difference between his version and mine.

===Chinese===
8 月 23 日，星期四。巴黎。

美国人和法国人的诸多区别之一是：美国人努力听懂法国人的英语，法国人努力听不懂美国人的法语。

今天我想从某个地方去大陆饭店（Hôtel Continental）。

「Hotel Con-tin-ent-al。」我对司机说。

他摇头。我又说一遍。他又摇头。如此这般，直到我把这名字念了五遍、他摇了五回头。我第六次说这个词，跟前五次说得一模一样。

「噢——！」司机喊了起来，脸上豁然开朗。「Hotel Con-tin-ent-al！」

而他的念法跟我的念法，连一丁点儿差别都没有。

===Original===
There was excitement in our village last night. At twenty-three-thirty o’clock, as we Parisians say, began a chorus of screaming sirens, the warning signal of an air raid. Those of us living in upstairs rooms experienced a sudden craving for a home Somewhere in the Basement, and in gratifying it didn’t stop to use the elevator. The majority taking part in the Great Descent wore pajamas or their female relatives, sometimes called *chemises de nuit*. A few, of which I was one, were still attired for the day, and we went outdoors and looked up.

A regular flock of planes was, you might say, planely visible, but there was no fight in the air and no dropping of bombs on our fair city. The birdmen soared round a while in a perfectly friendly manner and then retired to their nests. The sirens were stilled and we all went upstairs, the majority, mentioned above, grateful for the wartime lack of lights.

It seems that a Frenchman, returning from his day’s toil, forgot to flash his password, which is a red taillight, or something. And the patrol took him for a boche and gave chase. Fortunately for himself, he glimpsed his pursuers in time and turned on the required signal.

Today there has been a big demand for first-floor rooms.

===Chinese===
昨天晚上，我们村出了件热闹事。二十三点半钟——我们巴黎人都这么报时——一串尖啸的警报器齐声开嗓，那是空袭警报。我们这些住楼上的人突然强烈渴望一个「地下室某处」的家，为了满足这份渴望，下楼时都顾不上坐电梯了。参加这次「大下楼」的大多数人穿着睡衣，或者睡衣的女性亲属——学名 *chemises de nuit*（女式睡袍）。少数人——我是其中之一——还穿着白天的行头，我们就走到屋外，抬头看天。

好一大群飞机，可以说是明摆（planely）着看得见，但空中并没有交战，我们这座美丽的城市也没挨炸。那些飞行家们一团和气地盘旋了一会儿，便回巢去了。警报器哑了，我们全都上了楼，上面提到的那大多数人对战时的灯火管制心怀感激。

据说是一个法国人下班回家，忘了闪他的通行暗号——就是红色尾灯什么的。巡逻队把他当成了德国佬，追了上去。所幸他自己及时发现追兵，赶紧亮出了规定的信号。

今天一楼房间大抢手。

===Original===
Friday, August 24. Paris.

An American major﻿—it is interdict by the censor to mention the names of any officers save General Sibert and General Pershing﻿—asked a friend in London to buy him an automobile and ship it here for his use. The Londoner was able, after much difficulty, to purchase one of those things that grow so rapidly in Detroit. He packed it up and mailed it to Le Havre. From there it had to be driven to Paris.

The major had never learned to drive this particular brand. In fact, his proportions are such that not even a shoehorn could coax him into the helmsman’s seat. He asked me to go up and get it for him. I declined on grounds of neutrality. That was a week ago.

Well, yesterday one Mr. Kiley, who has been over here some time in the ambulance service, came back to town with the car and four flat tires, which, evidently, were far past the draft age when the sale was made in London. Mr. Kiley helped himself to a stimulant and then told me about his trip.

===Chinese===
8 月 24 日，星期五。巴黎。

一位美国少校——检查官禁止提及除西伯特将军和潘兴将军以外任何军官的大名——托伦敦的朋友替他买一辆汽车运到法国来自用。那位伦敦人费了九牛二虎之力，买到一件在底特律长得飞快的那种东西。他把它打上包，邮寄到勒阿弗尔。从那儿起，还得把它一路开到巴黎。

这位少校从来没学过开这个牌子。实际上，就他的体格而言，哪怕拿鞋拔子也塞不进舵手席。他便叫我去把车取回来。我以中立为由谢绝了。那是一个星期以前的事。

结果呢，昨天一位基利先生——他在救护队已经干了一阵子——带着那辆车和四条没气的轮胎回了城，那四条轮胎在伦敦成交的时候，显然早已过了应征年龄。基利先生给自己灌了点提神的东西，然后给我讲了他这一趟的经历。

===Original===
He reached Le Havre last Saturday afternoon. He had in his pockets no papers except an order for the car. He had been in Le Havre about two minutes when a gentleman attacked him from behind with a tap on the shoulder. The gentleman pulled back his coat lapel and flashed a star bearing the insignia of the British Intelligence Department. He was curious as to Mr. Kiley’s name and business. Mr. Kiley told him. Then he wanted to see Mr. Kiley’s papers. Mr. Kiley showed him the order for the car.

“I’m afraid that won’t do,” said the officer. “I’d advise you to leave town.”

“Give me just an hour,” pleaded Mr. Kiley, “just time enough to get the car and get out.”

“All right,” said the officer, “and be sure it’s only an hour.”

Mr. Kiley hastened to where the car was reposing, displayed the order, and started joyously to wind her up. He cranked and he cranked and he cranked. Nothing doing. He gave her a push downhill and tried to throw her into speed. Nothing doing. It occurred to him that something must be the matter. A thorough examination resulted in a correct diagnosis. There was no gas.

===Chinese===
他上星期六下午到的勒阿弗尔。兜里除了一张提车单，什么证件都没有。他到勒阿弗尔大约两分钟，一位先生就从背后拍拍他的肩膀，发起了袭击。那位先生撩起外套翻领，亮出一枚带英国情报部门徽记的星章。他对基利先生的姓名和来意颇感好奇。基利先生如实奉告。接着他要看基利先生的证件。基利先生给他看了提车单。

「恐怕这个不管用，」军官说。「我劝你离开本城。」

「给我一个钟头，」基利先生央求道，「够我把车取出来开走就行。」

「行，」军官说，「可说好了只有一个钟头。」

基利先生火速赶到汽车安卧之处，出示提车单，欢天喜地开始发动它。他摇了一曲又一曲又一曲。没戏。他把车往坡下推，想挂上挡。没戏。他恍然觉得事情有点不对劲。一番彻底检查得出了正确的诊断：没有汽油。

===Original===
Next to getting a drink of ice-water in Paris, the hardest job for a stranger is buying gasoline in any French town. Mr. Kiley was turned down five times before eighteen o’clock, when all the garages closed for the day.

He registered at a hotel and went into the café for dinner. He was just picking up the carte du jour when his friend, the officer, horned in.

“Mr. Kiley,” says this guy, “you have been in town more than an hour.”

“Yes, sir,” said Mr. Kiley. “But I’ve had trouble. I found my car, but I can’t run it because there’s no essence.”

“I think you’d better leave town,” said the officer.

“If you don’t mind,” said Mr. Kiley, “I’ll leave early in the morning.”

“I wouldn’t mind if you left right now,” said he.

There followed a long discussion and a cross-examination even crosser than mine in Bordeaux. Mr. Kiley revealed his whole family history and won the right to stay overnight, provided he remained indoors and departed from town first thing in the morning.

===Chinese===
在巴黎，除了想喝上一杯冰水，外地人最难的差事就是在法国随便哪座城里买汽油。到十八点钟——当天所有车行都关门的钟点——基利先生已经被拒绝了五回。

他登记住进一家旅馆，进咖啡馆吃晚饭。他刚拿起当日菜单（carte du jour），他那位军官朋友就挤了进来。

「基利先生，」这位老兄发话了，「你进城已经超过一个钟头了。」

「是的，长官，」基利先生说。「可我碰上麻烦了。车是找到了，可是没有 essence（汽油），开不动。」

「我看你最好离开本城。」军官说。

「你要是不介意，」基利先生说，「我明天一早就走。」

「你要是现在就走，我更不介意。」他说。

接着是一场漫长的谈话和盘问，比我在波尔多挨的那场还要横。基利先生交代了全部家史，换来了留宿一夜的权利，条件是待在旅馆里不出门，第二天一早头一件事就离开本城。

===Original===
But France is like America in that Saturday is usually succeeded by Sunday, and when Mr. Kiley arose from his hotel bed and resumed his search for gas he found every garage in town shut up tight. As I remember the United States, garages do not keep holy the Sabbath Day nor any other day. Over here, however, everything closes on Sunday except churches, theaters and saloons.

Mr. Kiley took in the situation and returned to his room to hide. Shortly before *midi* there was a knock at his door and a new officer appeared.

“You seem to like our town, Mr. Kiley,” said he.

“I’ll leave it as soon as I can get away,” said Mr. Kiley.

“No doubt,” replied the officer. “But I believe you will be here a long while.”

Mr. Kiley tried to look calm.

“Bone,” he said in perfectly good French.

“For the present,” said the officer, “you must not leave the hotel. Later on we’ll talk things over.”

===Chinese===
可是法国跟美国一样，星期六后面通常跟着星期天。基利先生从旅馆床上爬起来、继续找汽油的时候，发现全城的车行都关得死死的。据我对合众国的记忆，车行既不守安息日，也不守任何别的日子。可是在这边，星期天除了教堂、戏院和酒馆，样样都关门。

基利先生认清了形势，回房间躲了起来。快到中午（*midi*）时，有人敲门，来了个新军官。

「你好像挺喜欢我们这座城啊，基利先生。」他说。

「我一能脱身就走。」基利先生说。

「那还用说，」军官答道。「不过我相信，你会在这儿待上好一阵子。」

基利先生努力显得镇定。

「Bone。」他用一口地道的好法语说道。（把 bon「好」念成了 bone「骨头」。）

「眼下你不得离开旅馆，」军官说。「回头我们再从长计议。」

===Original===
In the café on Sunday night Mr. Kiley met an American and told him his troubles. The American had a car of his own in Le Havre and plenty of gasoline. He would be glad to give Mr. Kiley enough to start him on his way.

“But I can’t go,” said Mr. Kiley, “till I’ve fixed it with the police. I’ll have to look for them.”

He didn’t have far to look. No. 2 was in the lobby.

“Yes,” said No. 2, “you can leave town if you leave quick. There must be no more foolishness. The only thing that saves you from arrest is your uniform.”

Mr. Kiley left town and left quick, and, aside from his four blowouts, had an uneventful trip to Paris.

But what if I had taken that assignment﻿—I with no uniform except one willed me by the Chicago Cubs? O Boy!

===Chinese===
星期天晚上，基利先生在咖啡馆遇到一个美国人，对他大倒苦水。那人在勒阿弗尔有辆自己的车，汽油足足的。他乐意给基利先生够上路的量。

「可我走不了，」基利先生说，「除非跟警察把手续了结了。我得去找他们。」

他不用找多远。二号警官就待在大堂里。

「行，」二号说，「你可以出城，前提是马上走。不许再耍花样。救你免于逮捕的，就只有你那身制服。」

基利先生出了城，而且走得飞快，除了四条爆胎，一路无话到了巴黎。

可要是当初把这差事应下来的是我呢——我除了芝加哥小熊队遗赠给我的一身制服，什么制服都没有？好家伙！

===Original===
Saturday, August 25. Paris.

On advice of counsel I went to Colonel Anonymous of the American General Staff and besought him to fix it so that I might get to one of our camps without further stalling. Colonel Anonymous said it was all right with him and telephoned to Major Noname, who seemed to have authority in affaires journalistic.

Major Noname, fortunately, is a baseball fan. I told him what I did know, and lots that I didn’t know about our national pastime, and the reward was an American press pass to the infantry camp, S. in F.

I am going in a horseless carriage with Joe and Howard, fellow conspirators in the so-called journalistic game, and the start is to be made early Monday morning. Joe is going to drive his own car, and I hope he knows how.

===Chinese===
8 月 25 日，星期六。巴黎。

遵照法律顾问的意见，我去找美国参谋部的匿名上校，恳求他给通融通融，让我别再被拖延，好去成我们的某个营地。匿名上校说没问题，打电话给无名少校——这位在 affaires journalistic（「新闻事务」，法语英语一锅炖）方面似乎颇有权限。

所幸无名少校是个棒球迷。我把我知道的、还有许多我并不知道的全美第一消遣讲给他听，报酬是一张去步兵营地的美国新闻采访证，营地在法国某地（S. in F.）。

我将乘一辆无马马车前往，同行的还有乔和霍华德——所谓新闻行当里的同谋——定于星期一一大早出发。乔开他自己的车，我指望他真会开。

===Original===
*Dimanche, 26 Août.* Paris.

Yesterday was Saturday, and everybody had had a hot bath and felt like doing something. Three of us decided to take in the highly recommended show at Les Ambassadeurs.

A member of the Theatrical Geographic Society met us in the foyer and showed us a map of the playhouse. From it we were supposed to pick our seats. We chose three that, on paper, were in the sixth row in the center aisle. Our usher, female, led us to three which were in the tenth row, off to one side. Our usher stuck round as if she expected something. I was the party with the seat checks, and she got nothing. I was ignorant of the rules of the game. But not for long. Pretty soon in came three of the World’s Greatest Fighters, alias Canadian soldiers, and sat down behind us. Their usher was more persistent than mine.

“What do you want?” demanded one who seemed to be the financial leader. “I already gave you a franc.”

“*Un franc pour trois?*” said the lady in horror.

“Yes, and that’s enough,” said the Canuck. “Aller!” he added in perfect Canadian.

“*Je ne comprend pas*,” said the lady.

“Go to the devil then!” said the Canadian in perfect Portuguese.

The lady went somewhere, but whether to the proper destination I do not know.

“I wonder how much they charge to get out,” wondered the Canadian.

===Chinese===
*Dimanche, 26 Août.*（8 月 26 日，星期日。）巴黎。

昨天是星期六，人人都洗了个热水澡，都想干点什么。我们仨决定去看一场备受推崇的演出，地点在大使剧场（Les Ambassadeurs）。

一位「戏剧地理学会」的会员在前厅迎接我们，给我们看了一张剧场的地图。我们要照着它挑座位。我们挑了三个在纸面上位于中间过道第六排的座位。我们的领座员——女的——把我们领到第十排靠边上的三个座位。领座员赖在旁边不走，好像指望着什么。我是拿着座位票的那位，她一无所获。我不懂这行的规矩。但没多久就懂了。很快进来了三位「世界上最伟大的战士」，别名加拿大兵，在我们身后坐下。他们的领座员比我的那位执着得多。

「你要什么？」看样子是管钱的那位发话了。「我已经给过你一个法郎了。」

「*Un franc pour trois*（一个法郎给三个人）？」女士惊恐地说。

「对，够意思了。」这加拿大兵说。「Aller（走）！」他又用一口地道的加拿大话补了一句。

「*Je ne comprend pas*（我听不懂）。」女士说。

「那就见鬼去吧！」加拿大人用一口地道的葡萄牙语说。

女士去了某个地方，但去没去成该去的目的地，我就不得而知了。

「不知道出场他们收多少钱。」加拿大人纳闷道。

===Original===
Along about the middle of the show our own usher popped up before me and held out her right hand, at the same time exhibiting both teeth in an ingratiating smile. I shook the proffered hand. She withdrew her teeth.

“Non, non, non, non,” she said.

I asked her what she voulez-voued. She was coy.

“Do you want a tip?” I inquired in plain Michigan.

Both teeth reappeared. A dental curiosity drove me to hand her three francs. I had not underestimated.

In the second act a very nice-looking lady sang “A Broken Doll” in plain Thirty-ninth Street. The stage chorus tried to help her out on the second refrain, but, with all due modesty, I must say that it was the Canadians and I who earned the vociferous encore.

===Chinese===
演出进行到中场前后，我们那位领座员又蹦到我面前，伸出右手，同时露出全部两颗牙齿，堆出一个讨好的笑。我握了握那只伸过来的手。她把牙齿收了回去。

「Non, non, non, non（不，不，不，不）。」她说。

我问她到底 voulez-vous（想要）的是什么。她忸怩作态。

「你是想要小费吗？」我用平平常常的密歇根话问道。

两颗牙齿重新登场。一种牙科奇观的好奇心驱使我递给她三个法郎。我一点没有低估行情。

第二幕里，一位非常好看的女士用地道的三十九街腔（纽约剧院区口音）唱了一曲《破娃娃》。舞台合唱队在第二段副歌处试图帮衬她一把，但是，恕我毫无谦虚之意，我得说，赢得那一阵阵喝彩加唱的，其实是加拿大兵和我。

===Original===
*Lundi, 27 Août.* Paris.

The first batch of laundry was back when I returned from the theater Saturday night. Collars were done up in a neat package, tied with baby-blue ribbon. They looked just as when I had sent them out except that there was a high, shiny polish over the soiled spots. As for handkerchiefs, let us follow the British communiqué style:

“Eleven of our handkerchiefs went over the Blanchisserie lines. Two came back. Nine are missing.”

Some practical joker suggested that I go out yesterday afternoon and watch a baseball game between a Canadian team and a club from the American Red Cross. St. Cloud was the battle ground. You pronounce St. Cloud exactly as it is not spelled.

===Chinese===
*Lundi, 27 Août.*（8 月 27 日，星期一。）巴黎。

星期六晚上我从剧院回来时，第一批送洗的衣物回来了。衣领包成一个整齐的小包，扎着婴儿蓝的缎带。它们看上去跟我送出去时一模一样，只是脏的地方多了一层高亮的抛光。至于手帕，让我们采用英国战报的文风来报道：

「我方十一块手帕飞越了洗衣房（Blanchisserie）战线。两块返航。九块失踪。」

哪个缺德的促狭鬼提议我昨天下午出去看一场棒球赛，由一支加拿大队对一支美国红十字会的俱乐部。圣克卢（St. Cloud）是战场。圣克卢这个名字，念法恰恰和拼法毫不相干。

===Original===
A taxi man took us out there by way of Kansas City and El Paso, and during the forty minutes’ trip he was in high speed at least one minute. We bumped into a ceremony of awards. French soldiers to the number of two hundred were being given the Croix de Guerre.

The ceremony over, we crossed the race track and got on to the baseball field. There was an hour of badly needed practise, and then the two belligerents went at each other in a so-called ball game. It was stopped at the end of the eighth inning on account of rain, eight innings too late.

The rain, I am told, was long overdue, and we may expect gobs of it between now and then.

I am writing this early Monday morning, and early Monday morning is when we were supposed to start for the American camp. But there seems to be a difference of opinion over the meaning of the French adverb “early.”

===Chinese===
一个出租车司机领我们绕道堪萨斯城和埃尔帕索到了那儿，四十分钟的车程里，他至少有一分钟挂着高速挡。我们一头撞进了一场授勋仪式。多达两百名法国士兵正在被授予战争十字勋章（Croix de Guerre）。

仪式完毕，我们穿过赛马场，上了棒球场。先是足足一个钟头亟需补上的练习，随后两个交战方在这场所谓的棒球赛里扑向了彼此。比赛在第八局结束时因雨中止——晚了八局。

听说这场雨早就该下了，从现在到那时候之间，我们大可指望一场又一场瓢泼。

我这是在星期一一大早写这篇东西，而星期一一大早，正是我们该动身去美军营地的时候。可关于法语副词「一大早」（tôt）的含义，看来存在意见分歧。

===Original===
Tuesday, August 28. Somewhere in France.

“Early” proved to be half past ten yesterday morning. Joe drove us to the city limits, and there we had to pause. According to this year’s rules, ye automobilist pauses at the limits, has his gasoline measured, and then goes on. Returning to town, he has to pay a tax on the added amount of gasoline he brings, or something like that.

We were allowed to go out of town, and some thirty yards beyond the limits we found a garage. There we filled up with essence. Howard did the cranking, which is a necessity with all French cars, and away we went.

It was raining and it was cold. Joe and Howard were in the front seat, Joe driving and Howard studying the road map. I was in the back seat, catching cold.

“We’ll go right ahead,” said Joe, “to Such and Such a Place, and there we’ll stop and have lunch.”

Well, we stopped in Such and Such a Place, but it was not from a desire of lunch. It was because we were compelled to stop.

“Let’s see your papers,” said the stopper in French.

The stoppees, in English, displayed their passes to the American camp. The stopper didn’t know whether they were good or not. He asked us to wait a moment and disappeared out of the rain.
