We waited several moments. Finally there appeared another stopper, who read carefully our passes and told us they were no good and that we would have to loom up at the City Hall.

We went there, with Joe and Howard in the front seat and an officer and I in the back, me still catching cold, especially in the feet.

===Chinese===
8 月 28 日，星期二。法国某地。

「一大早」到头来是昨天上午十点半。乔把我们开到市区边界，我们只好停下。按今年的规矩，驾车人须在边界停车，让人量一量汽油，然后放行。回城时，他还得为自己带回来的增量汽油缴一笔税，大概是这么个意思。

我们获准出城，过了边界大约三十码就发现一家车行。我们在那儿加满了 essence（汽油）。摇车的活儿由霍华德包办——所有法国车都少不了这一道——然后我们就上路了。

天上下着雨，天很冷。乔和霍华德坐前排，乔开车，霍华德研究路线图。我坐后座，负责感冒。

「咱们一鼓作气，」乔说，「开到某某地方，在那儿停下来吃午饭。」

结果呢，我们是在某某地方停下了，可不是因为想吃午饭。是因为不得不停。

「看看你们的证件。」拦路的说，用的是法语。

被拦的用英语出示了去美军营地的通行证。拦路的搞不清灵不灵。他请我们稍等片刻，自己消失到雨外头去了。我们等了好几个片刻。最后又冒出来一个拦路的，仔细读了我们的通行证，说它们不作数，我们必须到市政厅去露面。

我们去了，乔和霍华德坐前排，一位军官和我坐后排，我还在感冒，尤其是脚。

===Original===
In the City Hall were French officers attired in all colors of the French army, which made the colors of the rainbow look like Simon Pure White. Our crime, it seems, was in not having an automobile pass on a red card. Or maybe it was blue. One of the thirty gentlemen in charge said we would have to wait till he telephoned back to Paris. Knowing the French telephone system, we inquired whether we might go across the street and eat. We were told we might.

We went across the street and ate, and it was a good meal, with meat, on a day which was meatless in Paris. A subaltern interrupted the orgy and said we were wanted back in the City Hall. Back there the startling information was that no telephonic satisfaction had been obtained. We asked whether we might go back to the café. There was no objection. We played pitch. French soldiers by scores came up and looked on. Joe thought, sub rosa, that it would be a grand idea to startle ’em. So we played pitch for one hundred francs a hand, it being tacitly understood that the money didn’t go. But we certainly had them excited.

Between pitch games in which thousands of francs were apparently lost and won, we visited, on summons, the City Hall five or six times. Every time there was the same heavy barrage of *français*.

Entered, finally, an English-speaking gent who said we might leave the city provided we went straight back to Paris.

“We’d much prefer,” said Joe, “to go on to where we were going.”

“You have the choice,” was the reply, “of returning to Paris or remaining here, in jail.”

Paris sounded the more attractive. They gave us back our car and away we went. It was after twenty o’clock, and it was pitch dark, and it was cold, and it was raining. And the man who had made the machine had forgotten to equip it with headlights.

===Chinese===
市政厅里的法国军官们穿着法军各兵种五颜六色的军服，相形之下，彩虹的七色都成了不折不扣的纯白。我们的罪名，好像是缺一张红卡片上的汽车通行证。也可能是蓝的。负责此事的三十位绅士之一说，我们得等他往巴黎打电话。鉴于对法国电话系统的了解，我们询问能否过条街去吃点东西。答复是可以。

我们过了街，吃了，还是顿有肉的好饭——而这一天巴黎是禁肉日。一位下级军官打断了这场饕餮，说市政厅找我们。回到那儿，得到的惊人消息是：电话那头仍旧一无所得。我们问能不能回咖啡馆去。没人反对。我们打起了皮奇牌（pitch）。成群结队的法国士兵凑过来看。乔私下里觉得，吓他们一跳会是个绝妙的主意。于是我们按一百法郎一手打皮奇，大家心照不宣：钱是不会真给的。可我们确实让他们开了眼。

在这输赢仿佛成千上万法郎的皮奇牌局之间，我们应召去了市政厅五六回。每一回都是同样猛烈的法语（*français*）弹幕。

最后进来一位说英语的老兄，说我们可以离城，条件是直接回巴黎。

「我们更想，」乔说，「继续开到我们要去的地方。」

「你们可以选择，」回答是，「回巴黎，或者留在这儿——坐牢。」

巴黎听着更诱人。他们把车还给我们，我们就上路了。已过二十点钟，天漆黑，又冷，还下着雨。而造这台机器的人，忘了给它装前灯。

===Original===
A little before midnight, on the downhill main street of a village, we saw ahead of us a wagon. It was two feet ahead of us. There being nothing else to do we banged into it. Then we stopped. The driver of the wagon sat suddenly down in the middle of the street and apologized. We all got out to see whether any damage had been done to the car. The only wounds discernible in the darkness were a smashed radiator and a bent axle.

“It’s lucky this happened in a town,” said I. “We can probably find a hotel.”

“We’re not going to look for one,” said Joe. “We’re going to drive to Paris.”

We got back in and, to our amazement, the darn thing started. There was plenty of headlight now, for the whole hood was ablaze. All lit up like a church, we went on our mad career until our conveyance dropped dead, overcome by the heat. This was four miles from a town that will be famous in the histories of this war.

“I guess we’re through,” said Joe. “One of us will have to stay with the car and see that nothing is stolen. The other two can go back to town and find a bed.”

By a vote of two to one, Howard was elected to stay with the car. He was the youngest.

===Chinese===
午夜前一会儿，在一个村子的下坡主街上，我们看见前方有一辆大车。它在我们前方两英尺处。既然别无他法，我们撞了上去。然后我们停了下来。赶大车的猛地坐到街当中，连连道歉。我们全下车查看车子有没有损伤。黑暗中辨认得出的唯一伤口，是一只撞碎的水箱和一根撞弯的轴。

「幸亏这事儿出在镇上，」我说。「咱们多半能找到一家旅馆。」

「咱们不找。」乔说。「咱们要开回巴黎。」

我们重新上车，令我们惊愕的是，这该死的东西居然发动了。这下前灯管够了，因为整个发动机罩都烧起来了。它像一座灯火通明的教堂，载着我们继续疯狂的征程，直到我们的坐骑热虚脱，当场断气。这儿离一座将在本次战争史上留名的镇子四英里。

「我看咱们完蛋了，」乔说。「得留一个人看守汽车，别让人偷了东西。另外两个回镇上找床。」

二比一表决，霍华德当选留守。他最年轻。

===Original===
Joe and I hiked our four miles in silence. The town was as brilliantly lighted as a cemetery and apparently void of inmates. We groped for an hour in a vain search for a hostelry. At length we gave up and resolved to sleep on the huge cathedral’s front porch. We were ascending the steps when a door opened and a human being stood before us.

“Arrested again,” thought I.

But the human being turned out to be not a copper, but a priest.

“Bonsoir, monsieur,” said Joe. “*Voulez-vous* show us *où* we can find a hotel?”

He led us across the street to a place we had doped out as the high school. He rapped on the door with his foot. In a few moments an aged lady, dressed for the night, appeared. There was a rapid exchange of *français*, after which we thanked the priest and were taken through a courtyard and upstairs to our room. We said a prayer for Howard and went to sleep, and I had a nightmare. I dreamed of a porterhouse steak.

===Chinese===
乔和我一言不发地跋涉了那四英里。镇子灯火辉煌得像座坟场，住户显然为零。我们摸黑找了一个钟头的旅馆，一无所获。最后我们放弃，决意睡在那座大教堂的前门廊上。我们正上台阶，一扇门开了，一个人立在我们面前。

「又被捕了。」我心想。

可那个人物原来不是条子，是个神父。

「Bonsoir, monsieur（晚上好，先生）。」乔说。「*Voulez-vous*（您能不能）show us *où*（指给我们看）哪儿能找到一家旅馆？」

他领我们过了街，进了一个我们原以为是中学的地方。他用脚敲了敲门。不一会儿，一位作夜间打扮的老太太出现了。法语你来我往了一阵，随后我们谢过神父，被领着穿过一个院子，上楼进了房间。我们为霍华德祈祷了一番，睡下，然后我做了个噩梦。我梦见了一客上等腰肉牛排。

===Original===
This morning we decided it wouldn’t be clubby to have breakfast before we had rescued Howard and the car. We went to a garage which was equipped with a beautiful lady, but no automobiles nor towropes. We found a livery stable that had everything but a horse. We commandeered a young man’s delivery cart from in front of a grocery store and drove out to the scene of our car’s demise. Howard and the corpse were still there. Howard thought it would be a good idea to go to the nearest farmhouse and rent a horse and a rope from the proprietor. The proprietor was very ignorant. He couldn’t understand our French. But in his employ was a German prisoner who could talk his own language and ours and the funny one that is prevalent round here. He explained our wants to the farmer and there ensued a few moments of haggling over price. We finally rented two horses and a rope for fifty francs and dragged the car back to town. From the looks of it, in daylight, I would say the economical course would have been to leave it out there in the road and keep the fifty francs.

The garage man says, in English, that he can make the necessary “reparations” in three weeks. So far as I’m concerned, he can devote three years to the job. Hereafter I’ll do my cross-country flitting about on a train.

It’s on one now, Paris bound, that I’m writing. There is nothing to do but write, for Howard is getting the sleep he missed last night and Joe is too angry to talk. He has spoken one sentence since we got up this morning.

“This is a queer war,” he said.

===Chinese===
今天早上我们一致认为，先吃早饭再去营救霍华德和车，不够哥们。我们去了一家车行，配备着一位漂亮女士，但没有汽车，也没有拖缆。我们又找到一家马车行，样样齐全，就缺马。我们从一家杂货铺门前征用了一个小伙子的送货板车，赶往我们汽车咽气的现场。霍华德和那具尸体还在那儿。霍华德觉得，到最近的农家去、向主人租一匹马加一根绳是个好主意。主人非常无知。他听不懂我们的法语。但他雇着一个德国战俘，会说自己的话、我们的话，还有本地通行的那种滑稽话。他向农夫转述了我们的需求，接着是几分钟的讨价还价。我们最后以五十法郎租到两匹马和一根绳，把车拖回了镇上。凭白天看到的模样，我敢说更经济的做法是把车撂在那条路上，省下这五十法郎。

车行老板用英语说，他能在三个星期内做好必要的「reparations」（修理；这词也当「战争赔款」讲）。就我而言，他花三年也随他。从今往后，我的跨乡越野飞行一律改乘火车。

此刻我正写在一列开往巴黎的火车上。没事可干只好写，因为霍华德正在补他昨晚缺的觉，而乔气得说不出话。从今早起床到现在，他就说过一句话。

「这场战争真邪门。」他说。

## IV Finally I Get to the American Camp; What I Find There / 四 我终于到了美军营地；在那儿见到了什么

===Original===
Thursday, August 30. At an American Camp.

Me and a regular American correspondent, Mr. Bazin, who has been here since before the war, but is still good-natured, took the train from Paris this morning and reached our destination shortly after lunch time. This is one of a string of villages in which the main body of the Expeditionary Forces are billeted.

We were met at the train by one of the correspondents’ cars, a regular he-man of a car from home, with eight cylinders and everything. Each correspondent rents a seat in one of the machines at a cost of sixty dollars a week. For this trifling sum he may be driven anywhere he wants to go along the line.

The correspondents have a tough life. They are quartered in a good—judged by French standards—hotel, and are not what you could call overworked. There is nothing to write about, and if you wrote about it you probably couldn’t get it through.

===Chinese===
8 月 30 日，星期四。美军某营地。

今早，我和一位正牌美国记者巴赞先生（Mr. Bazin）一起从巴黎上了火车，午饭刚过就到了目的地。这位先生战前就待在这儿了，脾气居然还没坏。这里是一串村子当中的一个，远征军主力就分散宿在这些村子里。

车站上有记者们的汽车来接——一辆从国内运来的、地道男子汉派头的车，八个汽缸，全套配备。每位记者在一辆车里租一个座位，周租金六十美元。花这笔小钱，沿线他想去哪儿，就能被拉到哪儿。

记者们的日子苦哇。他们住在一家按法国标准算好的旅馆里，也称不上劳累过度。没什么可写的；就算写了，多半也过不了审查。

===Original===
Mr. Corey, one of these slaves, invited me to accompany him to an infantry billet, some eighteen miles distant. We sailed along over the perfect roads at an average speed of about sixty, slowing up in the villages to dodge a harmless course among the cows, chickens and children, all of whom use the middle of Main Street for their playground.

We passed an occasional soldier, but it was a nice clear day, and the large majority were out in the fields and hills rehearsing. Our boys, I’m told, are getting quite a workout. Usually they leave their billets at seven in the morning, walk from six to twelve miles to a drill ground, and work till half past four in the afternoon. Then they take the long hike “home” and wonder how soon supper will be ready. Frequently, however, there is practise in night trench warfare, and then the grind continues till ten or eleven o’clock. The work is hard, but so, by this time, are the boys.

The captain on whom we called said he was glad to meet me, which is the first time that has happened in France. We asked him whether there was any news. He said yes, that the Salvation Army had established headquarters in the camp.

===Chinese===
科里先生（Corey）——这些苦命人中的一个——邀我同去一处步兵营舍，距此约十八英里。我们在平整的公路上飞驰，平均时速六十英里上下；进了村子便放慢，在牛、鸡和孩子中间躲闪穿行，无伤大雅——这几样全拿大街正中央当游乐场。

我们偶尔路过一两个士兵，不过天气晴好，绝大多数都到田野和山头上演习去了。听说咱们的小伙子们运动量相当可观。通常早晨七点离开营舍，步行六到十二英里到训练场，一直练到下午四点半。然后长途行军「回家」，边走边琢磨晚饭什么时候能好。不过经常还有夜间堑壕战演练，那样就得折腾到十点、十一点。活儿累，可到这会儿，小伙子们也练结实了。

我们拜访的那位上尉说，见到我很高兴——这在法国还是头一遭。我们问他有没有新闻。他说有：救世军（Salvation Army）在营里设了总部。

===Original===
“I’m glad,” he remarked, “that they’ve decided to go in on our side. It may influence the Kaiser’s friend Gott.”

The chief need of the soldiers, he went on, was amusement. The Salvation Army’s and Y.M.C.A.’s efforts were appreciated, but continual rations of soup and meat palled at times, and a little salad and dessert, in the form of Charlie Chaplin or the Follies, would make life more bearable.

“Some American theatrical producer,” said the captain, “could win our undying gratitude by shipping over a stock company with a small repertory of shows, with music, and *girls*. I believe he’d find it profitable too. When the boys get paid they don’t know what to do with their money. There’s nothing to spend it on in these parts.”

The captain invited us to dinner, but we had a previous date with members of the Censorship Bureau. These entertained us with stories which I voluntarily delete. From their hotel we returned to our own, held a brief song service in the correspondents’ mess, and called it a day.

===Chinese===
「我很高兴，」他说，「他们决定站到我们这边来了。说不定能影响影响德皇的那位朋友上帝（Gott）。」

他接着说，士兵们最缺的是消遣。救世军和基督教青年会（Y.M.C.A.）的努力大家领情，可天天顿顿汤加肉，再香也有吃腻的时候；要是能来点儿「色拉和甜点」——比如查理·卓别林（Charlie Chaplin），或者「富丽秀」（Follies）——日子就好熬多了。

「哪位美国戏剧制作人，」上尉说，「只要肯运一个带小剧目班的常驻剧团过来——要有音乐，还要有*姑娘*——就能赢得我们永世的感激。我相信他还会有利可图。小伙子们一发饷，都不知道拿钱怎么办。这一带压根儿没处花。」

上尉请我们留下吃饭，可我们已跟审查署（Censorship Bureau）的几位有约在先。他们讲的故事把我们逗乐了，我自愿删去不录。从他们的旅馆回到我们自己的，在记者食堂搞了场简短的歌咏会，就此收工。

===Original===
Friday, August 31. At an American Camp.

“Would you like to meet General Sibert?” asked Mr. Corey.

General Sibert’s name is one of the two that may be mentioned.

I said I would, and we left after breakfast for the next village, where headquarters is situate. In the outer office were some clerks and a colonel. The latter could never be accused of excessive cordiality.

===Chinese===
8 月 31 日，星期五。美军某营地。

「想不想见见西伯特将军（General Sibert）？」科里先生问。

西伯特将军的大名，是这里仅有的两个允许提及的名字之一。

我说想。早饭后我们动身去邻村，司令部就设在那儿。外间办公室里有几个文书和一位上校。后者绝对没人能指控他热情过头。

===Original===
“The general is busy,” he said.

“How long will he be busy?” inquired Mr. Corey.

“I have no idea,” said the colonel.

Mr. Corey and I felt we would be warmer outdoors, so we climbed back in our car and asked our sergeant-driver to take us to the nearest training grounds. Here an infantry regiment was going through simple drill, and calisthentics which were far from simple.

The nearest captain approached, smiled pleasantly and asked what he could do for us. We introduced ourselves.

“Correspondents, eh?” he said.

===Chinese===
「将军很忙。」他说。

「要忙多久？」科里先生打听。

「无可奉告。」上校说。

我和科里先生觉得还是外头暖和些，于是爬回车里，请我们的司机中士把我们拉到最近的训练场。那里一个步兵团正在做队列操，还有一套绝谈不上简单的体操（calisthenics）。

离我们最近的一位上尉走上前来，笑容可掬地问我们有何贵干。我们自我介绍了一番。

「记者，嗯？」他说。

===Original===
“Well, then, you can do something for us—make the newspapers and magazines quit calling us Sammies. We’ve never done anything to deserve a name like that.”

“What’s the matter with it?” we inquired.

“Everything!” said the captain. “It doesn’t fit, it sounds childish, and we just naturally hate it.”

We asked him whether there was an acceptable substitute.

“I don’t know of any,” he said. “In due time we’ll wish one on ourselves that will have pep and sound real. Meanwhile call us Julias, Howards—anything you like, except Sammies.”

We promised to do our best for him, and he was grateful enough to invite us to his mess for lunch.

===Chinese===
「那好，你们倒是可以为我们办件事——让报纸和杂志别再管我们叫美国大兵（Sammies）了。我们没干过任何配得上这种名字的事。」

「这名字有什么不好？」我们问。

「哪儿都不好！」上尉说，「不贴切，听着孩子气，我们天生就讨厌它。」

我们问他有没有可以接受的替代品。

「我想不出，」他说，「到时候我们自己会想出一个响亮带劲儿、听着像真的名字。眼下嘛，管我们叫朱莉娅也行，霍华德也罢——随你们喜欢，就是别叫美国大兵。」

我们答应尽力去办，他感激得很，索性请我们去他的食堂吃午饭。

===Original===
This young man—he looks about twenty-nine—hasn’t been to his home, somewhere out West, since he left West Point, six years ago. He hasn’t seen a show in six years. Mexico and the Philippines have kept him busy. His promotion from lieutenant to captain is very recent, and he still wears only one stripe. “I suppose I’ll be a major before I get the other,” he said. “A man can hardly keep up with his rank these days.”

He called our attention to the physical condition of his men.

“You’ve got to be in the pink to go through those exercises without yelling for help,” he said. “These fellas couldn’t have done it a month ago. Now they seldom get tired, though the hours are pretty stiff. Today is a cinch. It’s payday, and there’s a muster soon after lunch. So most of us will get a half holiday and nobody’ll object.”

The captain blew his whistle to indicate that the game was over. His boys quit happily, and we left him after agreeing to show up at his billet in time for lunch.

===Chinese===
这位年轻人——看上去二十九岁上下——自打六年前离开西点军校，就没回过西部的家。六年没看过一场戏。墨西哥和菲律宾一直没让他闲着。他从少尉升上尉是不久前的事，肩上还只有一道杠。「看来到第二道杠下来之前，我就该当上少校了，」他说，「如今一个人的军衔，自己都快追不上了。」

他让我们留意他的兵的体格。

「想不喊救命地做完全套动作，你得壮得红光满面，」他说，「这帮小子一个月前还做不下来。现在他们难得喊累，尽管钟点够狠。今天是轻松日子：发饷日，午饭一过就点名集合。咱们大半人能得个半天假，谁也没意见。」

上尉吹响哨子，示意游戏结束。他的小伙子们欢天喜地地收了工。我们跟他约好午饭前赶到他的营舍，便告辞了。

===Original===
“We have a fairly good cook,” he promised. “But what is much more important, we have a beautiful young lady to wait on us.”

Our next stop was at a trench school. Americans, under French tutelage, had constructed a perfect—so we were told—system of ditches and entanglements, and had shown aptitude in learning the offensive and defensive points of this pleasant method of warfare. They were now engaged in bomb-throwing drill. Some of them had tried the baseball throw, but had found the grenades too heavy. Several crooked-arm throws would do things to a person’s elbow. But, according to the officers, the youngsters had done very well with the bowling motion and had surprised the French with their accuracy.

This officer, another captain, spoke in complimentary terms of the French assistance.

“They’ve been more than diligent with us,” said he. “They’ve never shown impatience when we failed to grab their point, but have gone over it and over it till we’ve learned it to suit them. The difference in languages makes it hard sometimes to get what they’re after, but they eventually manage to make themselves understood. The only fault I have to find with them,” he confided, “is that they don’t give us credit for knowing anything at all. They tell us this thing’s a rifle, and the thing on the end of it is a bayonet, and so forth. And one of them showed me a barbed-wire entanglement one day, and told me what it was for. I’d always been under the mistaken impression that it was used for bedclothes.”

===Chinese===
「我们有个相当不错的厨子，」他许诺道，「但更要紧的是，我们有位漂亮姑娘伺候用饭。」

我们的下一站是一所战壕学校。美国人法国教官指导下修了一套——据人家说——尽善尽美的堑壕和障碍物体系，在学习这种愉快战法的攻防要点上也显出了天分。他们这会儿正练投弹。有几个试过棒球式投法，但发现手榴弹太沉。胳膊肘拧着甩上几下，肘部可吃不消。不过据军官们说，小子们用保龄球动作投得相当出色，准头让法国人大吃一惊。

这位军官，又是一位上尉，对法国的援助赞不绝口。

「他们对我们不可谓不尽心，」他说，「我们领会不到要领的时候，他们从不露出不耐烦，翻来覆去地讲，直到我们学得让他们满意为止。语言不通，有时很难弄明白他们要的是什么，但他们总有办法让咱们听懂。我对他们唯一的意见，」他透露道，「是他们压根儿不相信咱们懂任何东西。他们告诉你：这玩意儿叫步枪，步枪头上那玩意儿叫刺刀，如此等等。有一天他们中的一位指给我看一道铁丝网障碍，讲解它是干什么用的。我原先一直误以为那是铺床用的。」

===Original===
We had to turn down this captain’s luncheon invitation, but we stopped at his house for light refreshment. His lieutenant, a young University of Michigan boy, had come over on the first transport, and related interesting details of that historic trip.

We went on to the other captain’s, and lunched with him and his major and colonel. The beautiful young lady proved every bit as pretty as a pair of army shoes. But the food was good and the captain’s French better. He kept hurling it at the beautiful young lady, who received it with derisive laughter. His accent, it appeared, was imposseeb.

“I like to make her laugh,” he told me. “It takes me back home among the coyotes.”

===Chinese===
这位上尉的午饭邀请我们只好谢绝，但在他家歇脚用了些茶点。他的中尉是个密歇根大学（University of Michigan）出来的小伙子，乘第一支运输船队过来的，讲了不少那趟历史性航行的有趣细节。

我们接着去了另一位上尉那里，同他和他的少校、上校一起用了午餐。那位漂亮姑娘果然名不虚传——漂亮得赛过一双军鞋。不过饭菜好，上尉的法语更好。他一个劲儿朝漂亮姑娘抛法语，姑娘报以嘲弄的大笑。他那口音，看来是「英泡西布」级的（imposseeb，把 impossible 念出了法语腔）。

「我就爱逗她笑，」他对我说，「一下子就回到老家郊狼堆里了。」

===Original===
On the street of the village I held converse with a private, aged about twenty-three. I said I supposed he was glad it was payday.

“What’s the difference!” he said. “I got more money now than Rockefella. I ain’t spent more’n a buck since we been over, and then it was just to be spendin’ it, not because they was anything to buy. I seen a fella the other day light a cigarette with one o’ these here dirty twenty-franc notes. He was sick o’ carrying it round. And they was another fella went up to one o’ these here village belles and slipped her a hundred francs. He never seen her before, and he won’t never see her again. He just says ‘Souvenir’ and let it go at that.”

“Did she take it?”

“Oh, I guess not! She’s to gay Paree by this time already.”

“She won’t burn up that town with a hundred francs.”

“No, but all these girls don’t think o’ nothin’ but gettin’ there. From what I seen of it, I’d just as soon be in Akron.”

“Oh, I’d hardly say that!”

===Chinese===
村子的街上，我跟一个大兵聊了聊，二十三岁上下。我说，今儿发饷，你准挺高兴吧。

「高兴个啥！」他说，「我这会儿的钱比洛克菲拉（Rockefella，洛克菲勒的讹音）还多。过来这么久，统共没花上一块钱，那回也只是为了花着玩儿，并不是有什么可买的。前两天我瞧见一个家伙，拿这么一张脏兮兮的二十法郎票子点烟。他揣着它揣烦了。还有一个家伙，走到村里哪位美人儿跟前，塞给她一百法郎。他以前没见过她，往后也不会再见。他只说了声『纪念品』（Souvenir），完事儿。」

「她收了吗？」

「嗐，我看没有！这会儿她早到花花巴黎（gay Paree）去喽。」

「一百法郎可烧不着那座城。」

「烧不着，可这些丫头心里除了去那儿啥都不想。照我瞧下来的情形，我宁肯待在阿克伦（Akron）。」

「哟，这话可不敢苟同！」

===Original===
“Talk about spendin’ money! They was a poor fella here last week that got rid of a lot of it. He bought himself a bottle o’ champagne wine. I don’t think he’d tasted it before, but it’s cheap over here. So he got a hold o’ this bottle and poured it into him like it was excelsior water, and it acted on him like it was laughin’ gas. He went up alongside the officers’ billet and sang ’em a vocal solo. The captain heard him—you could of heard him in San Francisco—and the captain come out and invited him in. And when he got him in there he says: ‘So-and-So, how much did this little bun cost you?’ So the fella told him a buck and a half. So the captain says: ‘You’ve underestimated the amount by about seventy bucks. You’ll get your next pay the last day of October.’”

I asked my new friend how he liked his billet.

===Chinese===
「说起花钱！上礼拜这儿有个可怜家伙，撒出去一大把。他给自己买了一瓶香槟。我看他以前都没尝过，不过这东西在这儿便宜。于是他抱着那瓶酒，跟灌埃克塞尔西奥汽水（excelsior water，美国廉价饮料）似的往肚里灌，那酒在他身上起的作用跟笑气一模一样。他跑到军官营舍边上，给人家唱了支独唱。上尉听见了——旧金山都能听见——就出来请他进去。等把人请了进去，他说：『某某，你这场小醉花了多少钱？』那家伙就说，一块半。上尉就说：『你少算了大约七十块。你下一回发饷，得等到十月的最后一天。』」

我问这位新朋友，他的营舍住得怎么样。

===Original===
“Great!” he said. “I and a couple other fellas has a room next to a pig on one side and a flock o’ chickens on the other. We never get lonesome, and it makes it nice and handy when we want some ham and eggs. I know one fella that rooms next to a settlement o’ rats. Night times he sets his flashlight so’s it throws a narrow path o’ light acrost the floor, then he puts a little piece o’ meat in the path and stands over it with a bayonet. When Mr. Rat gets there the fella comes down whang with the bayonet and fastens him to the floor. It’s good target practise, and he’d ought to be sure fire by the time it’s Huns instead o’ rats.”

“Maybe,” said I, “the Huns would know better than to come out in the light.”

“They’d go anywheres for a piece o’ meat,” said the private.

===Chinese===
「棒极了！」他说，「我和另外两个家伙一间屋，一边挨着口猪，一边挨着一窝鸡。从来不闷，想吃火腿鸡蛋的时候还顺手。我认识一个家伙，屋隔壁是一窝耗子。夜里他把手电筒支好，让它在地板上照出一条窄道儿，再搁一小块肉在道儿上，自己端着刺刀守在上头。耗子先生一到，那家伙一刺刀剁下去，把它钉在地板上。这可是顶好的打靶练习，等靶子从耗子换成匈奴兵（Huns），他保管百发百中。」

「只怕匈奴兵，」我说，「没那么傻，肯往亮处跑。」

「给块肉，他们哪儿都去。」大兵说。

===Original===
He had to depart and report for muster. We took another road home, a road frequented by sheep and railroad crossings, both of which slow you up considerably.

In France the gates—strong iron ones—at grade crossings are kept closed except when someone wants to cross the tracks. The someone makes known his desire by tooting his horn or shouting, and the gatekeeper—usually an old lady with the pipe-smoking habit—comes out of her shack and opens the gates, expending anywhere from ten minutes to half an hour on the task. The salary attached to the position is the same as that of a French private: ten centimes a day, which is two cents in regular money. I presume the gatekeepers have a hot time in the old town on pay night.

As for the sheep, when you come up behind them you might as well resign yourself to staying behind them till they reach the village for which they are headed. They won’t get out of the way of their own accord, and neither the dog nor the aged shepherd will make any effort to sidetrack them.

Having led them into the village, the shepherd proceeds to deliver them to their respective owners. He stops in front of a house, plays a certain tune on his horn, and the sheep or sheeps belonging to that house step out of ranks and sheepishly retire for the night, or perhaps sit up a while in the parlor and talk war with the family.

===Chinese===
他得走了，点名去。我们换了条道回家——一条常撞见羊群和铁路道口的道，这两样都能让你慢得可观。

在法国，铁路平交道口的门——结实的铁门——平时一律关着，有人要过道口才开。想过去的人按喇叭或喊一嗓子表明意愿，看道口的——多半是位有抽板烟习惯的老太太——就从她的小屋里出来开门，这项工程耗时十分钟到半小时不等。这份差事的薪水与法国列兵相同：一天十生丁，折合正经货币两美分。我料想发饷那晚，看道口的老太太们必定在老城里狂欢一场。

至于羊，你一旦跟到羊群后头，不如认命，老老实实跟到它们要去的那个村子为止。它们自己绝不会让路，狗和老羊倌也绝不肯费劲把它们轰到边上。

进了村子，羊倌便着手把羊各归其主。他在一户人家门前停下，用号角吹一段特定的曲子，那家的羊便出列，臊眉耷眼地退下歇夜去了——兴许还在客厅里坐上一会儿，跟主人一家聊聊战局。

===Original===
There must be a lot of intermarrying among the sheeps of one village. A great many of those in the flock we saw looked enough alike to be cousins or something.

Somebody suggested a poker game for this evening’s entertainment, but I got all I wanted of that great sport coming across the bounding blue.

It has rained only an hour in two days, and the boys say we’ll get it good tomorrow.

===Chinese===
一个村子的羊之间，通婚的想必不少。我们看见的那群里，有好多长得跟表亲似的。

有人提议今晚的余兴节目来场扑克，可这种伟大运动，我在漂洋过海时已经受够了。

两天里只下了一个钟头的雨，小伙子们说明天有咱们好受的。

===Original===
Saturday, September 1. In an American Camp.

As exclusively predicted by everybody, it was pouring when we arose this morning, but rain doesn’t keep you indoors in France. If it did, you would live indoors.

We splashed the thirty miles to the other end of the camp and inflicted ourselves on a major of marines. He seemed deliberately unfriendly at first, but it was only his manner. After five minutes of awkward monosyllabic dialogue he gave us the usual refreshments and took us out to see the town, the name of which should be Mud if it isn’t.

“This is a grand climate,” he said. “They must have had conscription to get people to live here.”

===Chinese===
9 月 1 日，星期六。美军某营地。

正如人人独家预言的那样，今早起床时大雨倾盆。不过在法国，下雨可拦不住你出门。真拦得住的话，你就得常年住在屋里了。

我们蹚着水跑了三十英里，到营地另一头去骚扰一位海军陆战队少校。他起先似乎存心冷淡，其实只是做派如此。五分钟干巴巴的单字对话之后，他端出了例行的茶点，带我们出去参观镇子——那镇子要是不叫「泥浆镇」，就该叫这个名。

「这气候真壮丽，」他说，「当初准是靠征兵才弄来人住的。」

===Original===
He took us to the camp kitchen, of which he was evidently and justly proud. It was a model of convenience and cleanliness. He spoke to the cook.

“Are you very busy?” he asked.

“No, sir,” was the reply.

“Then I’d shave if I were you,” said the major.

“Daily shaving,” he told us when we got outside, “ought to be compulsory in our army as it is in the British. When a man hasn’t shaved he isn’t at his best, physically, morally, or mentally. When he has he’s got more confidence in himself; his morale is better. Shaving has a psychological effect, and I try to impress my men with the importance of it. They say it’s a difficult operation here, but I guess if the Tommies can do it in the trenches, we can in these billets.”

===Chinese===
他带我们去看营区厨房，他显然引以为豪，也确实当得起。那是便利与洁净的典范。他对厨子说话。

「很忙吗？」他问。

「不忙，长官。」对方答。

「那我要是你，就把胡子刮了。」少校说。

「每天刮脸，」出来后他对我们说，「应该像英军那样，在我们军队里定为强制。一个人没刮脸，身体、道德、心智都不在最佳状态。刮了，他就更有自信，士气更旺。刮脸有心理作用，我尽量让弟兄们领会它的重要性。他们说到这儿刮脸是件难事，可我想，英国兵（Tommies）在战壕里都刮得成，我们在这些营舍里也刮得成。」

===Original===
We remarked on the increasing popularity of mustaches among the men.

“I don’t object to them,” said the major. “Neither do I see any sense to them. To my mind they’re in a class with monocles or an appendix. But so long as the men keep their cheeks and chins smooth, they’re at liberty to wear as much of a misplaced eyebrow as they can coax out.”

The major showed us his hospital and his dentist shop and marched us up a steep hill, where, in the rain, we saw a great many interesting things and promised not to write about them.

After lunch we decided it would be patriotic to go home and remove our wet clothes. In my case, this meant spending the rest of the day in my room, and that’s where I am.

===Chinese===
我们提到弟兄们中间胡子日渐流行。

「我不反对，」少校说，「也看不出它有什么道理。依我看，它跟单片眼镜或阑尾同属一类。不过只要弟兄们把腮帮子和下巴刮得光光，那根长错了地方的眉毛，他们爱哄出多长就留多长。」

少校领我们看了他的医院和牙科诊所，又带我们爬上一座陡山。在雨里，我们看到了许许多多有趣的东西，并保证不写出来。

午饭后我们认定，回家脱掉湿衣服乃是爱国之举。对我而言，这意味着当天余下的时光都耗在房间里——我此刻正待在这儿。

===Original===
Sunday, September 2. Paris.

The driver assigned to take me to the train, which left from the next village this morning, lost his way, and we reached the station just as the engine was sounding the Galli-Curci note that means All Aboard. There was no time to buy a ticket, and you can’t pay a cash fare on a train in France. But the conductor, or whatever you call him here, said I could get a ticket at the destination, Paris; in fact, I must get a ticket or spend the rest of my unnatural life wandering about the station.

I found a seat in a compartment in which were a young American officer, beginning his forty-eight hours’ leave, and a young French lady who looked as if she had been in Paris before. The young officer and I broke into conversation at once. The young lady didn’t join in till we had gone nearly twenty kilomet’s.

Captain Jones, which isn’t his name, called attention to the signs on the window warning MM. Les Voyageurs to keep their anatomies indoors. The signs were in three languages. “*Ne pas Pencher au Dehors*,” said the French. The English was “Danger to Lean Outside.” And the Wop: “*Non Sporgere*”—very brief. It was evident that a fourth variation of the warning had been torn off, and it didn’t require a William Burns to figure out in what language it had been written.

“If there were a boche on this train,” said Captain Jones, “he could lean his head off without hurting anyone’s feelings.”

===Chinese===
9 月 2 日，星期日。巴黎。

派来送我去火车站的司机——今早的火车从邻村发车——迷了路，我们赶到站上时，车头正拉响加利-库尔奇（Galli-Curci，著名女高音）式的高音，意思是「全体上车」。来不及买票了，而法国的火车上又不兴补现金票。可列车员——或者这儿随便管他叫什么——说我可以在终点站巴黎补票；非但可以，是必须，不然就得把后半辈子非正常的人生都耗在站内流浪上。

我在包厢里找到个座位，同厢有一位年轻的美国军官，他四十八小时的假期刚刚开始；还有一位年轻的法国女士，看那样子像是在巴黎住过的。年轻军官和我立刻攀谈起来。年轻女士直到车开了将近二十公里（kilomet’s），才加入进来。

琼斯上尉（化名）——这不是他的真名——让我留意车窗上的告示，警告「旅客诸位先生」（MM. Les Voyageurs）把身体各部位留在车内。告示用三种语言。法文写的是「*Ne pas Pencher au Dehors*（请勿探身窗外）」。英文是「Danger to Lean Outside（探身窗外危险）」。意大利文的是「*Non Sporgere*（请勿探身）」——相当简短。显然原本还有第四种版本被撕掉了，那上头写的是什么文字，用不着请威廉·伯恩斯（William Burns）来破案。

「这车上要是有个德国佬，」琼斯上尉说，「他尽管把脑袋探个精光，谁的感情也伤不着。」

===Original===
“Languages are funny,” continued the captain sagely. “The French usually need more words than we do to express the same thought. I believe that explains why they talk so fast—they’ve got so much more to say.”

I inquired whether he knew French.

“Oh, yes,” he said. “I’ve been over here so long that I can even tell the money apart.”

The dining-car conductor came in to ask whether we wanted the first or second “*série*” luncheon. You must reserve your seat at table on trains here or you can’t eat. We decided on the second, and so did our charming compartment mate. Captain Jones, supposing she could not understand English, said: “Shall you take her to lunch or shall I?”

===Chinese===
「语言这东西真逗，」上尉煞有见地地接着说，「表达同一个意思，法国人通常比我们费词。我想这就解释了他们为什么说话那么快——要说的话实在太多。」

我问他懂不懂法语。

「哦，懂，」他说，「我过来这么久，连各种钱都认得清了。」

餐车列车员进来问我们要头等还是二等「*série*（席次）」午饭。在这儿的火车上用餐必须预订座位，否则没饭吃。我们要了二等，我们那位妩媚的同厢伙伴也要了二等。琼斯上尉以为她不懂英语，便说：「你带她去用午餐呢，还是我来？」

===Original===
I was about to be magnanimous when she remarked, with a scornful glance at the captain: “I shall myself take me to lunch if monsieur has no objection.”

The cap was temporarily groggy, but showed wonderful recuperative powers and in five minutes convinced her that he would toss himself into the Seine if she refused to eat with us. She accepted, after some stalling that convinced me she had been cordially inclined all the while.

General polite conversation ensued, and soon came the inevitable French question: How many American soldiers were there in France? I have heard it asked a million times, and I have heard a million different answers. The captain gave the truthful reply: “I don’t know.”

“This war,” he said, “should be called the War of Rumors. The war will be over by Christmas. The war won’t be over for ten years. The boche is starving. The Allies are getting fat. The boche has plenty to eat. The Allies are dying of hunger. Our last transport fleet sank five subs. Our last transport fleet was sunk by a whole flotilla of subs. Montenegro’s going to make a separate peace with Bosnia. There is talk of peace negotiations between Hungary and Indiana. Ireland, Brazil and Oklahoma are going to challenge the world. They’re going to move the entire war to the Balkans and charge admission. The Kaiser’s dying of whooping cough. You can learn anything you want to or don’t want to know. Why”—this to me—“don’t you fellas print the truth?”

===Chinese===
我正打算发扬风格，她却轻蔑地瞟了上尉一眼，开口了：「要是先生不反对，我自己带我自己去用午餐。」

上尉暂时被打懵了，但恢复力惊人，五分钟之内就让她相信：她若拒绝与我们同席，他就跳塞纳河。她推辞了一番之后答应了——那番推辞让我确信，她从头到尾都是乐意的。

接下来是彬彬有礼的交谈，很快就来了那个绕不开的法国问题：法国到底有多少美国兵？这个问题我听过一百万次，也听过一百万种答案。上尉给出了诚实的回答：「我不知道。」

「这场战争，」他说，「该改名叫谣言战争。战争圣诞节就结束。战争十年也完不了。德国佬在挨饿。协约国在长膘。德国佬吃的管够。协约国饿得要死。我们上一支运输船队击沉了五艘潜艇。我们上一支运输船队被整整一支潜艇舰队击沉了。黑山要跟波斯尼亚单独媾和。匈牙利和印第安纳之间在传和谈。爱尔兰、巴西和俄克拉何马要挑战全世界。他们要把整场战争搬到巴尔干去，还卖门票。德皇快死于百日咳了。你想知道什么、不想知道什么，都能打听到。我说」——这是冲我来的——「你们这帮家伙怎么不登点儿真的？」

===Original===
“And where,” I asked him, “would you advise us to go and get it?”

“The same place I got it,” said the captain.

“And what is it?”

“I don’t know.”

===Chinese===
「那依你之见，」我问他，「我们该上哪儿弄真的去？」

「跟我弄到它的地方一样。」上尉说。

「那是什么？」

「我不知道。」

===Original===
We adjourned to the diner. A sign there said: “*Non Fumeurs.*” The captain pointed to it.

“That’s brief enough,” he said. “That’s once when the French is concise. But you ought to see the Chinese for that. I was in a town near the British front recently where some Chinese laborers are encamped. In the station waiting-room, it says: ‘No Smoking’ in French, English, Russian and Italian. The Russian is something like ‘Do notski smokevitch,’ and the Italian is ‘Non Smokore.’ Recently they have added a Chinese version, and it’s longer than the Bible. A moderate smoker could disobey the rules forty times before he got through the first chapter and found out what they were driving at.”

Be that as it may, I have observed that everybody in France smokes whenever and wherever he or she desires, regardless of signs. We did now, and so did our guest, while waiting for the first course, which was black bread baked in a brickyard.

===Chinese===
我们移师餐车。那儿挂着一块牌子：「*Non Fumeurs*（禁烟）。」上尉指着它。

「够简洁了，」他说，「这回法国话总算精炼了一回。可你应该瞧瞧这意思的中文。前一阵我在英国前线附近一个镇子上，那儿扎着一些中国劳工。车站候车室里用法、英、俄、意四国文字写着『禁止吸烟』。俄文写出来差不多是『Do notski smokevitch（多不茨基·斯莫克维奇）』，意大利文是『Non Smokore（农·斯莫科雷）』。最近又添了中文版本，比《圣经》还长。一个烟量适中的人，可以把禁令违上四十回，才读完第一章，弄明白人家到底要干什么。」

话虽如此，据我观察，法国人人人想什么时候抽烟就什么时候抽，想在哪儿抽就在哪儿抽，牌子全不理会。我们现在就抽上了，我们的客人也抽上了，一边等头道菜——砖窑里烤出来的黑面包。

===Original===
“I would love to go to America,” said mademoiselle.

“You wouldn’t care for it,” replied the captain promptly. “It’s too wild.”

“How is it wild?”

“Every way: manners, habits, morals. The majority of the people, of course, are Indians, and you just can’t make them behave.”

She asked whether either of us had ever been in New York. The captain said he’d passed through there once on the way to Coney Island. She wanted to know if New York was bigger than Paris. “It’s bigger than France,” said Captain Jones.

Monsieur was trying to make a game of her.

===Chinese===
「我真想去美国。」小姐说。

「你不会喜欢的，」上尉立刻答道，「太野蛮了。」

「怎么个野蛮法？」

「哪方面都野蛮：规矩啦，习惯啦，道德啦。那儿的老百姓，大头当然是印第安人，你简直没法让他们守规矩。」

她问我们俩谁到过纽约。上尉说他有一回路过那儿，是在去科尼岛（Coney Island）的路上。她想知道纽约是不是比巴黎大。「比法国都大。」琼斯上尉说。

先生这是在拿她打趣呢。

===Original===
“Well, anyway,” said the captain, “you could lose France in Texas.”

What was Texas?

“Texas,” said the captain, “is the place they send soldiers when they’ve been bad. It’s way out west, near Chicago.”

The lady had heard of Chicago.

“This gentleman works there,” said the captain. “He’s part Indian, but he was educated at Carlisle and is somewhat civilized. He gets wild only on occasions.”

===Chinese===
「反正，」上尉说，「把法国搁进得克萨斯，都得找不着。」

得克萨斯是什么地方？

「得克萨斯，」上尉说，「是士兵们不守纪律时被发配去的地方。老远的西边，芝加哥附近。」

这位女士听说过芝加哥。

「这位先生就在那儿工作，」上尉说，「他有半个印第安血统，不过在卡莱尔（Carlisle）受过教育，算是开了化。只是偶尔发野。」

===Original===
The lady regarded me rather scaredly.

“He lives on the plains outside the city,” continued the captain, “and rides to his work and back on a zebra. Practically all the suburban savages have zebras, and the Chicago traffic police have a fierce time handling them during their owners’ working hours. They run wild around the streets and in the department stores, and snap at women, especially brunettes.”

We had attained the potato course. The French positively will not serve potatoes as other than a separate course. I was about to help myself to a generous portion when the captain cried: “Here! Better leave those things alone. You know what they do to you.”

I told him I didn’t believe two or three would hurt, and proceeded to take three.

“When a half Indian eats potatoes,” said the captain, “he usually forgets himself and runs amuck.”

===Chinese===
这位女士瞧我的眼神，颇有点儿害怕。

「他住在城外的平原上，」上尉接着说，「每天骑斑马上下班。郊区野蛮人差不多家家都有斑马，主人上班的钟点里，芝加哥的交通警察管它们管得苦不堪言。它们满街乱跑，闯进百货公司，见人就咬，专咬黑头发的女人。」

我们上到了土豆这道菜。法国人坚决不把土豆当配菜，非单独上一道不可。我正要给自己拨上满满一份，上尉嚷嚷起来：「喂！那玩意儿最好少碰。你知道它们对你干得出什么来。」

我告诉他，两三个想来不碍事，说着拿了三个。

「半个印第安人一吃土豆，」上尉说，「多半会把持不住，见人就砍。」

===Original===
Our guest probably didn’t know what a muck was, but it had an unpleasant sound, and the look she gave me was neither friendly nor trusting.

“The greatest difference between France and America,” continued Captain Jones, “is in the people. In America a man ordinarily takes the initiative in striking up an acquaintance with a woman. He has to speak to her before she’ll speak to him. This would never do in France, where the men are too shy. Then there’s a difference in the way men treat their wives and horses. Americans use whips instead of clubs. And Americans have funny ideas about their homes. Private bedrooms and playrooms are provided for their pets—zebras, lizards and wild cats—and the little fellows are given to understand that they must remain in them and not run all over the house, like one of your cows.”

He paused to ask me how the potatoes were acting. I said it was too soon to tell, but I felt a little dizzy in the head. He suggested it were better to go back to our compartment, where there were less things to throw in the event of my reaching the throwing stage.

“On the other hand,” I said, “if I am deprived of knives, forks and plates, I will pick on human beings, and I usually aim out the windows.”

But he said he was sick of the atmosphere in the diner. We asked for *l’addition* and argued over who should pay it. I won, and when he had been given his change we returned to our own car, where mademoiselle demonstrated her fear of my expected outbreak by going to sleep.

===Chinese===
我们的客人多半不知道「砍」是个什么讲究，可这词听着不祥，她看我的那眼神，既不友好，也不信任。

「法国和美国最大的不同，」琼斯上尉继续说，「在人。在美国，通常是男人主动跟女人搭讪。他得先开口，她才会搭理他。这在法国万万行不通，法国男人脸皮太薄。还有，男人对待老婆和马的方式也不一样。美国人用鞭子，不用棍子。美国人对他们的家也有些怪想法。他们的宠物——斑马啦、蜥蜴啦、野猫啦——都有专门的卧室和游戏室，还得让这些小家伙明白：必须待在自己屋里，不许满房子乱跑，跟你们家的牛似的。」

他停下来问我，土豆这会儿发作了没有。我说为时尚早，只是脑袋有点儿晕。他建议最好回我们包厢去，万一我到了要扔东西的阶段，那边能扔的东西少些。

「反过来讲，」我说，「要是没了刀叉盘子，我就得拿人开刀，而且我向来朝窗外瞄准。」

可他说他烦透了餐车里的空气。我们要了账单（*l’addition*），为谁付钱争了一通。我赢了。找完零，我们回到自己车上。小姐对我随时可能发作的担忧，表现为呼呼睡去。

===Original===
We turned our attention to the scenery, the most striking feature of which was the abundance of boche prisoners at work in the fields.

“Lucky stiffs!” said the captain. “The war is over for them if they can just manage not to escape, and I guess there’s no difficulty about that. Better food than the soldiers, a soft job, and a bed to sleep in. And wages besides. Every private in the Fritz army would surrender if the officers hadn’t given them a lot of bunk about the way German prisoners are treated. They make them believe we cut off their feet and ears and give them one peanut and a glass of water every two weeks.”

Paris hove into view, and we quarreled about the girl. The fair thing, we decided, would be to turn over her and her baggage to a porter and wish her many happy returns of the day. We were spared this painful duty, however, for when she awoke she treated both of us as strangers. And the gentleman who attended to her baggage was not a porter, but a French aviator, waiting on the station platform for that very purpose.

“She’ll tell him,” guessed the captain, “that an American soldier and half Indian tried to flirt with her on the train, but she froze them out.”

===Chinese===
我们转而看风景，风景中最触目的一项，是田野里干活的大批德国佬俘虏。

「幸运的苦力们！」上尉说，「只要设法别逃跑，战争对他们就算完了，我猜这也毫无难度。吃的比大兵好，活儿轻省，还有床睡，外加工资。弗里茨（Fritz）军队里哪个列兵都会投降，要不是军官们拿德国俘虏的待遇那一套鬼话哄他们。他们让兵相信，我们抓了俘虏要砍脚割耳朵，每两个星期才给一粒花生、一杯水。」

巴黎进了视野，我们为那位姑娘争执起来。最后议定，公正的做法是把她和她的行李一并交给搬运工，再祝她年年有今日。不过这桩痛苦的差事我们幸免了：她一觉醒来，把我们俩一律当作陌路。而照应她行李的那位先生也不是搬运工，是一位法国飞行员，正在站台上专为这个目的恭候。

「她会告诉他，」上尉猜道，「车上有个美国兵和一个半个印第安人想跟她调情，叫她给冻回去了。」

===Original===
Captain Jones stuck with me till my exit ticket was procured, a chore that ate up over an hour. Then we climbed into a dreadnought and came to this hotel, where I sat right down and versified as follows:

> If you don’t like the nickname Sammy,
>
> If it’s not all a nickname should be,
>
> You can pick out Pat or Mike,
>
> Whatever name you like—
>
> It won’t make no difference to me.
>
> Want a Thomas or Harry or Dick name?
>
> Dost prefer to be called Joe or Lou?
>
> You’ve a right to your choice of a nickname;
>
> Oh, Mr. Yank, it’s up to you.

===Chinese===
琼斯上尉一直陪我办完出站补票手续才走——这桩差事耗了一个多钟头。然后我们钻进一艘无畏舰（dreadnought，戏指巨型出租车），开到这家饭店。我坐下就写了下面这首诗：

> 要是你不喜欢「美国大兵」（Sammy）这诨名，
>
> 要是它配不上诨名该有的水准，
>
> 你可以挑「帕特」，可以选「迈克」，
>
> 随便哪个你喜欢的名——
>
> 对我都无所谓。
>
> 想要个「托马斯」「哈利」「迪克」式的名？
>
> 想被唤作「乔」还是「卢」？
>
> 诨名你有权自己挑；
>
> 哦，美国佬先生，全看你了。

## V My Adventures at the British Front / 五 我在英国前线的历险

===Original===
Monday, September 3. Paris.
In this morning’s mail was a letter from Somewhere in London, replying favorably to my request to go to the British front. I was directed to take the letter to the assistant provost marshal, who would slip me a pass and inform me as to the details of the trip.
At the A.P.M.’s I was given the pass and with it “an undertaking to be signed by all intending visitors to the front.” There are ten rules in the undertaking, and some of them are going to be hard to obey. For example:
“I understand that it is impossible to arrange for me to see relatives serving with the fighting forces.”
“I will not visit the enemy front during the present war.”
But No. 6 is the tough one:
“In no circumstances will I deliver a political or electioneering speech to troops.”
I must pray for strength to resist natural impulses along this line.

===Chinese===
9 月 3 日，星期一。巴黎。

今早的邮件里有一封来自伦敦某处的信，答复了我去英国前线的请求：准了。信上指示我把信交给宪兵副官（assistant provost marshal），由他发我一张通行证，并告知行程的细节。

在宪兵副官那儿，我领到了通行证，随证还附有一份「所有拟赴前线参观者均须签署的承诺书」。承诺书共十条规矩，有几条怕是不大好遵守。例如：

「本人知悉，不可能安排本人探望在作战部队服役的亲属。」

「本人不会在本次战争期间参观敌方前线。」

不过最难熬的是第六条：

「本人无论如何不对部队发表政治演说或竞选演说。」

我必须祈祷上天赐我力量，抵御这一方向上的天然冲动。

===Original===
Wednesday morning, said the A.P.M., would be our starting time. And he told us when and where to take the train﻿—“us” because I am to be accompanied by a regular correspondent, one who carries a cane and everything.
Mr. Gibbons, the regular correspondent, informs me I must wear a uniform, and tomorrow morning I am to try on his extra one, which he has kindly offered.
Another chore scheduled for tomorrow is the squaring of myself with the boss of the French Maison de la Presse, who invited me to visit the devastated territory Thursday and Friday. The invitation was accepted, but the British and French dates conflict, and I would rather see one real, live front than any number of broken-down barns and boched trees.

===Chinese===
宪兵副官说，动身时间定在星期三上午。他还交代了我们何时何地上车——之所以说「我们」，是因为将有一位正式记者陪同我，一位拄手杖、行头一应俱全的人物。

正式记者吉本斯（Gibbons）先生通知我，我必须穿军装，明早我去试他多余的一套，他好意出借。

明天排定的另一桩差事，是去跟法国新闻部（Maison de la Presse）的头儿把话说明白。他邀我星期四、星期五去参观满目疮痍的地区。请柬我已应下了，可英国和法国的日子撞了车——比起随便多少塌了架的谷仓和被德国佬（boche）糟蹋过的树，我还是更想看一条活生生的真前线。

===Original===
Tuesday, September 4. Paris.
I reported, after the French idea of breakfast, at the Maison de la Presse. This is situate on the fourth floor of a building equipped with an elevator that proves the fallacy of the proverb “What goes up must come down.” You can dimly see it at the top of the shaft, and no amount of button pushing or rope pulling budges it.
During the long climb I rehearsed the speech of apology and condolence framed last night, and wondered whether monsieur would be game and try to smile or break down completely or fly into a rage. He was game, and he not only tried to smile, but succeeded. And his smile was in perfect simulation of relief. These French are wonderful actors.
I returned thence to Mr. Gibbons’ room for my fitting. His extra uniform consisted of a British officer’s coat and riding breeches, puttees and shoes. Cap and khaki shirt I had to go out and purchase. The store I first selected was a gyp joint and wanted twenty-seven francs for a cap. I went to another store and got exactly the same thing for twenty-six. A careful shopper can save a lot of money in Paris.
Provided with cap and shirt, the latter costing a franc less than the former, I went to a secluded spot and tried on the outfit, Mr. Gibbons assisting. We managed the puttees in thirty-five minutes. It is said that a man working alone can don them in an hour, provided he is experienced.

===Chinese===
9 月 4 日，星期二。巴黎。

按法国人对早餐的概念用过餐之后，我到新闻部报到。部址在一座楼的四层，楼里装了一部电梯，足以证伪「上得去必下得来」这句谚语。你只能隐隐约约瞅见它待在井道顶上，任你把按钮按烂、绳子拽断，它纹丝不动。

在这段漫长的攀登途中，我把昨夜拟好的道歉兼慰问演说在心里排练了几遍，一边琢磨这位先生会拿出点儿骨气强颜欢笑呢，还是会彻底垮掉，抑或勃然大怒。他有骨气，不但试着笑，还真笑成了。而且那一笑，把如释重负模仿得惟妙惟肖。这些法国人，个个是了不起的演员。

随后我回到吉本斯先生的房间试装。他那套多余的军装包括：一件英军军官上衣、马裤、绑腿（puttees）和皮鞋。军帽和卡其衬衫则须我自己出门购置。我头一家选中的店是个宰客的黑店，一顶帽子开口要二十七法郎。我换了家店，一模一样的东西，二十六法郎拿下。在巴黎，精打细算的购物人能省下一大笔钱。

帽子衬衫到手——后者比前者便宜一法郎——之后，我寻了个僻静去处试穿全套行头，吉本斯先生从旁协助。绑腿我们花三十五分钟搞定。据说一个人单干，若是有经验，一个钟头也能穿上去。

===Original===
“You look,” Mr. Gibbons remarked when I was fully dressed, “as if you had been poured into it.”
But I felt as if I hadn’t said “when” quite soon enough. Mr. Gibbons and I differ in two important particulars﻿—knee joints﻿—and though I tried to seem perfectly comfortable, my knees were fairly groaning to be free of the breeches and out in the open fields.
“Wear it the rest of the day and get used to it,” advised Mr. Gibbons.
“No,” I said. “I don’t want to rumple it all up. I want to keep it neat for tomorrow.” And against his protest I tore myself out and resumed my humble Chicago garb.
It’s no wonder regular correspondents and British officers are obliged to wear canes. The wonder is that they don’t use crutches.
We leave at nine tomorrow morning. This means that myself and puttees will have to get up at four.

===Chinese===
「您瞧着，」我穿戴齐全之后吉本斯先生点评道，「像是被人倒进去的。」

可我觉得自己像是「够了」喊得不够及时。吉本斯先生和我在两个要害部位上有分歧——膝关节——尽管我竭力装出无比舒适的样子，我的膝盖却在低声哀号，巴不得挣脱马裤，到开阔的田野里撒欢去。

「剩下的一天都穿着它，穿习惯就好。」吉本斯先生建议。

「不，」我说，「我不想把它揉得皱皱巴巴。我要留它清清爽爽见明天。」不顾他的抗议，我把自己从行头里撕扯出来，重新套上我那身寒酸的芝加哥便装。

正式记者和英国军官非拄手杖不可，这不奇怪。奇怪的是他们怎么不拄拐棍。

明早九点出发。这就是说，我和绑腿四点钟就得爬起来。

===Original===
Wednesday, September 5. With the British.
The major has a very good sense of the fitness of things. The room where I’m writing, by candlelight, is the best guest room in our château and was once occupied by the queen.
The rules of the household call for the dousing of downstairs glims at eleven o’clock. After that you may either remain down there in total darkness or come up here and bask in the brilliant rays of a candle. You should, I presume, be sleepy enough to go right to bed, but you’re afraid you might forget something if you put off the day’s record till tomorrow.
I overslept myself, as they say, and had to get Mr. Gibbons to help with the puttees. The lower part of the breeches, I found, could be loosened just enough to make the knee area inhabitable.

===Chinese===
9 月 5 日，星期三。在英国人那儿。

少校对事情的得体与否极有分寸。我此刻借烛光写字的房间，是我们这座城堡里最好的客房，王后住过的。

家规规定，十一点钟楼下的灯盏一律掐灭。此后你要么待在楼下一团漆黑里，要么上楼来，沐浴在蜡烛的灿烂光辉里。我猜按理你该困得倒头就睡，可你又怕万一拖到明天再记，有些事就忘了。

我睡过了头——照他们的说法——只好又劳动吉本斯先生帮忙对付绑腿。我发现马裤膝盖以下那截可以放松到刚好让膝部地区勉强宜居。

===Original===
We skipped breakfast and reached the station in a taxi without hitting anything. It was fifteen minutes before train time, but there wasn’t a vacant seat in the train. A few of the seats were occupied by poilus, and the rest by poilus’ parcels and newspapers. A Frenchman always gets to a nine o’clock train by seven-thirty. He picks one seat for himself and one or two on each side of him for his impedimenta. This usually insures him privacy and plenty of room, for it is considered an overt act even to pick up a magazine and sit in its place. Mr. Gibbons and I walked from one end of the train to the other and halfway back again without anyone’s taking a hint. We climbed into a carriage just as she started to move. There were six seats and three occupants. We inquired whether all the seats were reserved, and were given to understand that they were, the owners of three having gone to a mythical dining-car.

===Chinese===
我们略过早饭，乘出租车抵达车站，一路没撞着任何东西。离发车还有一刻钟，可整列车上一个空座都没有。一部分座位被法国大兵（poilu）占着，其余的让大兵的包裹和报纸占着。法国人赶九点的火车，素来七点半就到。他给自己挑一个座，左右两边再各圈一两个，安顿随身杂物。这一般能保他清静宽敞，因为哪怕捡起一本杂志坐进那个空位，也被视为公然挑衅。吉本斯先生和我从车头走到车尾，又往回走了半程，没人领会我们的暗示。眼看车要开动，我们爬进一节车厢。六个座位，三位乘客。我们打听座位是否都有主了，得到的答复是都有——其中三个座的主人去了那趟传说中的餐车。

===Original===
We went into the aisle and found standing room among the Australians and Canadians returning from their leave. One of the former, a young, redheaded, scrappy-looking captain, smiled sympathetically and broke open a conversation. I was glad of it, for it gave me an opportunity of further study of the language. I am a glutton for languages, and the whole day has been a feast. We have listened to six different kinds﻿—Australian, Canadian, British, French, Chinese and Harvard. I have acquired an almost perfect understanding of British, Australian and Canadian, which are somewhat similar, and of Harvard, which I studied a little back home. French and Chinese I find more difficult, and I doubt that anyone could master either inside of a month or so.
The redheaded captain remarked on the crowded condition of the trine. That is Australian as well as British for train. The Canadian is like our word, and the French is spelled the same, but is pronounced as if a goat were saying it. Lack of space prevents the publication of the Chinese term.

===Chinese===
我们退到过道里，在休假归队的澳大利亚人和加拿大人中间觅得立足之地。澳方一位年轻的红头发上尉，一脸好斗相，投来同情的微笑，率先攀谈起来。我正求之不得，因为这给了我进一步研究语言的机会。我是语言饕餮，这一整天都是盛宴。我们听了六种不同的语言——澳大利亚话、加拿大话、英国话、法国话、中国话和哈佛话。英国话、澳大利亚话、加拿大话三者颇为相近，我已听得八九不离十；哈佛话我在国内时也学过一点。法国话和中国话我就觉得难了，我看谁也别想一个月光景就精通其中任何一种。

红头发上尉对「trine」的拥挤状况发表了高见——这是澳大利亚话，也是英国话里「火车」的意思。加拿大话跟我们那个词一模一样；法国话拼法相同，念起来却像一头山羊在念。至于中文的那个词，限于篇幅，恕不刊载。

===Original===
One of the captain’s best pals, he told us, had just been severely wounded. He was a gime one, though even smaller than the captain. The captain recalled one night when he, the pal, took prisoner a boche lieutenant who stood over six feet. Fritz was asked whether he spoke English. He shook his head. He was asked whether he spoke French. He lost his temper and, in English, called the entire continent of Australia a bad name. The captain’s little pal then marched him off to the proper authority, to be questioned in English. On the way the captain’s little pal made him take off his helmet and give it to him. This was as punishment for what Fritz had said about Australia.
Before the proper authority Fritz was as sweet-tempered as a bloody bear. This puzzled the proper authority, for making a boche prisoner is doing him a big favor.
“What iles you?” asked the authority when Fritz had refused to reply to any of a dozen questions. “You ine’t the first bloody boche officer we’ve tiken.”
Then Fritz bared his grievance. He didn’t mind, he said, being a prisoner. The size of his captor was the thing that galled. “And for Gott’s sake,” he added, “make him give back my helmet.”
The proper authority turned to the captain’s little pal. “He’s your prisoner,” he said. “What do you want to do with the helmet?”
“Keep it, sir,” said the captain’s little pal.
And it will be used back in Australia some day to illustrate the story, which by that time will doubtless have more trimmings.

===Chinese===
上尉告诉我们，他最要好的一个伙伴刚负了重伤。那是个有种的小个子，虽然个子比上尉还矮一截。上尉回忆起某个夜晚，这位伙伴俘获了一个身高六英尺开外的德国佬中尉。人家问弗里茨（Fritz，对德国兵的称呼）会不会说英语。他摇头。又问他会不会说法语。他火了，张口用英语把整个澳大利亚大陆骂了个遍。上尉的小伙伴于是押他去见主管长官，等着用英语受审。一路上，上尉的小伙伴命令他摘下头盔交出来。这是对他辱骂澳大利亚的惩罚。

到了主管长官跟前，弗里茨的脾气好得像头该死的狗熊。这可让主管长官纳闷了，因为当德国佬的俘虏，那是天大的恩典。

一连十几个问题弗里茨一概拒不回答，长官便问：「你哪儿犯拧？你又不是我们抓来的头一个该死的德国佬军官。」（原文带伦敦土腔讹音）

于是弗里茨倒出了苦水。他说，当俘虏他不在乎，扎心的是抓他的人那副尺寸。「看在上帝份上，」他补了一句，「叫他把我头盔还来。」

主管长官转向上尉的小伙伴。「他是你的俘虏，」他说，「那头盔你打算怎么处置？」

「留着，长官。」上尉的小伙伴说。

有朝一日回澳大利亚，这顶头盔正好给这段故事当物证——到那时候，故事想必又添了不少佐料。

===Original===
“But how about Fritz?” I asked. “When he gets home and tells the same story, he’ll have nothing with which to prove it.”
“He ine’t agoin’ to tell the sime story.”

===Chinese===
「可弗里茨怎么办？」我问，「等他回了家，讲起同一段故事，可就拿不出什么凭据了。」

「他是不会去讲同一段故事的。」（原话照旧是伦敦土腔：He ine’t agoin’ to tell the sime story）

===Original===
We were welcomed at our destination by a captain, another regular correspondent, and two good English cars. The captain said he was expecting another guest on this train, a Harvard professor on research work bent.
“I have no idea what he looks like,” said the captain.
“I have,” said Mr. Gibbons and I in concert, but it went over the top.
The professor appeared at length, and we were all whisked some thirty kilometers to a luncheon worth having. Afterward we were taken to the Chinese camp. Chinatown, we’ll call it, is where the Chink laborers are mobilized when they first arrive and kept until their various specialties are discovered. Then each is assigned to the job he can do best. I was told I mustn’t mention the number of Chinamen now in France, but I can say, in their own language, it’s a bigee lottee.

===Chinese===
目的地有位上尉迎接我们——又是一位正式记者——外加两辆好样的英国汽车。上尉说他还在这趟车上等一位客人，一位一心扑在研究工作上的哈佛教授。

「他长什么样，我可毫无概念。」上尉说。

「我们可有。」吉本斯先生和我异口同声——可惜这句妙语越过了壕顶，没人接住。

教授终于露面，我们全体被风驰电掣地送去约莫三十公里外，吃了一顿不虚此行的午饭。饭后我们被带到中国营。姑且管它叫「唐人街」吧：中国佬（Chink）劳工初来时先在这里集结，等各自的专业特长被摸清了，再分配到最擅长的岗位。人家告诉我，眼下在法国的中国人有多少，这个数字不许提；不过我可以用他们自己的语言说一句：那是 bigee lottee（洋泾浜英语「老鼻子多啦」）。

===Original===
They wear a uniform that consists of blue overalls, a blue coat, and no shirt whatever, which, I think, is bad advertising for their national trade. They brought shirts with them, it seems, but are more comfy without.
The minimum wage is three francs a day. Two-thirds of what they earn is paid them here, the other third given to their families in China. The system of hiring is unique. No names are used, probably because most Chinks have Sam Lee as a monniker, and the paymaster would get all mixed up with an army of Sam Lees. They are numbered and their finger prints are taken by an agent in China. He sends these identification marks to the camp here, and when the Chinks arrive they are checked up by a fingerprint expert from Scotland Yard. This gentleman said there had been several cases where the Chinaman landing here was a ringer, some “friend” back home having signed up and then coaxed the ringer to come in his place, believing, apparently, that the plot would not be detected and that his profit would be the one-third share of the wage that is paid in China. The ringer’s family would be done out of its pittance, but that, of course, would make no difference to the ringer’s friend. The fingerprint system serves not only to prevent the success of cute little schemes like that, but also to amuse the Chinks, who are as proud of their prints as if they had designed them.

===Chinese===
他们的军装由如下部件构成：蓝色工装裤、蓝色外套，外加一件也没有的衬衫。这我认为对本国的国计民生是则糟糕的广告。衬衫他们倒带了，看来，只是不穿更舒服。

最低工资每天三法郎。挣来的钱三分之二在此地发给本人，另外三分之一发给在中国的家属。雇工制度独一无二：不用姓名，大概因为多数中国佬的尊姓大名都是「三利」（Sam Lee），发饷官面对一支三利大军非乱套不可。于是人人编号，指纹由驻中国的代理人事先采集，寄到这边的营地；中国佬一到，再由苏格兰场来的指纹专家逐一核对。这位绅士说，曾有过好几起案子：在这儿上岸的中国佬是个冒名顶替的，是老家某个「朋友」自己签了约，再哄得顶替者代他前来——那朋友显然认定这小计谋不会被识破，自己还能净赚在中国支付的那份三分之一工钱。顶替者的家属会损失掉那点血汗钱，不过这当然碍不着顶替者的朋友一丝一毫。指纹制度不但能挫败此类小巧玲珑的骗局，还能给中国佬们逗乐——他们对自己的指纹骄傲得很，仿佛那图案是他们亲手设计的。

===Original===
We went into the general store, which is conducted by a Britisher. The Chinese had just had a payday and were wild to spend. One of them said he wanted a razor. The proprietor produced one in a case, and the Chink handed over his money without even looking at the tool. Another wanted a hat. The prop. gave him a straw with a band that was all colors of the rainbow. The Chinaman paid for it and took it away without troubling to see whether it fitted.
A block or so from the store we ran across two Chinks who had been naughty. Each was in a stock, a pasteboard affair on which was inscribed, in Chinese, the nature of his offense. One of them had been guilty of drinking water out of a fire bucket. The other had drunk something else out of a bottle﻿—drunk too much of it, in fact. They looked utterly wretched, and our guide told us the punishment was the most severe that could be given: that a Chinaman’s pride was his most vulnerable spot.

===Chinese===
我们走进杂货铺，掌柜的是个英国人。中国人刚发了饷，花钱花得发狂。一位说要买剃刀。掌柜的取出一把带盒的，那中国佬钱一交，连那家什看都没看一眼。另一位要帽子。掌柜的给他一顶草帽，帽带七彩缤纷，把彩虹的颜色占全了。这位中国佬付了钱拎着就走，懒得试试合不合适脑袋。

离铺子一两个街区，我们撞见两个惹了祸的中国佬。一人脖子上枷着一面牌子——纸板糊的，用中文写着各自罪状。一位的罪名是喝了消防桶里的水。另一位从一个瓶子里喝了点别的东西——确切说，喝得太多了。两人看上去惨极了，向导告诉我们，这是能给出的最重的惩罚：中国佬的骄傲，是他最经不起打的地方。

===Original===
The gent who had quenched his thirst from the fire bucket was sentenced to wear his stock a whole day. He of the stew was on the last lap of a week’s term.
We talked with one of the Lee family through an interpreter. We asked him if he knew that the United States was in the war against Germany. He replied, No, but he had heard that France was.
Just before we left the settlement a British plane flew over it. A Chink who was walking with us evidently mistook it for a Hun machine, for he looked up and said: “Bloody boche!”

===Chinese===
从消防桶里解渴的那位绅士，被判牌子挂足一整天。喝瓶子货的那位，一周期限已行至最后一程。

我们通过翻译同姓李的一位攀谈。我们问他，可知道美国已对德国开战。他答：不知道，不过听说法国参战了。

我们即将离开这片营地时，一架英国飞机从上空飞过。同行的一位中国佬分明把它错当成了匈奴兵（Hun）的飞机，因为他抬起头，骂了一句：「该死的德国佬！」

===Original===
From Chinatown we were driven to the American Visitors’ Château, where gentlemen and correspondents from the United States are entertained. It’s a real château, with a moat and everything. The major is our host. The major has seen most of his service in India and China.
He said he was glad to meet us, which I doubt. The new arrivals, Mr. Gibbons, the Harvard professor and myself, were shown our rooms and informed that dinner would occur at eight o’clock. Before dinner we were plied with cocktails made by our friend, the captain. The ingredients, I believe, were ether, arsenic and carbolic acid in quantities not quite sufficient to cause death.

===Chinese===
出了唐人街，我们被载往美国来宾城堡，专门款待美国绅士和记者的地方。一座地道的城堡，带护城河，一应俱全。主人是位少校。少校的军旅生涯大半是在印度和中国度过的。

他说很高兴见到我们，此话我持保留态度。新到的客人——吉本斯先生、哈佛教授和我——被领去看了各自的房间，并被告知晚膳将于八点钟发生。晚饭前，我们的朋友上尉调制的鸡尾酒一杯杯灌将上来。配料据我判断是乙醚、砒霜和石炭酸，剂量仅差一点儿不足以致命。

===Original===
Eleven of us gathered around the festal board. There were the major and his aids, three British captains, one with a monocle. There was the Harvard professor, and the head of a certain American philanthropical organization, and his secretary. And then there were us, me and Mr. Gibbons and Mr. O’Flaherty and Mr. Somner, upstarts in the so-called journalistic world.
The dinner was over the eighteen-course course, the majority of the courses being liquid. I wanted to smoke between the fish and the sherry, but Mr. O’Flaherty whispered to me that it wasn’t done till the port had been served.

===Chinese===
我们十一个人围坐节庆餐桌。有少校和他的副官们，三位英国上尉，其中一位夹单片眼镜。有哈佛教授，有美国某慈善组织的头目及其秘书。再就是我们——我、吉本斯先生、奥弗莱厄蒂（O’Flaherty）先生和桑纳（Somner）先生，所谓新闻界的暴发户。

这顿晚饭办的是十八道菜的排场，其中多数道是液体的。我想在鱼和雪利酒之间抽支烟，奥弗莱厄蒂先生悄悄告诉我：不到波特酒上桌，此事不成体统。

===Original===
Mention was made of the Chinese camp, and there ensued a linguistic battle between the major and the Harvard professor. The latter explained the theory of the Chinese language. He made it as clear as mud. In the Chinese language, he said, every letter was a word, and the basis of every word was a picture. For example, if you wanted to say “my brother,” you drew a picture of your brother in your mind and then expressed it in a word, such as woof or whang. If you wanted a cigar, you thought of smoke and said “puff” or “blow,” but you said it in Chinese.
Mr. Gibbons broke up the battle of China by asking the major whether I might not be allowed to accompany him and Mr. O’Flaherty and one of the captains on their perilous venture tomorrow night. They are going to spend the night in a Canadian first-line trench.
“I’m sorry,” said the major, “but the arrangement has been made for only three.”
I choked back tears of disappointment.

===Chinese===
席间提到了中国营，少校和哈佛教授随即展开一场语言学论战。后者讲解了中文的原理。讲得跟一锅烂泥一样清亮。他说，在中文里，每个字母都是一个词，每个词的基础是一幅图画。比如你想说「我兄弟」，就在脑子里给你兄弟画一幅像，然后用一个词把它表达出来，比如 woof 或 whang。你要是想来支雪茄，就想到烟，说「噗」或「吹」——不过得用中文说。

吉本斯先生出面终止了这场中华之战：他问少校，明晚他们那趟险象环生的行动，能否容我随行，与他、奥弗莱厄蒂先生和其中一位上尉同去。他们要在加拿大人的第一线战壕里过夜。

「很遗憾，」少校说，「安排只做了三个人的。」

我咽下了失望的泪水。

===Original===
The major has wished on me for tomorrow a trip through the reconquered territory. My companions are to be the captain with the monocle, the Harvard professor, the philanthropist, and the philanthropist’s secretary. We are to start off at eight o’clock. Perhaps I can manage to oversleep.

===Chinese===
少校把明天的差事甩给了我：游览一遍收复失地。我的同伴将是夹单片眼镜的上尉、哈佛教授、慈善家，以及慈善家的秘书。定于八点出发。兴许我能设法睡过头。

===Original===
Thursday, September 6. With the British.
I did manage it, and the car had left when I got downstairs. Mr. Gibbons and Mr. O’Flaherty were still here, and the three of us made another effort to get me invited to the party tonight. The major wouldn’t fall for it.
Mr. Gibbons and Mr. O’Flaherty motored to an artillery school, the understanding being that they were to be met at six this evening by one of our captains and taken to the trench. I was left here alone with the major.
We lunched together, and he called my attention to the mural decorations in the dining-room. It’s a rural mural, and in the foreground a young lady is milking a cow. She is twice as big as the cow and is seated in the longitude of the cow’s head. She reaches her objective with arms that would make Jess Willard jealous. In another area a lamb is conversing with its father and a couple of squirrels which are larger than either lamb or parent. In the lower right-hand corner is an ox with its tongue in a tin can, and the can is labeled Ox Tongue for fear someone wouldn’t see the point. Other figures in the pictures are dogs, foxes and chickens of remarkable size and hue.
“We had a French painter here a few days ago,” said the major. “I purposely seated him where he could look at this picture. He took one look, then asked me to change his seat.”

===Chinese===
9 月 6 日，星期四。在英国人那儿。

我还真办到了，等我下楼，车已经开走了。吉本斯先生和奥弗莱厄蒂先生还在，我们仨又努力了一把，想让我混进今晚的聚会。少校不上这个当。

吉本斯先生和奥弗莱厄蒂先生坐车去了一所炮兵学校，讲好今晚六点由我们的一位上尉接他们进战壕。我被单独留下，与少校相伴。

我们共进午餐，他提请我留意饭厅里的壁画装饰。一幅田园壁画：前景里一位年轻姑娘正在挤奶。她身量是那头牛的两倍，坐落方位与牛头同处一经度。她伸出一双能让杰斯·威拉德（Jess Willard）嫉妒的臂膀，够着了目标。另一片区域里，一只羔羊在和它父亲以及两只松鼠交谈，松鼠个头比羔羊和父亲都大。右下角一头牛，舌头伸在一只铁皮罐里，罐上贴着标签「牛舌」，生怕有人看不出门道。画中其余角色，是些尺寸与色彩都不同凡响的狗、狐狸和鸡。

「前几天我们有位法国画家来，」少校说，「我故意安排他坐在能看见这幅画的位置。他看了一眼，就求我给他换座。」

===Original===
The major inquired whether I had noticed the picture of the château which decorates the doors of our automobiles.
“When you go out tomorrow,” he said, “you’ll observe that none of the army cars is without its symbol. An artillery car has its picture of a gun. Then there are different symbols for the different divisions. I saw one the other day with three interrogation marks painted on it. I inquired what they meant and was told the car belonged to the Watts division. Do you see why?”
I admitted that I did.
“Well, I didn’t,” said the major, “not till it was explained. It’s rather stupid, I think.”

===Chinese===
少校问我可曾留意我们汽车车门上装饰的城堡图样。

「明天你出门，」他说，「会看到每辆军车都有自己的标志，无一例外。炮兵的车画一门炮。各师又各有各的标志。前几天我见一辆车画着三个问号。我问这是什么意思，人家说这车属于瓦茨（Watts）师。你看出门道了吗？」

我承认看出来了。

「嗬，我可没有，」少校说，「不等人解释明白，我是没看出来。我觉得这相当蠢。」（瓦茨 Watts 谐音 What's「啥」，三个问号即连问三声「啥？」）

===Original===
This afternoon an American captain, anonymous of course, called on us. He is stopping at G.H.Q., which is short for General Headquarters, his job being to study the British strategic methods. He and the major discussed the differences between Americans and Englishmen.
“The chief difference is in temperature,” said the captain. “You fellows are about as warm as a glacier. In America I go up to a man and say: ‘My name is Captain So-an-So.’ He replies: ‘Mine is Colonel Such-and-Such.’ Then we shake hands and talk. But if I go to an Englishman and say: ‘My name is Captain So-and-So,’ he says: ‘Oh!’ So I’m embarrassed to death and can’t talk.”
“ ’Strawnary!” said the major.
At tea time a courier brought us the tidings that there’d been an air raid last Sunday at a certain hospital base.
“The boche always does his dirty work on Sunday,” remarked the American captain. “It’s queer, too, because that’s the day that’s supposed to be kept holy, and I don’t see how the Kaiser squares himself with his friend Gott.”
I laughed, but the major managed to remain calm.

===Chinese===
下午，一位美国上尉来访，当然是匿名的。他下榻于总司令部（G.H.Q.，General Headquarters 之略），差事是研究英军的战略方法。他和少校讨论起美国人和英国人的差异。

「主要差异在温度，」上尉说，「你们这帮老兄跟冰川差不多热乎。在美国，我走到一个人跟前说：『我叫某某上尉。』他回敬：『我叫某某上校。』然后我们握手，开聊。可我要是走到一个英国人跟前说：『我叫某某上尉，』他说：『哦！』于是我被尴尬得半死，聊不下去了。」

「非同寻常！」少校说。

茶点时分，一名信使送来消息：上星期天，某医院基地遭了空袭。

「德国佬的脏活儿总是挑星期天干，」美国上尉评论道，「这也怪，星期天本该是守圣的日子，我不明白德皇怎么跟他的朋友上帝（Gott）交代。」

我笑了，少校则竭力保持镇定。

===Original===
The American captain departed after tea, and the major and I sat and bored each other till the Harvard professor and his illustrious companions returned. They told me I missed a very interesting trip. That’s the kind of trip one usually misses.
At dinner we resumed our enlightening discussion of Chinese, but it was interrupted when the major was called to the telephone. The message was from the captain who was supposed to meet Mr. Gibbons and Mr. O’Flaherty and take them to the trenches to spend the night. The captain reported that his machine had broken down with magneto trouble and he’d been unable to keep his appointment. He requested that the major have Mr. Gibbons and Mr. O’Flaherty located and brought home.
This was done. The disappointed correspondents blew in shortly before closing time and confided to me their suspicion that the trouble with the captain’s machine had not been magneto, but (the censor cut out a good line here).
Tomorrow we are to be shown the main British training school and the hospital bases.

===Chinese===
美国上尉喝完茶告辞了，少校和我便坐着互相无聊，直到哈佛教授和他那些显赫的旅伴归来。他们告诉我，我错过了一次非常有意思的游览。人错过的，通常就是这一种。

晚饭时我们重启了那场关于中文的启发性讨论，但少校被叫去接电话，讨论遂告中断。电话是那位上尉打来的，就是讲好去接吉本斯先生和奥弗莱厄蒂先生、送他们进战壕过夜的那位。上尉报告说，他的车因磁电机故障抛了锚，没法践约。他请少校设法把吉本斯先生和奥弗莱厄蒂先生找到并接回家来。

此事办妥了。两位失望的记者赶在宵禁前一刻旋风般进门，向我吐露了他们的怀疑：上尉那辆车的毛病恐怕不在磁电机，而在（审查官把这里一句妙语删掉了）。

明天安排我们参观英国主训练学校和各医院基地。

===Original===
Friday, September 7. With the British.
We left the château at nine and reached the training camp an hour later.
We saw a squad of ineligibles drilling, boys under military age who had run away from home to get into the Big Game. Their parents had informed the authorities of their ineligibility, and the authorities had refused to enroll them. The boys had refused to go back home, and the arrangement is that they are to remain here and drill till they are old enough to fight. Some of them are as much as three years shy of the limit.
The drill is made as entertaining as possible. The instructor uses a variation of our “Simon says: ‘Thumbs up.’ ” “O’Grady” sits in for Simon. For example, the instructor says: “O’Grady says: ‘Right dress.’ Left dress.” The youth who “left dresses” without O’Grady’s say-so is sent to the awkward squad in disgrace.
Out of a bunch of approximately two hundred only two went through the drill perfectly. The other one hundred and ninety-eight underestimated the importance of O’Grady and sheepishly stepped out of line. The two perfectos looked as pleased as peacocks.

===Chinese===
9 月 7 日，星期五。在英国人那儿。

九点钟离开城堡，一小时后抵达训练营。

我们看见一队「不够格者」在操练——些个没到兵役年龄、离家出走来赶这场大场面的男孩。他们的父母已向当局申报他们不够龄，当局拒绝收录。男孩们又拒绝回家，于是安排是：留在此地操练，练到够年龄上前线。其中有几位离年限还差着整整三岁。

操练办得尽可能有趣。教官用的是我们那套「西蒙（Simon）说：『立大拇指。』」的变体，由「奥格雷迪」（O'Grady）顶替西蒙上场。比如教官喊：「奥格雷迪说：『向右看齐。』向左看齐。」哪个小子没等奥格雷迪发话就「向左看齐」了，便丢人现眼地被打发进笨拙班。

一大批大约两百人里，只有两个一板一眼走完了全套。另外一百九十八个低估了奥格雷迪的分量，臊眉耷眼地出列认错。两位完人孔雀开屏似的得意。

===Original===
We saw a bayonet drill with a tutor as vivacious and linguistically original as a football coach, and were then taken to the bomb-throwing school. The tutor here was as deserving of sympathy as a Belgian. A bomb explodes five seconds after you press the button. Many of the pupils press the button, then get scared, drop the bomb and run. The instructor has to pick up the bomb and throw it away before it explodes and messes up his anatomy. And there’s no time to stop and figure in what direction you’re going to throw.
The Maoris were our next entertainers. The Maoris are colored gemmen from New Zealand. They were being taught how to capture a trench. Before they left their own dugout they sang a battle hymn that would make an American dance and scare a German to death. They went through their maneuvers with an incredible amount of pep and acted as if they could hardly wait to get into real action against the boche. Personally, I would have conscientious objections to fighting a Maori.
Then we were shown a gas-mask dress rehearsal. A British gas mask has a sweet scent, like a hospital. You can live in one, they say, for twenty-four hours, no matter what sort of poison the lovely Huns are spraying at you. We all tried them on and remarked on their efficacy, though we knew nothing about it.

===Chinese===
我们看了一场刺杀操练，教官的活力和用语的原创性不输橄榄球教练；随后被带去投弹学校。这儿的教官，其值得同情程度堪比比利时人。炸弹按下按钮五秒钟后爆炸。不少学员按下按钮，随即吓破了胆，撂下炸弹撒腿就跑。教官只好捡起炸弹，赶在它爆炸、把他一副骨架炸乱之前扔出去。而且根本没工夫停下来盘算该往哪个方向扔。

下一场献艺的是毛利人。毛利人是新西兰来的有色人种绅士。他们正在学习怎样夺取战壕。离开自家掩体之前，他们唱了一支战歌，能让美国人手舞足蹈，让德国人魂飞魄散。他们的演练元气充沛得令人难以置信，那架势仿佛迫不及待要跟德国佬真刀真枪干一场。就我个人而言，要是跟毛利人对打，我良心上先要抗议。

接着我们观摩了防毒面具的彩排。英国防毒面具有一股甜丝丝的气味，像医院。据说任凭可爱的匈奴兵朝你喷什么毒，你在里头都能活上二十四小时。我们一人试戴了一回，并对它的功效发表宏论——尽管我们对功效一无所知。

===Original===
We had lunch and were told we might make a tour of inspection of the hospitals in which the wounded lay. I balked at this and, instead, called on a Neenah, Wisconsin, doctor from whose knee had been extracted a sizable piece of shrapnel, the gift of last Sunday’s bomb dropper. This doctor has been over but three weeks, and the ship that brought him came within a yard of stopping a torpedo. Neither war nor Wisconsin has any terrors left for him.
Tomorrow we are to be taken right up to the front, dressed in helmets, gas masks, and everything.

===Chinese===
我们吃了午饭，人家说我们可以去各医院巡视一番伤员。这个我敬谢不敏，转而拜访了一位威斯康星州尼纳（Neenah）来的大夫——他的膝盖里刚取出一块相当可观的弹片，是上星期天那位投弹客馈赠的礼物。这位大夫过来才三个星期，载他来的那艘船离吃一颗鱼雷只差一码。如今无论战争还是威斯康星，都吓不着他了。

明天我们就要被一直送到前线去了，头戴钢盔、面戴防毒面具，全套武装。

===Original===
Saturday, September 8. With the British.
Two machine loads, containing us and our helmets, masks, and lunch baskets, got away to an early start and headed for the Back of the Front. In one car were the Captain with the Monocle, the Harvard prof., and the American philanthropist. The baggage, the philanthropist’s secretary, and I occupied the other. The secretary talked incessantly and in reverent tones of his master, whom he called The Doctor. One would have almost believed he considered me violently opposed to The Doctor (which I wasn’t, till later in the day) and was trying to win me over to his side with eulogistic oratory.
The first half of our journey was covered at the usual terrifying rate of speed. The last half was a snail’s crawl which grew slower and slower as we neared our objective. Countless troops, afoot and in motors, hundreds of ammunition and supply trucks, and an incredible number of businesslike and apparently new guns, these took up a healthy three-quarters of the road and, despite our importance, didn’t hunch to let us pass.
When we sounded our horns to warn of our approach, the subalterns, or whatever you call them, would look round, stand at attention and salute, first the Captain with the Monocle, and then, when our car came up, me. Me because I was the only one in the second machine who wore a British officer’s cap. I returned about three salutes, blushing painfully, and then threw my cap on the floor of the car and rode exposed. Saluting is a wear and tear on the right arm, and being saluted makes you feel slackerish and camouflagy, when you don’t deserve it.

===Chinese===
9 月 8 日，星期六。在英国人那儿。

两车人马，载着我们和我们的钢盔、面具、午餐篮，一早出发，直奔「前线的后方」。一辆车里坐着单片眼镜上尉、哈佛教授和美国慈善家。行李、慈善家的秘书和我乘坐另一辆。秘书一路上喋喋不休，用毕恭毕敬的语调谈论他的主子，他称之为「博士」（The Doctor）。听那口气，人们几乎要相信他认定我与「博士」不共戴天（其实还没有——当天晚些时候才算有了），并且正想用歌功颂德的雄辩把我争取到他们那一边去。

前半段路程照例以骇人的车速完成。后半段则成了蜗牛爬行，越接近目的地越慢。数不清的部队，步行的、坐车的；几百辆弹药卡车和补给卡车；还有数量多得难以置信的、一副公事公办派头、显然崭新的火炮——它们足足占据了路面四分之三的地方，全然不顾我们的重要性，不肯挪动分毫让路。

我们按响喇叭警告来者，那些低级军官——或者随你怎么称呼他们——便回头、立正、敬礼：先敬单片眼镜上尉，等我们的车赶到，再敬我。敬我，是因为第二辆车里只有我一人戴英军军官帽。我回敬了大约三个礼，脸红得苦不堪言，随后索性把帽子扔到车地板上，光着头坐车。敬礼这活儿磨右胳膊，而无端受敬——当你配不上时——会让你觉得自己像个逃兵兼冒牌货。

===Original===
We attained the foot of the observation hill round noon, left our machines, and ate our picnic lunch, consisting of one kind of sandwiches and three kinds of wine. Then we accomplished the long climb, stopping halfway up to don helmets and masks. Our guide told us that the boche, when not otherwise pleasantly employed, took a few shots at where we were standing to test his long-distance aim.
I wore the mask as long as I could, which was about half an hour. It was unpleasantly reminiscent of an operation I once had, the details of which I would set down here if I had time. Without it, I found, I could see things much more plainly. Through strong field glasses the British trenches were discernible. The German front line was behind a ridge, two hundred yards away﻿—from the British, not us﻿—and invisible. No drive was in progress, but there was the steady boom, boom of heavy guns, the scary siren, with a bang at the end, of grenades, and an occasional solo in a throaty baritone which our captain told us belonged to Mr. Trench Mortar.
The firing was all in one direction﻿—toward the northeast. Fritz was not replying, probably because he had no breath to waste in casual repartee.
Convinced that our hill was a zone of safety, for this afternoon at least, I wanted to stay up there and look and listen till it was time to go home. But our captain had arranged a trip to a sniping school, and our captain would rather have broken his monocle than have made the slightest alteration in the program for the day.

===Chinese===
晌午时分我们抵达观测山脚下，弃车野餐，吃的是一种三明治配三种酒。随后完成长途攀登，半山腰停下，戴上钢盔和面具。向导告诉我们，德国佬闲得没别的好消遣时，会朝我们站的方位放上几枪，试试他的远距离枪法。

面具我尽力戴了下去，尽力了约莫半个钟头。它令人不快地叫我回想起一次做过的手术，细节倘若有工夫，我本想记在这儿的。摘了它我发现，看东西反倒清楚得多。透过高倍望远镜，英军战壕隐约可辨。德军第一道防线在一道山脊后面，两百码开外——离英军两百码，不是离我们——看不见。攻势虽然不在进行，重炮的轰、轰声却绵延不断，手榴弹那骇人的哨音带一声爆响收尾，间或还有一段沙哑男中音的独唱，我们上尉介绍说，那是战壕迫击炮先生的嗓子。

炮火全朝一个方向——东北。弗里茨没有还嘴，大概是因为连随便斗个嘴的闲气都腾不出来。

确信我们这座山至少今天下午属于安全区之后，我很想一直待在上面，又看又听，直到该回家的钟点。可我们的上尉安排了去狙击学校的行程，而我们的上尉宁可摔碎他的单片眼镜，也不肯把当天的节目单改动一根毫毛。

===Original===
To the sniping school we went, and saw the snipers sniping on their snipes. It was just like the sniping school I had visited at the American camp, and I got pretty mad at our captain for dragging us away from a sight far more interesting. But he redeemed himself by having the major in charge show us real, honest-to-goodness camouflage, staged by an expert.
We were taken to a point two hundred yards distant from a trench system.
“Standing up in front of one of those trenches,” said the major, “there’s a sergeant in costume. He’s in plain sight. Now you find him.”
Well, we couldn’t find him, and we gave up.
“Move, Sergeant!” shouted the major.
The sergeant moved and, sure enough, there he was!
“I had him spotted all the time,” said The Doctor.
The major directed the sergeant to change to a costume of a different hue. When the change had been made we were required to turn our backs till he had “hidden” himself again. Again he was “in plain sight,” and again we had to give up. Again he was ordered to move, and we saw him, this time in colors diametrically opposed to those of his first garb.
“I had him spotted all the time,” said The Doctor.
The sergeant went through his entire repertory of tricks, but the rest must not be reported.

===Chinese===
狙击学校还是去了，看见狙击手们狙击他们的狙击目标。跟我早先在美国营地参观的那所狙击学校一模一样，我对我们上尉颇光火了一阵，怨他把我们从远为有趣的景象跟前拖走。不过他随后将功补过：请主管的少校给我们表演了真正的、如假包换的伪装术，专家亲自操刀。

我们被带到离一处战壕体系两百码的一个点上。

「在那几条战壕其中一条的前面，」少校说，「站着一位穿伪装服的中士。他就明摆在眼前。现在，你们把他找出来。」

得，我们找不出来，只好认输。

「动一动，中士！」少校喊道。

中士一动，果然，他在那儿呢！

「我从头到尾都锁定他了。」「博士」说。

少校吩咐中士换一身不同色系的行头。换装完毕，我们奉命转过身去，等他重新「藏」好。他又一次「明摆在眼前」，我们又只好认输。又命令他动一动，我们看见了他——这回他穿的颜色与第一套行头截然相反。

「我从头到尾都锁定他了。」「博士」说。

中士把他的全部看家把戏演了一遍，其余的恕不能奉报。

===Original===
It occurred to me on the way back to our machines that some football coach could make a fish out of the defensive team by camouflaging his back field.
Our captain and the Harvard prof. climbed into the front car, leaving The Doctor, his secretary, and me to bring up the rear. The sec. sat with the driver; The Doctor and I in the back seat.
“How long have you been over here?” inquired The Doctor at length.
I told him.
“How many American soldiers are there in France?”
I told him.
After an impressive pause, he said:
“As a matter of fact, there are really﻿—” And he increased my estimate by four hundred percent. “Of course,” he continued, “I have the right figures. They were furnished me by the Defense League before I left home. They naturally wouldn’t give them to a writer because they don’t want them published.”
“And naturally,” says I, “whenever they tell a writer anything in strict confidence, he rushes to the nearest Local and Long Distance Telephone Booth and gets Wilhelmstrasse on the wire.”
“Oh, no,” said The Doctor. “But a writer might think it was his duty to send the correct information to his paper.”
“Did you ever hear of the censorship?” I asked him.
“There are ways of eluding it.”
“And do you think all writers are that kind?”
He shrugged a fat shoulder.
“Not all, possibly a very few. But one never can tell the right kind from the wrong.”
His guard was down, and I took careful aim:
“Do you think the Defense League used good judgment in entrusting that secret to you, when you spill it to the first irresponsible reporter you happen to run across?”
If I hadn’t won this argument, I wouldn’t repeat it.
Not until we reached our château did I realize why I had been so catty. I’d gone without my tea.

===Chinese===
回车的路上我忽然想到：哪位橄榄球教练若给自家后卫群做上伪装，准能把对方防守队变成一群任钓的鱼。

我们的上尉和哈佛教授爬进了前一辆车，「博士」、他的秘书和我殿后。秘书跟司机坐一起；「博士」和我坐后座。

「您过来多久了？」「博士」终于开了口。

我告诉了他。

「法国现在有多少美国兵？」

我告诉了他。

一阵肃穆的停顿之后，他说：

「实不相瞒，实际上有——」说罢把我的估算数字上浮了百分之四百。「当然，」他接着说，「我手里是准确数字。临出国前国防同盟（Defense League）提供给我的。他们自然不肯把数字给一个作家，因为不想让它见报。」

「那自然，」我说，「他们但凡跟一个作家咬耳朵说句机密，那作家立马冲进最近的市内长途电话亭，接通威廉街。」

「哦，不，」「博士」说，「不过作家也许会觉得，把正确的情报发给自己报社是他的职责。」

「您听说过新闻审查么？」我问他。

「办法总归有的，绕得过去。」

「那您是不是觉得，作家全是这路人？」

他耸了耸肥厚的肩膀。

「不全是，兴许很少几个。可谁也分不清好人和坏人。」

他门户大开，我瞄准了要害：

「您把机密透露给撞见的头一个不着调的记者——您觉得国防同盟把这个秘密托付给您，是用了好判断力吗？」

这场辩论我要是没赢，我是不打算复述的。

回到城堡我才明白自己方才何以那么刻薄。原来我错过了下午茶。

===Original===
Sunday, September 9. Paris.
Mr. Gibbons and I this morning bade goodbye to our genial hosts and were driven to the station at which we arrived last Wednesday. On the Paris-bound train I wondered audibly why the servants had given me that queer look before we left.
“Did you tip them?” asked Mr. Gibbons.
“Certainly!” I snapped.
“I’ll bet I know,” said Mr. Gibbons. “You probably packed your own suitcase.”
He was right.

===Chinese===
9 月 9 日，星期日。巴黎。

今早吉本斯先生和我向殷勤的主人们道了别，被送到上星期三抵达的那个车站。在开往巴黎的火车上，我出声纳闷：临走前仆人们干吗那么古怪地看我。

「你给他们小费了吗？」吉本斯先生问。

「当然给了！」我没好气地说。

「我保准知道原因了，」吉本斯先生说，「八成是你自己动手收拾的手提箱。」

他说对了。

## VI How I Didn’t Drive Major Blank’s Car to Camp Such-and-Such / 六 我怎么没能把某某少校的车开去某某营地

===Original===
Monday, September 10. Paris.

The American major who owns the car which Mr. Kiley drove down from Le Havre, whither it had been sent by the man who bought it in London for the American major﻿—well, anyway, this American major, he’s in the artillery camp at Such-and-Such, and he wants me to bring it down there for him. I’ve never handled, or, rather, footled one of the little birds, but it’s something everybody should learn, like French and auction and how to swim. Besides, I want to see the artillery camp. So I’m accepting the commission and intend to get busy tomorrow morning.

===Chinese===
9 月 10 日，星期一。巴黎。

那辆车的主人是一位美国少校；基利（Kiley）先生把它从勒阿弗尔开了过来，而在那之前，车又是被替这位美国少校在伦敦买下它的那位先生寄到勒阿弗尔去的——得，总之这位美国少校，他如今在某某营地的炮兵营里，想让我把车给他开过去。这么一只小鸟儿我还从来没摆弄过——或者更准确地说，从来没拿脚伺候过，不过这是人人都该学的本事，跟法语、拍卖叫价和游泳是一个道理。再说，我也想见识见识炮兵营地。于是我接下了这桩差事，打算明天一早就动手。

===Original===
Tuesday, September 11. Paris.

With an American pass and an order for the car, I taxied to the United States army garage, in the Quai Debilly.

“*Avez-vous* fixed *vous* with passes?” inquired a friendly inmate of the garage.

I showed him my American card.

“That isn’t *bien suffisant*,” he said. “You’ll have to get a pink one to go through the French army zone.”
