I recalled then our troubles on a previous automobile trip and was glad he had spoken.

===Chinese===
9 月 11 日，星期二。巴黎。

手里攥着一张美军通行证和一张提车的单子，我打车去了美军车库，地点在德比利滨河道（Quai Debilly）。

「*Avez-vous* 给 *vous* 办好通行证了吗？（『你们』给『你们』——又是法语又是英语）」车库里一位和气的住客问道。

我把美国证件出示给他看。

「那个不算 *bien suffisant*（足够好用），」他说，「要穿过法军防区，你还得办一张粉红色的。」

我这才想起上一回汽车之旅的种种磨难，暗自庆幸他开了这个口。

===Original===
“Where do I go for that?” I inquired.

“Go,” said he, “to the Préfet de Ligne du Communications.” Or something like that.

“*Où* is *il*?”

“I think he’s in the Rue François Premier.”

“And is the car all right?”

“I guess so. Nos haven’t looked at it yet.”

===Chinese===
「上哪儿办去？」我问。

「去，」他说，「找『交通线省长』（Préfet de Ligne du Communications，法语英语杂糅的衙门名）。」大概就是这么个名目。

「*Où* 是 *il*？（『哪儿』是『他』——他在哪儿？）」

「我想他在弗朗索瓦一世街（Rue François Premier）。」

「车没问题吧？」

「应该吧。*Nos*（我们）还没瞧过它呢。」

===Original===
I had let my taxi go, and twenty minutes were spent in getting another. It was another hour before we located the *préfet*.

A secretary examined my passport and American pass and took my dossier:

Name, nationality, birthplace, age, ancestry, real purpose in coming to France. Hair﻿—black; forehead﻿—high; eyes﻿—brown; nose﻿—prominent; mouth﻿—medium; chin﻿—round; complexion﻿—dark; height﻿—six one and three-quarters. Sign here.

“Now,” said the sec., “monsieur will *avez* to come across *avec a photophie*.”

“I’m just out,” I said. “I’d no idea I’d be so popular.”

“*Nos* can issue no passes sans a *photophie*,” says he, so out I went in search of a rapid-fire studio.

===Chinese===
我先前把出租车打发走了，又花了二十分钟才叫到一辆。等找到那位省长（préfet）的衙门，又过去了一个钟头。

一位秘书查验了我的护照和美国通行证，然后录取我的档案（dossier）：

姓名、国籍、出生地、年龄、祖上、来法国的真实目的。头发——黑；额头——高；眼睛——棕；鼻子——突出；嘴巴——中等；下巴——圆；肤色——深；身高——六英尺一又四分之三英寸。此处签字。

「现在嘛，」秘书先生说，「先生就得 *avec*（带着）一张 *photophie*（照片，他对 photographie 的讹拼）*avez*（交上）过来了。」

「我手头刚好一张不剩，」我说，「没想到我这么抢手。」

「*Nos*（我们）不能 *sans*（缺着）*photophie* 发通行证。」他说。于是我出门找快照照相馆去了。

===Original===
The driver pulled up in front of a gallery on the Rue de la Paix, where the artist promised to have six copies of my map printed by *midi*.

To kill time I rode back to Billy’s *rue*.

“The car’s on the blink,” said my friend in French. “The connecting rod is *lâche* and some bearings are burned out. Besides, *vous* would be a rummy to *partir* on these tires.”

“*Comme beaucoup* new ones do *je* need?”

“Just plain *quatre*,” says he.

“Well,” says I, “put them on and get busy *avec* the reparations. I want to start away before dark.”

===Chinese===
司机在和平街（Rue de la Paix）一家照相馆前停了车，馆里的艺术家答应在 *midi*（正午）前把我这张「地图」（map，戏指本人尊容）印出六份来。

为了消磨工夫，我坐车折回了「比利那条街」（Billy’s *rue*，即德比利滨河道那边）。

「车子出毛病了，」我那位朋友用「法语」说。「连杆 *lâche*（松了），有几副轴承烧坏了。再说，*vous*（您）要开这副轮胎 *partir*（上路），准是个傻子。」

「*Comme beaucoup*（多少——他把 Combien 又念岔了）条新的 *je*（我）需要？」

「不多不少，*quatre*（四条）。」他说。

「那，」我说，「装上吧，*avec*（着手）搞修理（reparations）忙活起来。我要赶在天黑前动身。」

===Original===
“Ah, *oui*,” says he, “but we have no tires and we have no tools to make the reparations *avec*.”

“Can’t you get them?”

“*Vous devoir* get them yourself.”

“*Où?*”

“At the branch factory of the —﻿—﻿—,” and he said the name of the car right out loud.

“*Où est le* branch factory?”

“*Il est* in *un* suburb﻿—Le Vallois-Perret. The address is 6163 Rue Corneille.”

===Chinese===
「啊，*oui*（是呀），」他说，「可我们这儿没有轮胎，也没有 *avec*（拿来）搞修理的工具。」

「你不能去弄来吗？」

「*Vous devoir*（您得）。自己去弄。」

「*Où*（去哪儿）？」

「在———的分厂，」说着他把那车的牌子一五一十大声念了出来。

「*Où est le*（哪儿是那个）分厂？」

「*Il*（它）在 *un*（一个）郊区——勒瓦卢瓦-佩雷（Le Vallois-Perret）。地址是高乃依街 6163 号（Rue Corneille）。」

===Original===
“What tools are required?”

“*Une roue-tirer et un* offset *clef à vis*.”

Which means a wheel puller and an offset wrench.

“And can *je aussi* tires get there?”

“Ah, *oui*.”

===Chinese===
「需要什么工具？」

「*Une roue-tirer et un* offset *clef à vis*（一个拉轮器和一把偏头扳手）。」

意思就是一个拉轮器（wheel puller）和一把偏头扳手（offset wrench）。

「还有，*je aussi*（我-也）能在那儿弄到轮胎吗？」

「啊，*oui*（能）。」

===Original===
It was noon, and my trusting driver and I returned to the studio on the Rue de la Paix. The pictures weren’t *fini*. They never are.

“Take me to Maxim’s,” says I, “and we’ll call it a half day.”

After lunch I walked back to the studio. The pictures were not *fini*, but would monsieur *rester*? Monsieur would. Monsieur rested till fourteen o’clock, got six *photophies* that had him looking more than ever like a German spy, and taxied back to the Rue François Premier. The *préfet*’s joint was closed.

===Chinese===
到了正午，我和我那位深信不疑的司机回到和平街的照相馆。照片还没 *fini*（完工）。照片从来就不会准时完工。

「送我去马克西姆（Maxim’s），」我说，「今天就算干了半天。」

午饭之后，我步行回到照相馆。照片仍未 *fini*，不过先生愿否 *rester*（稍等片刻）？先生愿意。先生一直等到十四点钟，拿到了六张让他比以往任何时候都更像德国间谍的 *photophie*（照片），随即打车回到弗朗索瓦一世街。省长的衙门关门了。

===Original===
I asked the driver how far it was out to Le Vallois-Perret.

“Come on,” he said, and I climbed in, but “come on,” in French, means “I don’t get you,” so I had to repeat the directions four or five times.

“Ah, *oui*,” he said at last. “Le Vallois-Perret. *Quatorze kilomet*’s.”

“What is that in American money?”

“Come on,” said the driver.

“Hotel Con-tin-en-tal,” I said.

I’ll tackle ’em afresh tomorrow morning.

===Chinese===
我问司机，到勒瓦卢瓦-佩雷有多远。

「来吧（Come on）。」他说，我便上了车。可是法语里的「来吧」，意思是「我没听懂」，于是我只好把地址重复了四五遍。

「啊，*oui*，」他总算开了口，「勒瓦卢瓦-佩雷。*Quatorze kilomet*（十四公里，kilomètre 的讹拼）。」

「这个数折成美国钱是多少？」

「来吧。」司机说。

「大——陆——饭——店（Con-tin-en-tal）。」我说。

明天一早，我再重新对付他们。

===Original===
Wednesday, September 12. Paris.

The *préfet*’s secretary approved my picture and gave me a beautiful salmon-colored pass. It is good for five days, which is plenty, as I will come back on the train.

At the city gates, en route to Le Vallois-Perret, my taxi and I were stopped and our essence measured. If we brought back more than we took out, we would have to pay taxes on the difference.

*Quatorze kilomet*’s was a very conservative estimate of the distance, and it was nearly eleven when we reached Cornelia’s rue and the branch factory.

===Chinese===
9 月 12 日，星期三。巴黎。

省长的秘书认可了我的照片，发给我一张漂亮的鲑鱼色通行证。有效期五天，绰绰有余，因为回程我坐火车。

在去勒瓦卢瓦-佩雷的路上，城门口，我和我的出租车被拦了下来，汽油（essence）接受了测量。要是带回来的比带出去的多，超出部分我们就得缴税。

对于这段路程来说，*Quatorze kilomet* 是个相当保守的估计；等我们开到「高乃依那条街」和那家分厂门口，已经快十一点了。

===Original===
An American heard my plea for four new tires, an offset wrench, and a wheel puller.

“It can’t be done,” he said. “All we do is own this place. But the French Government has taken it over and runs it.”

“But this is a United States army car,” I said, “and we’re supposed to be allies of the French.”

“Without special permission,” said he, “you stand as much chance as if you were the Crown Prince.”

“Where can I get special permission?”

“Your best bet is to see Captain Vandervelde. If anybody can fix it, he’s the boy. You’ll find him in the Passage de Haynau, Rue Croix Nivert.”

===Chinese===
一位美国人听取了我对四条新轮胎、一把偏头扳手和一个拉轮器的恳求。

「办不到，」他说，「我们对这地方的全部权限，就是拥有它。可是法国政府已经把它接管，由人家经营。」

「可这是辆美国陆军的军车，」我说，「何况我们跟法国人理应是盟友啊。」

「没有特别许可，」他说，「你这指望，就跟你是德国皇太子（Crown Prince）差不多。」

「特别许可上哪儿领去？」

「你最好的赌注是去找范德维尔德上尉（Captain Vandervelde）。要说有谁能办成事，非他小子莫属。他在克鲁瓦尼韦街（Rue Croix Nivert）的阿约通道（Passage de Haynau）。」

===Original===
“What number?”

“There is no number.”

I thanked him, or perhaps I forgot to, and returned to my taxi.

“Passage de Haynau in Rue Croix Nivert,” I said.

“*Q’numéro?*”

“There ain’t none.”

===Chinese===
「几号？」

「没有号。」

我谢过了他，也可能是忘了谢，然后回到出租车上。

「克鲁瓦尼韦街的阿约通道。」我说。

「*Q’numéro*？（几——号？）」

「就是没有号。」

===Original===
“Come on,” demanded the driver.

“I told you there was no number. We’ll just have to keep looking till we find it.”

We convinced the guardian of the gate that we weren’t trying to cheat on gasoline, and rolled into Rue Croix Nivert about thirteen o’clock. My chauffeur sat nonchalantly in his accustomed seat while I made a house-to-house canvass of Haynau’s Passage. The last house was the right one. I knew it in an instant, for when I entered the corridor a French sentry popped up and placed the end of his bayonet within an inch of Nose-prominent.

“Captain Vandervelde,” said I, making a short strategical retreat.

“Come on,” said Frenchy without lowering his sticker.

A password was what he wanted, and Mr. Poincaré had forgotten to call me up and give me the correct one for the day. I produced a two-franc piece and held it out. The sentry withdrew his weapon, accepted the coin, and allowed me to pass.

===Chinese===
「来吧。」司机不依道。

「我说了没有号。咱们只好一路找下去，找到为止。」

我们说服了守门人，让他相信我们没打算在汽油上捣鬼，然后在十三点钟左右驶入克鲁瓦尼韦街。我的司机师傅气定神闲地坐在他惯常的座位上，由我对阿约通道展开了挨家挨户的摸排。最后一户正是要找的那家。我一眼便知没错了，因为刚一踏进门厅，一名法国哨兵就蹦了出来，把刺刀尖顶到了「鼻子——突出」前方一英寸处。

「范德维尔德上尉。」我说，同时来了个短促的战术性撤退。

「来吧。」这位法国兵说，手里那根签子纹丝不放。

他要的是口令，而普恩加莱先生（Mr. Poincaré，法国总统）忘了打电话把当天正确的口令告诉我。我掏出一枚两法郎的硬币递过去。哨兵收起武器，收下硬币，放我通行。

===Original===
“The word,” I thought to myself, “must be *Liberté, Egalité, Fraternité*.”

Captain Vandervelde was in and made me wait only half an *heure*, the while I thought more than once of yon taxi. Finally I was summoned to the inner office.

“What can *je faire pour vous*?” he inquired.

I told him I wanted an order on the —﻿—﻿— branch factory for some tools and four new tires.

“*Rien fairing* on the tires,” he said.

“*Pourquoi*?” I asked him.

===Chinese===
「那句口令，」我心想，「想必就是『自由、平等、博爱』（*Liberté, Egalité, Fraternité*）了。」

范德维尔德上尉在，只叫我等了半个 *heure*（钟头），其间我不止一次惦念着外头那辆出租车。终于，我被召进了里面的办公室。

「有什么 *je* 能 *faire pour vous*（为您效劳）的？」他问道。

我告诉他，我想要一张向———分厂提取若干工具和四条新轮胎的单子。

「轮胎的事，*rien fairing*（啥也办不成——rien faire 的英法杂拼）。」他说。

「*Pourquoi*（为什么）？」我问他。

===Original===
“Orders *pour* tires must come from the Maison de la Guerre.”

“Can you fix me for the tools?”

“Ah, *oui*. What tools *voulez-vous*?”

“*Une roue-tirer et un* offset *clef à vis*.”

“*Votre* papers, *s’il vous plaît*.”

I handed him passport, American pass, and salmon-pink card. He glanced them over, then began rummaging in a drawer. I knew what was coming﻿—another dossier.

===Chinese===
「轮胎的单子，必须由陆军部（Maison de la Guerre）开具。」

「工具的事您能给我办吗？」

「啊，*oui*。*Voulez-vous*（您想要）什么工具？」

「*Une roue-tirer et un* offset *clef à vis*（一个拉轮器和一把偏头扳手）。」

「*Votre*（您的）证件，*s’il vous plaît*（劳驾）。」

我递上护照、美国通行证和那张鲑鱼粉卡片。他草草翻检一遍，然后开始在抽屉里翻找。我知道接下来是什么——又一份档案。

===Original===
“*Avez-vous une photophie*?” he asked.

“Ah, *oui*,” says I, and slipped him one of the remaining five.

He kept the dossier and *photophie* for the amusement of himself and progeny. He gave me only a mauve card which said I was entitled to one wheel puller and one left-handed offset monkey wrench.

I told my driver we had to hurry right back to Le Vallois-Perret. He looked crestfallen.

“*Je* have had no *déjeûner*,” he said.

“Neither have *je*,” I said, and climbed in.

===Chinese===
「*Avez-vous une photophie*（您有照片吗）？」他问。

「啊，*oui*（有）。」我说着，从剩下的五张里抽出一张塞给了他。

档案和 *photophie* 他留下了，供他自己和他的子子孙孙取乐。给我的只是一张淡紫色卡片，上书：本人有权领取拉轮器一件、左手偏头活络扳手一把。

我告诉司机，我们得火速赶回勒瓦卢瓦-佩雷。他一脸垂头丧气。

「*Je*（我）还没吃上 *déjeûner*（午饭）。」他说。

「*Je*（我）也没吃呢。」我说着，钻进了车。

===Original===
Thursday, September 13. Paris.

Up early and to the garage. Delivered the tools. “*Vous* had better buy a tire pump,” said my adviser.

“*Je* suppose,” said I, “that I’ll have to get an order for one from Papa Joffre.”

“No,” he said. “That’s *une chose vous* can buy sans an order.”

“*Voulez-vous* get to work on the car right away?”

“Ah, *oui*,” says he.

===Chinese===
9 月 13 日，星期四。巴黎。

一早起身，去了车库。工具交割完毕。「*Vous*（您）最好买个打气筒，」我的顾问说。

「*Je*（我）琢磨着，」我说，「这个也得找霞飞老爹（Papa Joffre，法军元帅）开张单子吧。」

「不，」他说，「那是 *une chose*（一样东西），*vous*（您）*sans*（不用）单子就能买。」

「*Voulez-vous*（你们愿不愿）马上动手修车？」

「啊，*oui*。」他说。

===Original===
I asked my chauffeur to take me to a *maison du* tire pumps. We found one on the Champs Élysées. Other things for sale in the store were watches and perfumery. I proceeded thence to French General Headquarters.

The gentleman authorized to sign orders for tires received me cordially and spoke English.

“Certainly,” he said in answer to my request, “if the car is for an American officer. And what is the license number?”

I had to confess I didn’t know.

“Well,” said he, “you go to the garage and find out. Then come back and I’ll give you the order.”

I went to the garage to find out. There was no license.

===Chinese===
我请司机师傅送我去一家『*maison du*（售卖）打气筒』的铺子。我们在香榭丽舍大街（Champs Élysées）找到一家。店里发售的其他商品还有手表和香水。随后我便前往法军总司令部。

那位有权签发轮胎单子的先生热忱地接待了我，而且说的是英语。

「当然可以，」他答复我的请求道，「只要这车是给一位美国军官用的。那么，牌照号码是多少？」

我只好坦白说不知道。

「那，」他说，「你回车库查清楚，回来我就给你开单子。」

我回车库去查。压根就没有牌照。

===Original===
“*Où* can *je* get one?” I asked my friend.

He gave me the address of the license bureau, on Rue Oskaloosa or something. The driver knew where it was.

Monsieur du License surprised me by asking for a picture and taking my description, which I could almost have rhymed by this time﻿—

> Hair jet black, but a paucity of it;
 
>  Forehead high as the Eiffel tower;
 
>  Prominent nose, but it’s mine; I love it;
 
>  Eyes the brown of the pansy flower;
 
>  Medium mouth, not the best for kisses;
 
>  Chin as round as a billiard ball;
 
>  Dark complected﻿—Oh, Mister, this is
 
>  Me, and I’m better than six feet tall.

===Chinese===
「*Où*（哪儿）能 *je*（让我）弄一块？」我问我的朋友。

他给了我牌照局的地址，在奥斯卡卢萨街（Rue Oskaloosa）还是哪条街上。司机认得地方。

牌照局先生（Monsieur du License）又要照片又要外貌描述，令我大吃一惊；这份描述到这会儿我几乎都能押着韵背出来了——

> 头发乌黑，可惜所剩不富余；
>
> 额头高耸，堪比埃菲尔铁塔；
>
> 鼻子突出，好在是我自己的，我爱它；
>
> 眼睛棕色，恰似三色堇之花；
>
> 嘴巴中等，接吻并非行家；
>
> 下巴浑圆，赛过台球一颗；
>
> 肤色黝黑——先生啊，这正是
>
> 在下我，身高六英尺还多。

===Original===
“What *est* the *numéro* of the engine?”

“Four hundred and fifty-six thousand three hundred and four,” I replied sans batting an eyelash.

He took it down and disappeared into an adjoining room. In a little while he returned with a license plate﻿—secondhand to match the car.

I carried it along to display to the man at G.H.Q., as it is technically known.

“*Où* can I get the tires?” I asked.

“Anywhere, with that order,” he said.

===Chinese===
「发动机的 *numéro*（号码）*est*（是）多少？」

「四十五万六千三百零四。」我应道，连眼皮都没 *sans*（眨——他把英语俗语「眼都不眨」也塞进了个法语）一下。

他记了下来，进了隔壁一间屋。不多时，他拿着一块牌照回来了——二手货，倒跟那辆车正般配。

我带着它去出示给总司令部（G.H.Q.，人家的正规术语叫法）里的那位先生。

「*Où*（哪儿）能买到轮胎？」我问。

「有了那张单子，哪儿都行。」他说。

===Original===
So I told the driver to go anywhere, and he misunderstood and took me everywhere. The tire *maison* he chose was as far away as he could drive without crossing the Swiss border.

“Now back to the United States garage,” said I, and we arrived just as they were closing.

My friend told me the car had been “taken down.” When I saw it I was convinced that the “taking down” had been accomplished with shrapnel.

“How many months will it take to put it together again?” I asked.

“*Très* few minutes,” said the mechanic. “It will be all finished tomorrow *midi*.”

“It looks all finished now.”

===Chinese===
于是我吩咐司机随便去个地方，他理解偏了，拉着我把所有地方逛了个遍。他选的那家轮胎 *maison*（铺子），远得再往前开一步就要越境进瑞士了。

「现在回美军车库。」我说。我们赶到的时候，人家正打烊。

我的朋友告诉我，车已经「拆开」了。一见实物我立刻确信，这场「拆解」是靠榴霰弹完成的。

「重新装回去得几个月？」我问。

「*Très*（很）少几分钟，」机械师说，「明天 *midi*（正午）保准全部完工。」

「它现在这副样子看着就已经挺完工的了。」

===Original===
“*Avez-vous votre* license?” he inquired.

I displayed it triumphantly.

“Ah, *oui*,” he said. “But that’s just the license for the car. *Vous* must *aussi* have a driver’s license.”

“*Bonne nuit!*” I yelped. “And what for?”

“*C’est la loi*,” said he. “Everybody who drives in France must have one.”

“How do you get it?”

===Chinese===
「*Avez-vous votre*（您有没有您的）牌照？」他问。

我得意扬扬地出示了。

「啊，*oui*，」他说，「可那只是车自己的牌照。*Vous*（您）还必须 *aussi*（另外）有一张驾驶员执照。」

「*Bonne nuit!*（好家伙——他又拿『晚安』当惊叹词使）」我嚎道，「这又是为哪般？」

「*C’est la loi*（这是法律），」他说，「凡在法国开车的人，人人必有一张。」

「怎么个领法？」

===Original===
“You’ll have to go to the Chef de Traffic Police and pass the examination.”

“How long does it take?”

“*Très* brief. Not more than *une heure*.”

“Well, will you guarantee to have the car all ready when I come for it at noon tomorrow?”

“*Je* promise,” he said, and I drove back to the hotel.

Oh, Major, wait till you see that taxi bill!

===Chinese===
「你得去找交通警察总监（Chef de Traffic Police），通过考试。」

「要耗多长时间？」

「*Très*（非常）简短。不超过 *une heure*（一个钟头）。」

「那好，明天中午我来提车，你担保车能全部弄妥吗？」

「*Je*（我）担保（promise）。」他说。于是我便坐车回饭店了。

哦，少校，等您瞧见那叠出租车账单再说吧！

===Original===
Friday, September 14. Paris.

The traffic chief said that before he could examine me for a license I must show him my registration card from a regular police commissioner. I had been told I ought to have one of those darn things, but had passed it up. Now I was face to face with the necessity of acquiring the card and doing it quick. The nearest station was only a few blocks away. I found it jampacked with people who looked as if they all worked in East St. Louis. I flagged an attendant.

“I want to register,” I told him.

“You’ll be called when it’s your turn,” he said, and gave me a number. It was 89,041.

“How long will I have to wait?”

He pondered.

===Chinese===
9 月 14 日，星期五。巴黎。

交通总监说，考核我驾照之前，我必须先出示由正牌警察局长签发的登记卡。早有人叮嘱过我，那类劳什子玩意儿理应办一张，可我一直没当回事。这下子，我不得不面对现实：非弄到那张卡不可，还得快。最近的警察局只隔几个街区。到那儿一看，挤得水泄不通，满屋子的人个个看着像在东圣路易斯（East St. Louis）上班。我拦下了一名工作人员。

「我要办登记。」我告诉他。

「轮到您了会叫号的。」说着他发给我一个号。是 89041 号。

「我得等多久？」

他寻思了一下。

===Original===
“I think they’re now in the twenty-thousands,” he said.

Suddenly I bethought me of a document in my pocket, a letter from the boss of the Maison de la Presse. I flashed it on him.

“Ah-h-h!” he sighed, and led me through the mob to the inner shrine.

In ten minutes I had my card. The commissioner didn’t even want a picture, or nothin’. I plunged through the gang again and was stared at enviously. Some of the poor blokes have undoubtedly been waiting there since the Kaiser was forced into the war.

===Chinese===
「我记得眼下才叫到两万多号。」他说。

猛然间我记起口袋里有份文件——新闻部（Maison de la Presse）主管的一封信。我把它朝他一亮。

「啊——！」他喟然一叹，领着我穿过人海，直入内殿。

十分钟后，卡到手了。局长居然连照片都不要，什么都不要。我又一头扎出人堆，招来一路艳羡的目光。有些可怜哥们儿，无疑从德皇被迫卷入大战那年起，就一直坐在那儿等。

===Original===
Again I appeared before the traffic chief. “Of course,” he said, “I will have to examine your papers. And *avez-vous une photophie*?”

I came through.

“Now,” I said, “we’re fifty-fifty. You have one and I have one.”

But he wasn’t listening. He was rummaging for the deadly dossier.

“This,” he said, when he had found one, “will have to be filled out.”

“Yes,” I replied, “I think I recall filling one out last time I was in France.”

===Chinese===
我再度出现在交通总监面前。「当然，」他说，「我得查验一下您的证件。另外，*avez-vous une photophie*（您有照片吗）？」

我又交出了一张。

「这下，」我说，「咱们二一添作五了。您一张，我一张。」

可他根本没在听。他正在翻找那要命的档案。

「这个，」他找出来一份之后说，「必须填写。」

「是，」我答道，「我记得上回在法国的时候，也填过一份来着。」

===Original===
“This car belongs to an American army officer?”

“Ah, *oui*.”

“What does he intend to do with the car?”

The temptation was strong to say he intended using it to tour the trenches. But it was no time to trifle.

“He expects to ride round the camp in it, sir. He is in one of the high commands and has to do a lot of inspecting.”

“Do you know the traffic laws of Paris?”

===Chinese===
「这辆车归一位美国陆军军官所有？」

「啊，*oui*（是）。」

「他打算拿这车派什么用场？」

我有一股强烈的冲动，想说他打算开着它周游战壕。但眼下不是闹着玩的时候。

「他指望坐它在营区里兜兜风，长官。他在高级指挥机关里任职，视察任务繁重得很。」

「您知道巴黎的交通法规吗？」

===Original===
“Ah, *oui*.”

He didn’t ask me what they were. But I could have told him. Any part of the street you like, with a minimum speed limit of forty miles on the straightaway and sixty-five miles round the corners.

“You are going to take the car right out of Paris?”

“Ah, *oui*.”

“That’s all,” he said, and handed me a driver’s license, horizon blue with saffron stripes.

I thanked him and bowed myself out of the place.

===Chinese===
「啊，*oui*。」

他没有问我法规的内容是什么。可我本来是答得上来的：街道随便哪一侧，随您挑；最低限速直线四十英里，转弯六十五英里。

「您这就打算把车直接开出巴黎？」

「啊，*oui*。」

「好了。」他说着递给我一张驾驶执照，地平线蓝，带藏红花色条纹。

我谢过了他，一路打着躬退出了那个地方。

===Original===
“From now on,” I thought, “it’s clear sailing.”

The car was ready. I had in my mind’s eye a nearby unfrequented street, where I was going to master the driving of it in ten minutes. Then I was going to shoot her up to the hotel, get my baggage and leave town.

“How about gas and oil?” I inquired.

“Oil, *oui*, but essence, no,” said the mechanic.

“Well, throw in ten gallons,” said I.

“Ah, but has monsieur an essence ticket?”

===Chinese===
「从此以后，」我心想，「一帆风顺了。」

车已备好。我心里已相中附近一条清静无人的街，打算在那儿用十分钟把驾驶术练精。然后一溜烟把车开到饭店，取上行李，出城走人。

「汽油和机油怎么说？」我问。

「机油，*oui*（有）；汽油（essence）嘛，*non*（没有）。」机械师说。

「那，来十加仑。」我说。

「啊，可是先生有汽油票吗？」

===Original===
Monsieur never heard of it.

“Ah, then, monsieur can get no essence.”

“Well for﻿—” and monsieur used harsh words.

“Monsieur can easily obtain a ticket,” said the guy when things had quieted down. “Monsieur’s military passes will be *suffisant*.”

“Where at?”

“At the Maison du Contrôle de l’Essence.”

===Chinese===
先生闻所未闻。

「啊，那么，先生就弄不到汽油。」

「这不——」于是先生骂了几句难听的。

「先生弄一张票不难，」等风波平息下来，那家伙说道，「先生的军事通行证就 *suffisant*（够用的了）。」

「上哪儿弄？」

「汽油管理局（Maison du Contrôle de l’Essence）。」

===Original===
“And that is﻿—?”

“*Vingt sept*, Rue Yaki Hula Hickey Dula.”

“Is that as far away as it sounds?”

“Monsieur can go there and be back in *une heure*.”

Monsieur crawled wearily into a taxi and started for Honolulu. The military passes did prove *suffisant*, and there was no trouble getting a fifty-gallon book at two francs per gal.

“I’ll save time now,” I thought. “I’ll pick up my baggage on the way back to the garage.”

===Chinese===
「那地方在——？」

「*Vingt sept*（二十七号），亚基胡拉希基杜拉街（Rue Yaki Hula Hickey Dula，仿当时夏威夷风流行歌 Yaka Hula Hickey Dula 胡诌的街名）。」

「那地方有它听上去那么远吗？」

「先生去了，*une heure*（一个钟头）之内准能打来回。」

先生拖着疲惫的身子爬进一辆出租车，向檀香山进发。军事通行证果然 *suffisant*（管用），顺顺当当买到一本五十加仑的汽油票，每加仑两法郎。

「这回能省点时间了，」我心想，「回车库的路上顺道把行李取上。」

===Original===
So I told my driver to stop at the hotel. A telegram was waiting there for me.

“Hold car in Paris,” it said. “Camp may be moved any day.”

This blow fell at fourteen o’clock this afternoon. By half-past fifteen I had called up every steamship office and learned that the next boat for America would leave from England next Wednesday night. I am going to be aboard.

And now I have for sale, at auction:

- One pass through the French war zone.

- One pass good in the American camp.

- One driver’s license.

- One book of essence tickets.

- One road map.

- One registration card.

I think I will leave the four tires and the offset *clef à vis* and the wheel puller with the car. Also the car’s license. The major is perfectly trustworthy. I only hope he doesn’t get killed before my expense account reaches him.

===Chinese===
于是我吩咐司机在饭店门口停一停。一封电报正在那儿等着我。

「车留巴黎。」电报上说，「营地随时可能迁移。」

这一记打击，于今天下午十四点钟当头落下。到十五点三十分，我已给每一家轮船公司打过电话，查明下一班去美国的船下周三夜里从英国启航。我打算就在那条船上。

现在，本人在此公开拍卖：

- 法国战区通行证一张。

- 美军营地有效通行证一张。

- 驾驶执照一张。

- 汽油票一本。

- 路线图一张。

- 登记卡一张。

我想，那四条轮胎、那把偏头扳手（*clef à vis*）和那个拉轮器，就随车留下吧。车的牌照也一样。少校这个人，绝对信得过。我只盼他别在我那份报销单送达之前阵亡。

## VII I Start Home, with a Stopover at London / 七 我启程回国，中途在伦敦逗留

===Original===
Saturday, September 15. Paris.

The gentleman at the American Embassy, which I visited late yesterday afternoon, spake truth when he said it was some job to get away from this place.

“If you want to leave on Sunday,” quoth he, “you’ll have to rise early Saturday and keep going all day. See our consul first thing in the morning, and he’ll tell you all you have to do.”

So I saw our consul first thing this morning. In fact, I beat him to his office. When he came in he was cordial and unsuspicious, rare qualities in a consul. He stamped my passport “*Bon pour se rendre en Amérique par Grande Bretagne*” and a great deal more.

“Now,” he said, “you’ll have to be viséed by the *préfet de* police and approved by the British Military Control. I don’t know in what order. They change it every two or three days to keep you guessing.”

I chose the British Control first and, of course, was wrong. But it took an hour to find this out.

There was a big crowd of us, and we were all given numbers, as in a barber shop of a Saturday night. But the resemblance to the barber shop ceased with the giving, for they called us regardless of number. A guinea sitting next to me was 42 and I was 18. He preceded me into the sanctum. And I got there ahead of No. 12, a British matron.

===Chinese===
9 月 15 日，星期六。巴黎。

昨天傍晚偏晚的时候我去了一趟美国大使馆，那里那位先生说的一句大实话是：想离开这地方，确是一桩大工程。

「你要是想星期天走，」他如是曰，「星期六一早就得爬起来，一整天马不停蹄。头一件事就是一早去见咱们领事，他会把你要办的事一样样告诉你。」

于是今天一早，我头一件事就去见了咱们领事。实际上，我比他先到了他的办公室。他进门时态度热络，毫无疑心——这在领事身上是难得的品质。他在我的护照上盖了章：「*Bon pour se rendre en Amérique par Grande Bretagne*（准予取道大不列颠前往美洲）」，此外还有一大串。

「现在，」他说，「你还得由警察总监（préfet de police）签发签证，再经英国军事管制处批准。先后次序我也不清楚。人家每两三天换一回，就为让你猜不着。」

我挑了英国管制处先去，当然，猜错了。不过花了一个钟头才发现这一点。

我们一大批人，人人领了个号，跟星期六晚上的理发店一个样。但与理发店的相似之处也止于发号，因为人家叫号全然不管号码先后。坐我旁边的一个意大利佬是 42 号，我是 18 号。他比我先进圣殿。而我呢，抢在了 12 号——一位英国太太——前头。

===Original===
My session was brief.

“The police visé must come first,” said the officer in charge.

Monsieur le Préfet has his office conveniently located about eight miles away from the Control, over the river. And he’s on the fourth floor of a building constructed before the invention of the elevator. From behind an untrimmed hedge of black whiskers he questioned me as to my forebears, musical tastes and baseball preferences. Then he retired into chambers and presently issued forth with my passport, on which his stamp had been added to the beautiful collection already there. It says I’m *Bon* for a trip to Amérique *par* Angleterre, so I don’t know whether I’m to go that way or through Grande Bretagne.

Thence back to Rue Napoléon Lajoie, and another long wait.

“Yes,” said the officer when my turn came again, “the visé is all right, but where is your steamship ticket? You’ll have to show that before we can pass you.”

===Chinese===
我那一回合很短暂。

「警察的签证必须先办。」主管军官说。

总监大人的衙门，位置便利，就在河对岸离管制处约八英里开外。而且他设在四楼，楼是电梯发明之前盖的。他从一丛没修剪过的黑胡须篱笆后面发问，问我的祖宗三代、音乐品味和棒球偏好。然后他退入密室，不多时拿着我的护照出来，上面的美丽收藏又添了他的一个章。章上说我 *Bon*（准予）取道英格兰（Angleterre）前往美洲（Amérique），所以我至今闹不清自己该走那条道，还是走大不列颠（Grande Bretagne）那条。

接着回到拿破仑·拉乔伊街（Rue Napoléon Lajoie，拿的是棒球明星的名字），又是一场漫长的等候。

「行了，」再轮到我时军官说，「签证是没问题了，可你的船票呢？得先出示船票，我们才能放你。」

===Original===
In order to show it I had to go and buy it, and in order to buy it I had to scare up some money, which is no mere child’s play in Gay Paree these days. I called on four people before I found one who was touchable. With what he grudgingly forked over I hastened to the booking office and felt at home there, it being on Rue Scribe. There was a customer ahead of me—our president’s youngest son-in-law.

“Do you know who that was?” said the agent excitedly when the young man had departed.

“Yes,” I replied, “but we don’t speak to each other.”

“Now,” said the agent, “I’m afraid I’ll have to ask you a few questions. It’s annoying, I know, but it’s the wartime rule.”

“Shoot,” I told him. “I’m thoroughly used to being annoyed.”

He ran through the familiar list and saved a new one for the windup.

“Why are you going to America?”

I could have spent an entire week replying to that, but even minutes were precious.

“Because it’s where I live,” proved satisfactory.

He apologized again for having to propound the queries, which shows he must be new on the job. The rest of them don’t care whether you like it or not. I signed six or seven pledges, gave over the bulk of my borrowed fortune, and set out again with my ticket for the Rue Jacques Johnson. I got there just in time, for they close early on Saturday. Other days the poor devils have to work right through from ten to four.

===Chinese===
要出示，就得先去买；要买，就得先凑钱——如今这年月在快活巴黎（Gay Paree，美国人对巴黎的俚称），凑钱可不是小孩子过家家。我拜访了四个人，才访着一位肯出血的。揣着他肉痛掏出来的那笔钱，我火速奔向售票处，到了那儿倒有宾至如归之感，因为它坐落在斯克里布街上。我前头有一位客人——我们总统最年轻的女婿。

「你知道刚才那位是谁吗？」年轻人走后，售票员兴奋地说。

「知道，」我答道，「不过我们彼此不讲话。」

「现在，」售票员说，「恐怕我得问你几个问题。我知道烦人，可这是战时规矩。」

「放马过来，」我告诉他，「烦人这事，我早彻底习惯了。」

他把那套熟悉的问题清单过了一遍，还留了个新的压轴。

「你为什么要回美国？」

这个问题我本可以花整整一星期来作答，可眼下连分钟都是宝贵的。

「因为我住那儿。」——这个回答通过了。

他又为不得不提这些问题而道歉，可见他必是新手。别的人才不管你乐意不乐意。我签了六七张保证书，交出我那笔借来的财产的大头，然后揣着船票再次出发，奔雅克·约翰逊街（Rue Jacques Johnson，拳王的名字法文化）去。我到得正是时候，因为人家星期六提早关门。别的日子，这帮可怜虫得从上午十点整干到下午四点，中间不带歇的。

===Original===
The officer also wanted to know why I was going to America. And he asked me at what hotel I would stop in London. I told him I’d never been there and knew nothing about the hotels.

“You must make a choice,” he said. “We have to know your address.”

“Is there one called the Savoy?”

“Yes.”

“Well, let’s say the Savoy.”

“All right. You’re to stay there, then, while you’re in London, and you’re to leave England on this ship Wednesday night. Otherwise you may have trouble.”

I’ll be surprised if I don’t anyhow.

He decorated my passport with a heliotrope inscription, naming the port from which I’m to depart from France, the hotel in London, and my good ship, and sent me into the next room, where a vice-consul confirmed the military visé and relieved me of two francs.

The train leaves at seven tomorrow morning, and between now and then I have only to pack and to settle with the hotel. The former chore will be easy, for I possess just half as much personal property as when I came. Parisian laundries have commandeered the rest.

===Chinese===
军官也想知道我为什么要回美国。他还问我在伦敦打算住哪家饭店。我说我从没去过伦敦，对那儿的饭店一无所知。

「你必须选一家，」他说，「我们得知道你的地址。」

「有没有一家叫萨伏伊的？」

「有。」

「那好，就说萨伏伊吧。」

「好。那么你在伦敦期间就住那儿，星期三夜里乘这班船离开英国。不然你可能有麻烦。」

要是我反倒没遇上麻烦，那才叫奇怪呢。

他用一行藕荷色的字装点了我的护照，写明我从法国哪个港口启程、在伦敦住哪家饭店、搭哪条好船，然后打发我去隔壁屋，一位副领事确认了军人签证，顺手收了我两个法郎。

火车明早七点开，从现在到那时，我只需收拾行李、跟饭店结账。前一件活计不难，因为我现有的个人财产恰好是来时的一半。其余的都叫巴黎的洗衣店征用了。

===Original===
Monday, September 17. London.

With tear-dimmed eyes, I said farewell to Paris yesterday morning at the unearthly hour of seven. There was not even a gendarme on hand to see me off.

The trip from Paris to England is arranged with the customary French passion for convenience. They get you out of bed at five to catch the train, which arrives in the port at noon. The Channel boat leaves port at ten o’clock at night, giving you ten solid hours in which to think. Not ten either, for the last two are consumed in waiting for your turn to be examined by the customs and viséed by the Authorities du Exit.

Customs examination in this case is a pure waste of time. The gentleman only wants to know whether you are trying to smuggle any gold money out of France. I’d like to see the departing guest who has any kind of money left to smuggle.

The Authorities du Exit are seven in number. They sit round a table, and you pass from one to the other until something has been done to you by each. One feels your pulse, another looks at your tongue, a third reads your passport right side up, a fourth reads it upside down, a fifth compares you with your photograph, a sixth inspects your visés for physical defects, and the seventh tries to throw a scare into you.

I got by the first six easily. No. 7 read both sides of the passport and then asked by whom I was employed. I told him.

“Where are your credentials?” he demanded.

“What do you mean, credentials?”

“You must have a letter from the magazine, showing that it employs you.”

“You’re mistaken. I have no such letter.”

He looked very cross. But there were others left to scare, so he couldn’t waste much time on me.

“I’ll pass you,” he said, “but if you come back to France again, you can’t leave.”

He and I should both worry.

===Chinese===
9 月 17 日，星期一。伦敦。

昨天早晨七点这个不像话的钟点，我泪眼朦胧地告别了巴黎。到场送行的连一个宪兵都没有。

从巴黎到英格兰的旅程，安排得处处体现着法国人一如既往对「便利」的热爱。他们早晨五点把你从床上薅起来赶火车，火车正午抵达港口。渡海船晚上十点才离港，给你整整十个钟头用来思考。其实连十个也没有，因为最后两个钟头要用来排队等海关检查，再由「出境当局」（Authorities du Exit，法语英语杂交）给你签发签证。

此情形下的海关检查纯属浪费时间。那位先生只想知道你是不是企图把金币走私出境。我倒想见识见识，哪位离境的客人身上还剩什么钱可走私。

「出境当局」共计七位。他们围桌而坐，你从一位跟前挪到另一位跟前，直到每人都对你做完点什么。一位给你把脉，一位看你的舌头，一位正着读你的护照，一位倒着读你的护照，一位拿你和你的照片比对，一位检查你的签证有无生理缺陷，第七位负责吓唬你。

前六位我轻松过关。第 7 号把护照正反两面都读了，然后问我的雇主是何人。我告诉了他。

「你的证明文件呢？」他质问。

「证明文件指什么？」

「你必须有一封杂志社的信，证明它雇用你。」

「您弄错了。我没有这样的信。」

他显得非常恼火。不过还有别人可吓，犯不上在我身上多费工夫。

「放你过，」他说，「可你要是再回法国，就别想走了。」

咱俩谁犯愁谁是小狗。

===Original===
But it does seem pathetic that the written and stamped approval, in all colors of the rainbow, of the Paris chief of police, the American consul, the British Military Control, the British consul, the French consul in New York, and nearly everybody else in the world, including our own Secretary of State, sufficeth not to convince a minor-league official that an innocent native of Niles, Michigan, isn’t related by marriage to the Hohenzollerns.

On the dark deck of our Channel boat I had a ’strawnary experience. A British colonel to whom I had not been introduced spoke to me. He wanted a light from my cigarette. And when I had given it to him he didn’t move away, but stayed right there and kept on talking.

“This is my first leave,” he said (but in his own tongue), “since last March. Last year we were let off ten days every three months. Now we get twenty days a year.”

“In 1918,” said I, for something to say, “you’ll probably have no vacation at all.”

“In 1918,” he replied confidently, “I believe we’ll get three hundred and sixty-five days.”

We settled the war in about half an hour. Then he asked me to join him in a Scotch and soda. I was too gentlemanly to refuse. The bar, we ascertained, was closed. But we might find something in the dining-room. We did, but to make it legal we had to order biscuits, alias crackers, with the beverage. We didn’t have to eat them, though. They looked to be in their dotage, like the permanent sandwiches which serve a similar purpose in certain blue-law cities of Les Etats Unis.

We settled the war all over again, and retired, the colonel politely expressing the hope that we would meet for breakfast.

The hope was not realized. I was through and out on deck by the time we docked at the British port, which was about six o’clock this morning.

===Chinese===
可是，巴黎警察总监、美国领事、英国军事管制处、英国领事、纽约的法国领事，外加世上几乎所有人——包括咱们国务卿——的书面盖章批准，彩虹七色俱全，竟不足以让一位小联赛官员相信：密歇根州奈尔斯的一位清白土著，跟霍亨索伦家族没有姻亲关系。这确实叫人伤感。

在我们渡海船漆黑的甲板上，我有了一段「非同寻常」（’strawnary，伦敦腔把 extraordinary 的开头吞掉了）的经历。一位没人给我介绍过的英国上校跟我搭话。他想借我的烟点个火。我给他点了之后，他并没走开，就原地站住，说个没完。

「这是我自去年三月以来的头一回休假，」他说（不过用的是他本国话），「去年我们每三个月放十天假。如今一年总共二十天。」

「到了 1918 年，」我为了找话说，「你们恐怕要一天假都没有了。」

「到了 1918 年，」他信心十足地答道，「我相信我们能放三百六十五天。」

我们花了约莫半个钟头就把战争解决了。然后他邀我同喝一杯威士忌加苏打。我绅士风度过剩，不便拒绝。据查明，酒吧关门了。但餐车里兴许能找到点什么。果然找到了，不过为了合法起见，我们得随酒点些饼干（biscuits，亦即 crackers）。好在不必吃。那些饼干看着已届垂暮之年，就像美国（Les Etats Unis，法语）某些蓝法城市里起同类作用的常年三明治。

我们把战争又从头解决了一遍，然后各自就寝，上校彬彬有礼地表示，希望明早共进早餐。

这一希望落了空。船靠上英国港口时——也就是今晨六点上下——我已办完手续，人在甲板上了。

===Original===
No one was permitted to leave the ship till the customs officials and alien officers reported for duty, two hours later. Then we were unloaded and herded into a waiting-room, where an usher seated us. Another usher picked us out, four at a time, for examination, using a system of arbitrary selective draft. Mine was a mixed quartet, three gents and a female.

An officer looked at our passports and recorded details of them in a large book. Another officer ran the gamut of queries. And here I got into a little mess by telling the truth. When he asked me what countries I had visited, I told him France and added “Oh, yes, and for one day Belgium.” He marked this fact on a slip of paper and sent me to the next room. The slip of paper was there ahead of me and I was once more a suspect.

The young lady of our quartet, a French girl, was getting hers, and there was nothing for me to do but listen. She had a letter from her mother to a friend in England. The mother, it seems, had expected to come along, but had decided to wait three weeks, “till the submarine warfare is over.” The officers were very curious to know where the mother had picked up that interesting dope. The young lady couldn’t tell them. Well, she would not be permitted to leave town till an investigation had been made. She was led back into the waiting-room and may be there yet for all I can say.

It was my turn.

“Are you an American?”

“Yes, sir.”

“How long ago were you in Belgium?”

“About ten days ago.”

“You told our officer outside that you had been in Paris five weeks.”

“I told him Paris had been my headquarters and I’d made frequent trips in and out.”

“How did you get to Belgium?”

“In an automobile.”

“An automobile!”

“Yes, sir.”

“What were you doing?”

“I was being the guest of your army.”

A great light dawned upon them.

“Oh!” said one, smiling. “He means he was behind our lines, not theirs.”

“I should hope so,” said I.

“We’re sorry to have misunderstood, sir,” said the other, and I was escorted into the baggage-room. There my sordid belongings were perfunctorily examined, the official not even troubling to open my typewriter case nor a large ungainly package containing a toy for certain parties back home.

It was eleven o’clock when the examinations were all over and we entrained for this town. I got off at Waterloo and asked a taxi to take me to the Savoy. It did and it drove on the left side of all the streets en route. I’m still quaking.

===Chinese===
海关官员和外侨官员两小时后上岗之前，谁也不许下船。然后我们被卸下船，赶进一间候检室，一名引座员安排我们就座。另一名引座员用一套任意甄选征召制，每次拣出我们四个去受检。我这组是个混合四重奏：三位先生，一位女士。

一位军官查看我们的护照，把详情录进一本大册子。另一位军官把问题从头到尾问了个遍。就在这儿，我因为说实话惹出了点小麻烦。他问我到过哪些国家，我说法国，又补了一句「哦对，还有比利时，待了一天」。他把这一情况记在一张纸条上，打发我去隔壁屋。纸条比我先到，我再度沦为嫌疑犯。

我们四重奏里的年轻女士，一位法国姑娘，正在接受她那份盘问，我别无他事，只好旁听。她带着一封母亲写给英国一位朋友的信。母亲本来似乎要同来，后来决定等上三个星期，「等潜艇战结束」。军官们非常好奇，想知道母亲大人从哪儿听来的这则有意思的内幕。姑娘说不上来。好嘛，调查清楚之前不许她离城。她被领回候检室，据我所知，没准儿到现在还坐在那儿。

轮到我了。

「你是美国人吗？」

「是，长官。」

「你多久前到的比利时？」

「大约十天前。」

「你跟外面的军官说，你在巴黎待了五个星期。」

「我跟他说的是，巴黎是我的大本营，我频繁进出。」

「你怎么去的比利时？」

「坐汽车。」

「汽车！」

「是，长官。」

「去干什么？」

「去给你们军队当客人。」

他们心头豁然亮堂。

「哦！」一位笑着说，「他的意思是，他在我们防线的后面，不是在敌人那边。」

「但愿如此。」我说。

「很抱歉误会了，先生。」另一位说。随后我被押送进行李房。我在那儿的不体面家当被草草检查了一遍，那位官员连打开我的打字机箱都嫌麻烦，还有一个大而笨的包裹——里面是给国内某些人士带的玩具——也没打开。

全部检查结束时已是十一点，我们登上来本城的火车。我在滑铁卢车站下车，叫了辆出租车去萨伏伊饭店。车是真把我送到了，只是一路上每条街都靠左边开。我至今还在打颤。

===Original===
Tuesday, September 18. London.

This morning I had my first experience with an English telephone. I asked the hotel’s operator to get me the office of Mr. O’Flaherty, the American correspondent I had met at the British front. In a few moments she rang back.

“Are you there?” she said, that being London for “Hello.”

“Here’s your number, then. Carry on,” she said.

But carrying on was not so easy. There is a steel spring on the combination transmitter-receiver which you must hold down while you talk. I kept forgetting it. Also I kept being electrically shocked. But in the course of half an hour, with the operator’s assistance, I managed to convey to the gentleman an invitation to call.

He came, and we started for the Bow Street police station, where every visitor has to register within twenty-four hours of his arrival. On the way we met Lew Payne, the actor, and Gene Corri, racing man and box-fight referee. Gene has friends among the bobbies, and I was put through in record time. They told me I’d have to go to the American consul for a visé and then come back for a second registration with the police. Mr. O’Flaherty opined that these jobs should be attended to at once, as my boat train was supposed to leave at nine tomorrow morning. Mr. Payne had a better idea.

“Let’s telephone the steamship office,” he said, “and find out whether your ship is really going to sail on schedule. They usually don’t these days.”

Mr. O’Flaherty did the telephoning, and, sure enough, the blamed thing’s been postponed till Saturday night.

They asked me what I wanted to do next, and I said I’d like to pay my respects to George and Mary. But I hadn’t let them know I was coming and they’re both out of town.

We went to Murray’s (pronounced Mowrey’s) Club for lunch, though no one in the party was a member and you have to sign checks to get anything. Unlike most clubs, however, you pay cash simultaneously with signing the check, so we weren’t cheating. I signed “Charles Chaplin” to one check and it went unchallenged.

Gene’s two sons are in the British army, and the conversation was confined to them. I was told they were the best two sons a man ever had, but I knew better.

Murray’s Club’s orchestra is jazz and it gave Mr. O’Flaherty and me an acute attack of homesickness.

From there we rode to the National Sporting Club, of which Mr. Corri is king. He asked me to put on the gloves with him, but I’m not one of the kind that picks on people five or six times my age.

On Mr. Payne’s advice, Mr. O’Flaherty and I purchased seats for a show called *Seven Day’s Leave*, and that’s where we’ve been tonight, we and another scribe, Mr. Miller of Dowagiac, Michigan, which, as everyone knows, is a suburb of Niles.

===Chinese===
9 月 18 日，星期二。伦敦。

今天早晨我头一回体验了英国的电话。我请饭店的话务员接奥弗莱厄蒂（O’Flaherty）先生的办公处——就是我在英国前线结识的那位美国记者。不多时她回了铃。

「你在这儿吗？」（Are you there?）她说——这是伦敦话的「喂」。

「那这是你的号了。接着讲吧。」她说。

可接着讲没那么容易。那种收发合一的话机上有一根钢簧，讲话时必须按住。我一再忘记。我还一再被电到。不过半个钟头下来，靠话务员从旁协助，我总算向那位先生传达了登门拜访之邀。

他来了，我们出发去弓街警察局——每位来客抵达后二十四小时内都得到那儿登记。路上我们遇见了演员卢·佩恩（Lew Payne）和吉恩·科里（Gene Corri，赛马圈人士兼拳击裁判）。吉恩在英国警察（bobbies）里有朋友，我以创纪录的速度办完了手续。他们告诉我，我还得去美国领事馆签个证，再回来在警察局做第二次登记。奥弗莱厄蒂先生认为这些事必须马上办，因为接我船的专列明早九点就该发车了。佩恩先生有个更好的主意。

「咱们给轮船公司打个电话，」他说，「问问你的船到底是不是真会准点开。这些天他们通常都不准点。」

奥弗莱厄蒂先生去打的电话，果不其然，那该死的东西推迟到星期六夜里了。

他们问我下一步想干什么，我说我想去给乔治和玛丽（即英王乔治五世与玛丽王后）请个安。可惜我没事先通知他们我要来，而他们俩都出城了。

我们去默里俱乐部（Murray’s，英国人念作「莫里」）吃的午饭，虽然一行人没有一个是会员，而且要任何东西都得签单。不过跟多数俱乐部不同，这里签单的同时就得付现钱，所以我们不算作弊。有一张单我签的是「查理·卓别林」（Charles Chaplin），居然无人异议。

吉恩的两个儿子都在英国军队里，话题便全绕着他们转。我被告知他们是天底下男人所能有的最好的两个儿子，不过我心里有数。

默里俱乐部的乐队奏的是爵士乐，听得奥弗莱厄蒂先生和我急性思乡病发作。

随后我们驱车去全国体育俱乐部，科里先生在那儿称王。他邀我戴上手套跟他过招，可我不是那种专挑年纪比我大五六倍的人欺负的角色。

遵佩恩先生的建议，奥弗莱厄蒂先生和我买了两张票，看一出名叫《七日假期》（*Seven Days’ Leave*）的戏。今晚我们就是打那儿回来的——同行的还有另一位爬格子的，密歇根州道瓦吉亚克的米勒先生——众所周知，道瓦吉亚克是奈尔斯的郊区。

===Original===
The show is a melodrama with so many plots that the author forgot to unravel two or three hundred of them. Of the fifteen characters, one is the hero and the rest are German spies, male and female. The hero is a British officer. Everybody wanted to kill him, and so far as I could see there was nothing to prevent. But he was still alive when the final curtain fell. The actors made all their speeches directly to the audience, and many of them (the speeches) were in the soliloquy form ruled off the American stage several years ago.

In the last act the hero pretends to be blotto (British for spiflicated), so that, while he is apparently dead to the world, he can eavesdrop on a dialogue between two of the boche plotters and obtain information invaluable to England. The boches were completely deceived, which is more than can be said of the audience.

Wednesday, September 19. London.

Took a walk past Westminster Abbey and Buckingham Palace and found they looked just like their postcard pictures.

It’s almost as bad crossing streets here as in Paris. The taxis don’t go as fast, but their habit of sticking to the left side keeps an American on what are known as tenterhooks.

Mr. O’Flaherty loomed up at noon and guided me to the office of a friend with money. This rara avis honored a check on an American bank, and now I think there’s enough cash on hand to see me through. The only trouble is that my education in English money has been neglected and I don’t know when I’m being short-changed. Constantly, I presume.

Living conditions here have it on those in Paris. There are no meatless days, and a hot bath is always available. The town is dark at night, but it’s said to be not for the purpose of saving fuel, but as a measure of protection against air raids.

One of those things was staged last week and a bomb fell uncomfortably close to ye hotel. The dent it made in Mother Earth is clearly visible to the naked eye. I trust the bombers take every other week off. At dinner we met two American naval officers—a captain from Baltimore and a lieutenant from Rockford, which is in Illinois. What they told us was the most interesting stuff I’ve heard yet. But, like all interesting stuff, it’s forbidden to write it.

===Chinese===
这出戏是情节剧，情节线多得作者忘了解开其中两三百条。十五个角色里，一个是男主角，其余全是德国间谍，男女都有。男主角是位英国军官。人人都想杀他，而据我所见，没有什么能拦着。可大幕落下时他还活着。演员们的台词一律直接冲观众说，其中不少（台词）用的还是几年前就被美国舞台取缔的独白形式。

最后一幕里，男主角假装烂醉（blotto，英国话，相当于 spiflicated），这样，他表面人事不省，却可以偷听两个德国佬阴谋分子之间的对话，搞到对英格兰无比珍贵的情报。德国佬们被彻底骗过了——观众们就不敢这么说了。

9 月 19 日，星期三。伦敦。

散步经过了威斯敏斯特教堂和白金汉宫，发现它们长得跟明信片上的画一模一样。

在这儿过马路，几乎跟在巴黎一样要命。出租车没有跑得那么快，可它们死抱左边行驶的习惯，让美国人时刻处于所谓如坐针毡的状态。

正午时分奥弗莱厄蒂先生冒了出来，领我去了一位有钱的朋友的办公处。这位珍禽（rara avis，拉丁语「罕见之鸟」）兑了一张美国银行的支票，我想如今手头的现金够撑到底了。唯一的麻烦是，我在英国货币方面的教育一直荒疏，分不清什么时候被少找了钱。估计是回回都少找。

此地的生活条件胜过巴黎。没有无肉日，热水澡随时可洗。城里夜里一片黑，但据说不是为了省燃料，而是防空袭的措施。

上星期就上演了一场，一颗炸弹落在离敝饭店近得让人不舒服的地方。它在大地母亲身上砸出的凹坑，肉眼清晰可见。我祈盼轰炸机们隔周休息。晚餐时我们遇见两位美国海军军官——一位上校来自巴尔的摩，一位中尉来自罗克福德，在伊利诺伊州。他们讲给我们听的是我迄今听过的最有意思的东西。但是，跟一切有意思的东西一样，禁止写出来。

===Original===
Thursday, September 20. London.

The American naval officers took me to luncheon. After luncheon I went to the American consul’s where I was viséed. Thence to the Bow Street station for final registration.

This evening to *The Boy*, a musical play which could use some of the plot so prodigally expended in *Seven Days’ Leave*. But the music isn’t bad.

Friday, September 21. London.

The naval officers and three of us holdup men had a bitter argument over the respective merits of Baltimore, Dowagiac, Rockford, Niles, and What Cheer, Iowa, of which Mr. O’Flaherty is a native, and, so far as I know, the only one. It was finally voted to award What Cheer first prize for beauty of name, Dowagiac for handsome young men, Niles for scenic grandeur, Rockford for social gaieties, and Baltimore for tunnels.

I wanted to do some work, but the rest of the crowd seemed to think my room was open house for the balance of the day, and here they stuck despite all efforts to oust them.

Tonight it was *Chu Chin Chow* at His Majesty’s Theater. You have to keep going to theaters in London. They’re the only places that are lit up.

*Chu Chin Chow* is a musical comedy based on *The Forty Thieves*, and the music, according to our unanimous opinion, is the best since *The Merry Widow*. I seem to have resigned as war correspondent to accept a position as dramatic critic. But, as Mr. O’Flaherty says, there’s nothing to write about the war, and what you do write the censors massacre.

Our ship still thinks it’s going to sail tomorrow night, and the train leaves at nine-thirty in the morning. I am to be convoyed to port by the captain and the lieutenant, whose holiday is over.

===Chinese===
9 月 20 日，星期四。伦敦。

美国海军军官们带我吃了午饭。饭后我去美国领事馆签了证。接着去弓街警察局做了最终登记。

今晚看的是《男孩》（*The Boy*），一出音乐剧——它很能用得上些《七日假期》里那样大手笔挥霍掉的情节。不过音乐不赖。

9 月 21 日，星期五。伦敦。

海军军官们和我们三个「劫道的」展开了一场激烈的论战，比的是巴尔的摩、道瓦吉亚克、罗克福德、奈尔斯，以及艾奥瓦州瓦特彻（What Cheer，镇名意为「好哇」）各自的长处——奥弗莱厄蒂先生就是瓦特彻人，而且据我所知是独一份。最后投票表决：瓦特彻获镇名优美头奖，道瓦吉亚克俊美青年奖，奈尔斯风景壮丽奖，罗克福德社交欢乐奖，巴尔的摩隧道奖。

我本想干点活儿，可那帮人似乎把我的房间当成了当日余下时间的免费会所，怎么撵都撵不走，一直赖在这儿。

今晚看的是国王剧院的《朱清周》（*Chu Chin Chow*）。在伦敦你只好不停地跑剧院。全城就那儿是亮着灯的。

《朱清周》是根据《四十大盗》改编的音乐喜剧，按我们一致的意见，音乐是《风流寡妇》（*The Merry Widow*）以来最好的。我似乎已经辞去战地记者之职，改任剧评人了。不过，正如奥弗莱厄蒂先生所说，战争没什么可写的，你写出来的那点东西，又都叫审查官们屠戮殆尽。

我们的船仍然认为明晚就开，火车明早九点半发。我将由那位上校和中尉护送去港口，他们二位的假期到头了。

===Original===
Saturday, September 22. In Bond.

We’re anchored in the middle of the river and have no apparent intention of moving tonight. And everybody’s out of cigarettes, and it’s illegal to sell them while we’re in bond, whatever that may mean. But I guess I’d rather be in it than in a spy’s cell, which seemed to be my destination at one time today.

The United States naval gentlemen were down at the train early and commandeered the best compartment on it. They had saved a seat for me and an extra one on general principles. This was awarded to Mr. Hanson, one of the active members of the French Line conspiracy which caused my arrest in Bordeaux. I hope he’s seasick all the way home.

On the trip up from London we scored a decisive verbal victory over the submarines and formulated the terms of peace. Captain Baltimore and Lieutenant Rockford said farewell at the Liverpool dock and started for wherever they were going. We found seats in the inspection room and waited. Mr. Hanson grew impatient at length. He flashed his passport, a diplomatic one, on the usher and was sent through in a hurry. Not so with this well-known suspect. I was among the last to be called. My passport, strangely enough, was approved, but the baggage examination was yet to come.

I found my four pieces—two containers of clothes and such, a typewriter, and the ungainly toy—and had them hoisted on to the inspection counter. The most curious man I ever knew went at them.

The typewriter came first.

“What is this?” he asked when he had opened the case.

“A typewriter.”

“Where did you buy it?”

“In Chicago.”

“What do you use it for?”

“For typewriting.”

“Typewriting what?”

“Stuff for newspapers and magazines.”

“Pretty handy, isn’t it?”

“Very.”

“Have you written any articles over here?”

“Yes.”

“Where are they?”

“Some are in America by this time; others are in the censors’ hands.”

===Chinese===
9 月 22 日，星期六。船在「保税」中（In Bond）。

我们抛锚停在河当间儿，今晚显然不打算动窝。而且人人都断了烟，而在「保税」状态中卖烟是违法的——管它是什么意思呢。不过我想，比起间谍牢房来，我还是宁可保税，今天有一阵子我似乎正要被送进那里面。

美国的海军先生们早早到了火车站，征用了车上最好的包厢。他们为我留了一个座，又照惯例多留了一个。这个座位奖给了汉森先生——策划了我在波尔多被捕的那场法国航线阴谋的活跃分子之一。祝他一路晕船晕到家。

从伦敦北上的旅途中，我们对潜艇取得了一场决定性的口头大捷，并拟定了和约条款。巴尔的摩上校和罗克福德中尉在利物浦码头道了别，奔他们要去的去处去了。我们在检查厅里找座坐下，干等。汉森先生终于不耐烦了。他把护照——一本外交护照——朝引座员一亮，便被火速放了进去。这位著名嫌疑犯就没这份待遇了。我属于最后被叫到的那批。说来奇怪，我的护照居然获准通过，可行李检查还在后头。

我找到我那四件行李——两箱衣服杂物、一台打字机，还有那件笨拙的玩具——叫人搬上了检查台。一位我这辈子见识过的最好奇的人扑了上去。

先查打字机。

「这是什么？」他打开箱子后问。

「打字机。」

「在哪儿买的？」

「芝加哥。」

「你拿它干什么用？」

「打字。」

「打什么字？」

「给报纸和杂志写的稿子。」

「挺方便的，是吧？」

「非常方便。」

「你在这儿写过什么文章吗？」

「写过。」

「在哪儿呢？」

「这会儿有些已经在美国了；其他的在审查官手里。」

===Original===
He wanted to know what publications I was connected with, and I told him. He allowed me to close up the typewriter case, and next launched an offensive against a young trunk. He examined my collars one by one and found them all the same size. He came upon a package containing five or six hundred sheets of blank copy paper. He inspected every sheet, holding many of them up to the light. He gave individual attention to each of the few bits of lingerie the Parisians had not considered worth keeping. He exhibited an amazing interest in my other suit. He fondled a beautiful gray sweater for fully five minutes. He went through a copy of the *Chu Chin Chow* score, page by page. I wondered he didn’t sing it. Holding out only the blank paper, he repacked, and tackled the suitcase.

He counted the bristles in the toothbrush. He found two French dictionaries and a French grammar and studied them for approximately one semester. He opened a nest of shirts and handkerchiefs and spread them out for a thorough review. I should hate to be a clerk in a gents’ furnishing store and have him wished on me as a customer.

In the lower southeast corner he discovered an unopened box of shaving cream. As everyone knows, this commodity comes in a tube, which is wrapped in transparent paper, and the tube, thus wrapped, is contained in a pasteboard box for protection or something. Old Curiosity opened the box and extracted the tube. He gazed at it through the wrapper, then removed the wrapper and stared at the nude tube.

“Where is this made?” he asked.

“In America. It comes out like a ribbon and lies flat on the brush.”

Without comment, he reclothed the tube as well as he could in its mutilated wrapper, put it back in its box, and repacked the suitcase and shut it.

“Is that all you have?” he inquired.

“No,” I said. “There’s that big square package containing a toy.”

===Chinese===
他想知道我跟哪些出版物有瓜葛，我告诉了他。他准许我把打字机箱合上，随即向一只年轻的小皮箱发动了攻势。他把我的领子一片一片验过，发现尺寸全然一致。他翻出一包五百来张的空白稿纸。他逐张检查，其中不少还举起来对着光照。巴黎人不屑留下的那几件小小内衣，他一一单独垂询。他对我的另一套西装表现出惊人的兴趣。他抚摩一件漂亮的灰色毛衫，足足抚了五分钟。他把一册《朱清周》曲谱逐页翻阅。我很诧异他竟没唱出来。只扣下那沓白纸，他把皮箱重新装好，转向手提箱。

他数了牙刷上的毛。他发现两本法语词典和一本法语语法书，研修了大约一个学期。他打开一窝衬衫和手帕，摊开来彻底复盘。要我是在男装杂货铺当伙计，这么位客人砸到头上，我可消受不起。

在东南角下头，他发现了一盒未开封的剃须膏。众所周知，此物装在软管里，软管裹着透明纸，裹好的软管再装进纸板盒里，以求保护还是什么的。「老好奇」打开纸盒，抽出软管。他隔着包装纸凝视良久，继而剥去包装纸，盯着那赤裸的管子。

「这东西哪儿造的？」他问。

「美国。挤出来像根带子，平摊在刷子上。」

他未置一词，尽力给软管重新穿好那件被扯坏的包装衣，放回盒里，把手提箱重新装好、合上。

「你就这些东西？」他问。

「不止，」我说，「还有那个大方包裹，里头装着个玩具。」

===Original===
Now about this toy. It’s a complete but ridiculously impractical system of trenches. French soldiers of leaden composition are resisting a boche attack. Some are supposed to be throwing bombs. Others are fighting with bayonets. A few are busy with the trench guns. There are threads to represent barbed-wire entanglements and a few Huns enmeshed in them. Other Huns are prone, the victims of the sturdy poilu defense.

The package had been opened for private exhibition purposes in London, and as I am an awful washout (British slang) at doing up bundles, I had left the job to a chambermaid, who had discarded the Parisian wrapping paper and used some on which no firm name appeared.

Well, Mr. Question Mark now laboriously untied the cord, took off the paper and the cover of the box, and exposed the toy to the public and official view. Instantly two British officers, whom we shall call General Bone and Major Thick, flitted up to the counter and peered at the damning evidence.

“What is this gentleman’s name?” asked the general.

He was told.

“When did you make this thing?” he demanded.

“I didn’t,” said I. “It was bought in a shop in Paris.”

“What shop?”

“You can’t expect a person to remember the name of a Parisian shop.”

“Where is the firm’s name on the paper?”

I explained that the original wrapper had been left in London.

“What is your business?” demanded the major.

“He’s a correspondent,” replied the inspector.

There ensued the old familiar cross-examination and the request for credentials I didn’t have. The major asked the inspector whether I was carrying any papers.

“These,” said the latter, and showed him the pile of blank copy sheets.

The major dived for it.

“It’s all blank paper,” said the inspector, and the major registered keen disappointment.

===Chinese===
现在说说这个玩具。它是一套完整却荒唐得不切实用的战壕体系。铅铸的法国士兵正在抵抗德国佬的进攻。有的摆出扔炸弹的姿势。有的在拼刺刀。还有几个忙着操作战壕炮。另有几根线代表铁丝网障碍，网里缠着几个匈奴兵。别的匈奴兵则一概趴倒，是法国大兵（poilu）坚固防御的牺牲品。

这包裹在伦敦时曾为私人展览之便被打开过，而我捆包裹的本事蹩脚透顶（washout，英国俚语，意即「废物」），就把这活儿交给了旅馆女仆，她把巴黎的包装纸扔了，换了些没有字号的纸包上。

于是，问号先生费力地解开业绳，揭下包装纸和盒盖，让玩具暴露于公众与官方的视野。霎时间两位英国军官——咱们姑且称之为「骨头」将军和「榆木」少校（Bone 与 Thick 皆英俚「蠢材」之意）——翩然扑到检查台前，细审这份罪证。

「这位先生尊姓大名？」将军问。

有人告诉了他。

「你什么时候做的这个东西？」他质问。

「不是我做的，」我说，「是巴黎一家铺子里买的。」

「哪家铺子？」

「总不能指望一个人记住巴黎铺子的字号吧。」

「纸上哪儿有字号？」

我解释说，原来的包装纸落在伦敦了。

「你是干什么的？」少校质问。

「他是记者。」检查员答道。

接下来便是那套熟门熟路的盘问，外加索要我的没有的证明文件。少校问检查员我身上带没带文件。

「这些。」后者说着，把那沓空白稿纸呈了上去。

少校扑将过去。

「全是白纸。」检查员说。少校把强烈的失望记录在了脸上。

===Original===
Next to my suitcase lay a bag belonging to a gentleman named Trotter, and on it was a Japanese hotel label. The general glimpsed it and turned on me. “When were you in Japan?” he asked.

I told him never.

“That piece isn’t his,” said the inspector. “It belongs to a Mr. Trotter.”

“His first name is Globe,” said I, but it was a wild pitch.

The major and the general had a whispered consultation. Then the former said: “Well, I guess he’s all right. Let him go.”

Some devil within me suggested that I say goodbye to them in German, which I learned in our high school. I cast him out, and here I am, aboard ship, sitting still in the middle of the river. But I don’t like being indefinitely bottled in bond and I appeal to you, Mr. Captain—

> Take me somewhere west of Ireland where they know I’m not a spy,
> Where nobody gazes at me with a cold, suspicious eye—
> To the good old U.S.A.,
> Where a gent can go his way
> With no fear of being picked on forty thousand times a day.

===Chinese===
我的手提箱旁边躺着一只提包，主人是位姓特罗特（Trotter）的先生，包上贴着一张日本饭店的标签。将军瞥见了，猛地转向我。「你什么时候去的日本？」他问。

我告诉他，从来没去过。

「那件不是他的，」检查员说，「是一位特罗特先生的。」

「他的名儿叫环球（Globe），」我说——环球·特罗特（Globe Trotter，意即「环球旅行家」）——可惜这一球投飞了。

少校和将军咬了一阵耳朵。然后前者开口：「呃，我看他没毛病。放他走吧。」

我肚子里有个小魔鬼撺掇我，用咱们高中学的德语向他们道别。我把他驱逐了出去，然后我就到了这儿——人在船上，一动不动坐在河当间儿。可我不喜欢被无限期地封在保税瓶子里，所以我向您呼吁，船长先生——

> 带我去爱尔兰以西的什么地方吧，那儿的人知道我不是间谍，
> 那儿没有人拿又冷又疑的眼神把我打量——
> 去美好的老美国吧，
> 在那儿一位体面人可以走自己的路，
> 不怕一天被人盘查四万次。

## VIII Back in Old “O Say”; I Start Answering Questions / 八 回到老「哦，说」；我开始回答问题

===Original===
Sunday, September 23. At Sea.

A card on the wall of my stateroom says: “Name of Steward﻿—Ring Once. Name of Stewardess﻿—Ring Twice.” If they’ll give us deck space, we can put on a three Ring circus.

The ship was still in bond when we awoke this morning, and the cheerful rumor floated round that she sometimes remained in harbor a week before securing the Admiralty’s permission to sail. But lifeboat drill was ordered right after breakfast, and Ring Once told me this indicated a speedy departure. My boat is No. 9. It’s a male boat except for one Japanese lady, Mrs. Kajiro Come-here-o, whose husband is also of our select crew.

Our drillmaster advised us to wear plenty of heavy clothes till we were out of the danger zone, advice which it is impossible for me to follow. He said five blasts of the whistle would mean we were attacked. I think, however, that if I hear as many as three I’ll start sauntering toward No. 9.

At noon we felt the throb of the engines, and forty minutes later we were out of bond and able to buy cigarettes.

===Chinese===
9 月 23 日，星期日。海上。

我特等舱的墙上贴着一张卡片：「侍者姓名——响铃一次（Ring Once）。女侍者姓名——响铃两次（Ring Twice）。」他们要是肯给咱们腾块甲板场地，咱们就能上演一出三「铃」马戏了（three Ring circus，谐仿「三环大马戏」three-ring circus，又扣侍者 Ring 之姓）。

今早醒来时，船还处在「封存待放」状态（in bond），四下飘着一条喜气洋洋的谣言：这船有时候要在港里一停一个礼拜，才等得着海军部的放行许可。可是早饭后立刻下令搞救生艇演习，「响铃一次」告诉我，这是速航在即的兆头。我分在 9 号艇。艇上清一色男性，只有一位日本女士例外——梶路·「到这儿来哟」夫人（Mrs. Kajiro Come-here-o，姓氏被作者谐写成「到这儿来哟」）——她丈夫也在这支精挑细选的队伍里。

演习教官劝我们在驶出危险地带之前多穿厚衣裳，这条忠告我是没法照办的。他说，汽笛响五声，就表示咱们遭了袭击。不过依我看，只要听到第三声，我就会开始朝 9 号艇溜达过去。

正午时分，我们感到了引擎的震颤，四十分钟后，船解除了封存，能买香烟了。

===Original===
Before luncheon we were assigned to our permanent seats. Naturally, I am at the captain’s table, with a member of the House of Commons, a member of the House of Lords, a plain English gentleman, a retiring attaché of our embassy in London, his journalistic wife, and M. de M. Hanson of Washington and Peoria, his first name being Mal de Mer.

The talk today has been of nothing but submarines. The superstitious call attention to the fact that with us is a lady who was on the *Lusitania* when they torpedoed it. To offset that, however, we carry the president’s youngest son-in-law, and surely there must be a limit to boche ruthlessness.

===Chinese===
午饭前，我们被指定了全程的固定座位。我自然在船长那一桌，同桌的有：一位下院议员、一位上院议员、一位不列颠平民绅士、一位我国驻伦敦使馆即将卸任的随员、他那位跑新闻的太太，还有华盛顿—皮奥里亚的 M. de M. 汉森先生（M. de M. Hanson，M. de M. 是法语「晕船先生」Monsieur de Mer 的戏称），他的教名便叫「晕船」（Mal de Mer）。

今天的谈资清一色是潜艇。迷信的人提请大家注意一个事实：船上有一位女士，当年卢西塔尼亚号（*Lusitania*）中鱼雷时她就在那船上。不过话又说回来，咱们船上还载着总统最年轻的女婿呢，德国佬（boche）的狠劲儿总该有个限度吧。

===Original===
Monday, September 24. At Sea.

Our ship’s cargo consists principally of titles, rumors and celebrities. Most of the titles belong to members of the British Commission which is coming over to talk food to Mr. Hoover. But there is also a regular baroness, round whom the young bloods swarm like bees.

The rumors deal with the course of the ship. Some folks say we are going up Iceland way; others that we are headed straight south; a few that we are taking the Kansas City route, and so on. The sun refuses to come out and tell us the truth, but there’s a shore line in sight on our starboard, and Ring Once tells me it’s the east coast of Ireland. That ought to indicate something about our general direction, but I don’t know what. Of the celebrities, most of them are American journalists and other spies.

===Chinese===
9 月 24 日，星期一。海上。

咱们这船的货物，主要由头衔、谣言和名流构成。头衔大都属于英国代表团（British Commission）的诸位成员，他们此来是要跟胡佛先生谈吃的。不过船上还有一位如假包换的男爵夫人，年轻公子哥儿围着她也跟蜜蜂似的。

谣言则一律围着船的航向打转。有人说咱们要朝冰岛那边开；有人说直奔正南；还有几位说咱们走的是堪萨斯城航线，等等。太阳死活不肯露面吐露实情，好在右舷望得见一线海岸，「响铃一次」告诉我那是爱尔兰东海岸。照理说这能说明点咱们的大方向，可我说不上来是什么。至于名流，多半是美国记者，以及其他间谍。

===Original===
Tuesday, September 25. At Sea.

Between eight and nine every morning the bath steward, one Peter James, raps on the door and says: “Your bath is ready, sir.” And you have to get up and go and take it for fear of what he’d think of you if you didn’t. But it’s pretty tough on a man who’s just spent a month in France and formed new habits.

I stayed up all night playing bridge. I wanted to be sleepy today because I needed a hair cut and the best way to take ’em is unconsciously. The scheme was effective, and I didn’t hear a word the barber said.

The three others in the bridge game were members of the British Food Commission. Britishers, I notice, are much slower at bridge than we are. They think a long while before they make a play; then they make the wrong play. I do the same thing with only half the expenditure of thought and time.

===Chinese===
9 月 25 日，星期二。海上。

每天早上八点到九点之间，浴室侍者——彼得·詹姆斯（Peter James）——就来敲门说：「您的浴水放好了，先生。」你只好起床去洗，生怕不洗会叫他看轻了你。可对一个刚在法国泡了一个月、养成一整套新习惯的人来说，这一套实在够呛。

我通宵打桥牌。我要的就是今天犯困，因为需要理发，而对付理发这档子事，最好的法子就是趁无意识状态进行。计策奏效了，理发师说了什么，我一个字也没听见。

桥牌桌上另外三位都是英国食品委员会（British Food Commission）的成员。我注意到，英国人打桥牌比我们慢得多。出一张牌之前先酝酿良久；然后出错的牌。我干的是同样的事，只消花费一半的脑子和时间。

===Original===
Wednesday, September 26. At Sea.

Captain Finch appeared at breakfast this morning. It was the first time he had honored us. His presence at table, I’m told, indicates that we are out of the danger zone.

On board we have a doctor, a D.D., who intends to lecture in America on the war. He happened to be at our table in the lounge this afternoon. Someone asked him if he had visited the front.

“Indeed, yes,” he said. “I was there less than a month ago. The British entertained me and showed me everything. Why, one day they were taking me through the front-line trenches and I asked how far we were from the German front line. ‘Hush, Doctor,’ said one of the officers. ‘The Germans can hear you talking now. They’re only twenty yards away.’”

I asked him what part of the front he’d been on. He told me. It was exactly the same front I’d seen. But when I was there﻿—and it was also less than a month ago﻿—the depth of No Man’s Land was two hundred yards, and there weren’t any noncombatants batting round within sixty feet of a boche trench. No, nor a British trench either. I said as much right out loud, and I’m afraid I’ve spoiled his trip.

But honest, Doc, somebody was kidding you or else your last name is Cook.

===Chinese===
9 月 26 日，星期三。海上。

芬奇上尉（Captain Finch）今早在早餐桌上露面了。这是他头一回赏光。听说他老人家驾临餐桌，就表示咱们已经驶出危险地带了。

船上有一位博士——神学博士（D.D.）——打算到美国去就战争问题作巡回演讲。今天下午他碰巧跟我们同在休息室的一张桌上。有人问他去没去过前线。

「去过，当然去过，」他说，「不到一个月前我还身在彼处呢。英国人殷勤招待，什么都让我看了。嗐，有一天他们领我逛前线战壕，我问德国人的一线离我们还有多远。『嘘，博士，』一位军官说，『您说话德国人这会儿就听得见了。他们才二十码远。』」

我问他去的是哪一段前线。他说了。正是我看过的一段。可我在那儿的时候——那也是不到一个月前——无人区的纵深足有二百码，而且方圆六十英尺之内，见不着任何非战斗人员在德国佬战壕跟前晃悠。不，英国战壕跟前也没有。我把这话原原本本大声说了出来，只怕我把他这趟巡讲之行给搅黄了。

不过说真的，博士，不是有人存心耍您，就是您贵姓库克（Cook）。

===Original===
Thursday, September 27. At Sea.

> The sea was calm, the day was fair.

  E’en Mal de Mer came up for air.

The voyage is getting sort of tiresome to us Americans. For the British it’s not so bad. Their five meals per day break the monotony. They breakfast from nine to ten, lunch from one to two, tea from four to five, dine from seven to eight, and sup from eleven on. But we can’t stand that pace, and have to waste a lot of time reading.

There is a ship library full of fairly good stuff, but by far the most interesting matter is to be found in a paper published on board every day. Its title is *The Ocean Times and the Atlantic Daily News*. It contains two pages of news, two pages of editorial causerie, one of them in French, and four pages of real hot stuff, such as “Softness and Grandeur. A Brief Appreciation of a Delightful Excursion in Norway”; “Chance Meetings. The Long Arm of Coincidence and the Charm of Surprise”; “The Introduction of Electric Tramways into Cape Town.” These essays and articles are boiler plate, as we journalists say, and we find them an excellent sedative.

The news is received by wireless from both sides of the ocean. Today’s dispatches from Washington fairly made our hair stand on end. One of them said: “The decision of the milk dealers here that they would not pay more than thirty-two cents per gallon for milk after October one was met by a counterproposal on the part of the Maryland and Virginia Milk Producers’ Association last night with an offer to fix the price at thirty-three and one-half cents per gallon instead of at thirty-five cents as originally planned.” Another informed us that Brigadier-General Somebody, for three years assistant to the Major-General Commandant at the Marine Corps Headquarters, had been ordered to command the Marine Cantonment at Somewhere, Virginia. A person who fails to get a thrill out of that must be a cold fish. But I can’t help wishing they’d let us know when and where the world series is to start.

It is announced that Doc Cook will preach at the ship’s service Sunday morning. His text, no doubt, will be “Twenty Yards from the German Trenches.”

===Chinese===
9 月 27 日，星期四。海上。

> 大海平静无波，天气晴和可喜。

  连「晕船」先生都浮上水面换气。

这趟航行，咱们美国人开始有点腻了。英国人倒不觉得太坏。他们一天五顿饭，正好调剂调剂：九点到十点早饭，一点到两点午餐，四点到五点下午茶，七点到八点正餐，十一点往后宵夜。这个节奏咱们受不了，只好浪费大把时间读书。

船上有间图书室，藏书还算不错，但最有趣的读物，当数船上每天出版的一份报纸，报名《海洋时报暨大西洋日报》（*The Ocean Times and the Atlantic Daily News*）。两版新闻，两版随笔闲谈的社论（其中一版是法文的），另有四版真正的热辣货色，诸如《温婉与壮丽：挪威愉快小游简赏》《不期而遇：巧合的长臂与惊喜的魅力》《有轨电车引入开普敦》。这些文章，按我们新闻行话说，叫「通用铸版稿」（boiler plate），我们觉得它们是上好的安眠药。

新闻靠无线电从大洋两岸接收。今天来自华盛顿的电讯简直叫我们头发根根倒竖。其中一条说：「本地牛奶经销商决定，十月一日以后牛奶收购价不超过每加仑三十二美分；马里兰及弗吉尼亚牛奶生产者协会昨晚提出反建议，愿把价格定在每加仑三十三美分半，而非原定的三十五美分。」另一条通知我们：某某准将（Brigadier-General Somebody），三年来一直担任海军陆战队总部总指挥官的副手，现奉命出任弗吉尼亚州某地（Somewhere, Virginia）海军陆战队训练营的指挥官。读了这样的消息还不激动的人，准是个冷血动物。可我不免巴望他们能告诉我们，世界职业棒球大赛什么时候、在哪儿开打。

有通告说，库克博士（Doc Cook）将于周日上午在船上的礼拜布道。他的讲题，毫无疑问，将是《离德国战壕二十码》。

===Original===
Saturday, September 29. At Sea.

Captain Finch says we will reach New York Tuesday. But if they don’t quit turning the clock back half an hour a day we’ll never get there.

===Chinese===
9 月 29 日，星期六。海上。

芬奇上尉说，我们星期二抵达纽约。可他们要是再这么每天把钟往回拨半小时，咱们永远也到不了。

===Original===
Sunday, September 30. At Sea.

The doctor preached, but disappointed a large congregation with a regular sermon.

After we had sung “God Save the King” and “America,” I came to my stateroom to work and immediately broke the carriage cord on my typewriter. I said one or two of the words I had just heard in church; then borrowed a screw driver from Ring Once and proceeded to dilacerate the machine. It took over an hour to get it all apart and about two hours to decide that I couldn’t begin to put it together again.

I went on deck and told my troubles to Mr. Hollister of Chicago. Mr. Hollister was sympathetic and a lifesaver. He introduced me to a young man, named after the beer that made Fort Wayne famous, who is a master mechanic in the employ of the Duke of Detroit. The young man said he had had no experience with typewriters, but it was one of his greatest delights to tinker. I gave him leave to gratify his perverted taste and, believe it or not, in forty minutes he had the thing running, with a piece of common binding twine pinch-hitting for the cord. Then I went entirely off my head and bought him wine.

===Chinese===
9 月 30 日，星期日。海上。

博士布了道，可惜拿的是一篇四平八稳的布道文，让满堂会众大失所望。

唱完《天佑吾王》和《美利坚》，我回舱干活，一上手就把打字机的滑架拉线崩断了。我把刚在教堂里听来的那些词，说了那么一两个；随后找「响铃一次」借来一把螺丝刀，动手把机器大卸八块。全部拆开用了一个多钟头，又花了大约两个钟头，才断定我压根儿没法再把它装回去。

我上了甲板，向芝加哥的霍利斯特先生（Mr. Hollister）诉苦。霍利斯特先生富有同情心，是个救苦救难的人物。他给我引见了一位年轻人——名字取自让韦恩堡（Fort Wayne）扬名的那款啤酒——受雇于「底特律公爵」（Duke of Detroit）手下的一位机械大师傅。年轻人说，打字机他从来没碰过，但摆弄机械正是他平生最大乐趣之一。我批准他满足这份变态嗜好，信不信由你，四十分钟后，那玩意儿就又能转了，一根普普通通的捆货麻绳临时代替了拉线。于是我彻底昏了头，竟请他喝了酒。

===Original===
Monday, October 1. Nearly There.

It’s midnight. An hour ago we went on deck and saw the prettiest sight in the world﻿—an American lighthouse. First we felt like choking; then like joking. Three of us﻿—Mr. and Mrs. P. Williams and I﻿—became extremely facetious.

“Well,” said Mrs. Williams, “there’s ‘ ’Tis of Thee.’”

“Yes,” said her husband, “that certainly is old ‘O Say.’”

I’ve forgotten what I said, but it was just as good.

The light﻿—standing, they told me, on Fire Island﻿—winked at us repeatedly, unaware, perhaps, that we were all married. I’ll confess we didn’t mind at all and would have winked back if we could have winked hard enough to carry nineteen nautical miles.

===Chinese===
10 月 1 日，星期一。快到了。

此刻午夜。一个钟头前我们上甲板看了世上最美的景致——一座美国灯塔。起初我们想哽咽；继而想说笑。我们仨——P. 威廉姆斯夫妇（Mr. and Mrs. P. Williams）和我——妙语大发。

「哎呀，」威廉姆斯太太说，「那边唱的可不正是《正是吾土》（'Tis of Thee）。」

「可不是嘛，」她丈夫说，「这千真万确是老『哦，说』（O Say，美国国歌第一句「哦，说，你可看见」，代指美国）。」

我说了什么已经忘了，不过想必同样精彩。

那座灯——据说立在火岛（Fire Island）上——冲我们频频眨眼，兴许不知道我们都是有妇之夫。我坦白，我们一点儿也不介意，只恨我们的眨眼功力不够火候，传不到十九海里之外，不然真想回敬它一个。

===Original===
Ring Once was waiting at the stateroom door to tell me to have all baggage packed and outside first thing in the morning.

“I’ll see that it’s taken off the ship,” he said. “You’ll find it under your initial on the dock.”

“What do you mean, under my initial?”

He explained and then noticed that my junk was unlabeled. I’d worried over this a long while. My French Line stickers had not stuck. And how would New Yorkers and Chicagoans know I’d been abroad? I couldn’t stop each one and tell him.

The trusty steward disappeared and soon returned with four beautiful labels, square, with a red border, a white star in the middle, and a dark blue L, meaning me, in the middle of the star.

“Put those on so they’ll stay,” I instructed him. “There’s no sense in crossing the ocean and then keeping it a secret.”

===Chinese===
「响铃一次」等在我舱门口，通知我把行李全部打包，明早头一件事放到门外。

「我会负责把它们弄下船，」他说，「您到码头上，找您姓氏首字母底下那一堆就是。」

「首字母底下，这话怎么讲？」

他解释了一番，随即发现我那堆破烂一件标签也没贴。这事我已经愁了很久。法国航线的贴纸没粘住。纽约人和芝加哥人又怎么知道我出过洋？我没法挨个拦住他们亲口相告。

这位可靠的侍者消失了一阵，很快捧回四张漂亮的标签：方形，红边，正中一颗白星，星中央一个深蓝色的 L——那就是我。

「把这些贴牢了，」我嘱咐他，「漂洋过海一趟，犯不上再瞒着人。」

===Original===
Tuesday, October 2. A Regular Hotel.

M. de M. Hanson, looking as if he’d had just as much sleep as I, was in his, or somebody else’s deck chair, reading a yesterday’s New York paper, when I emerged to greet the dawn.

“I don’t know where this came from,” he said, “but it’s got what you want to know. The series opens in Chicago next Saturday. They play there Saturday and Sunday, jump back to New York Monday and play here Tuesday and Wednesday.”

“And,” said I, “may the better team win﻿—in four games.”

We were anchored in the harbor, waiting for a pilot, that was, as usual, late. I was impatient but M. de M. didn’t seem to care. He’s wild about ocean travel so long as it’s stationary.

===Chinese===
10 月 2 日，星期二。一家正规饭店。

我出舱迎接黎明时，M. de M. 汉森正窝在他自己的——也可能是别人的——甲板藤椅里，读一份隔日的纽约报纸，那气色，看上去跟我一样缺觉。

「这报不知打哪儿来的，」他说，「不过你想知道的上头都有。大赛下周六在芝加哥开锣。周六周日在那边打，周一杀回纽约，周二周三在这儿打。」

「那么，」我说，「愿强队获胜——四场解决战斗。」

我们抛锚在港里等引水员，那家伙照例迟到。我心急火燎，M. de M. 却满不在乎。他酷爱海上旅行，只要船停着不动。

===Original===
Presently the youngest of the food commissioners, one Mr. Bowron, joined us. He asked the name of every piece of land in sight. We answered all his questions, perhaps correctly.

“That one,” said M. de M., pointing, “is Staten Island. Of course you’ve heard of it.”

“I’m afraid not,” said Mr. Bowron.

“What!” cried Mr. Hanson. “Never heard of Staten Island!”

===Chinese===
不多时，食品委员里最年轻的一位——鲍伦先生（Mr. Bowron）——加入了我们。视野之内的每一块陆地，他都要问名字。他的问题我们一一作答，答案兴许都对。

「那一块，」M. de M. 指着说，「是史泰登岛。您当然听说过。」

「怕是没有。」鲍伦先生说。

「什么！」汉森先生嚷了起来，「居然没听说过史泰登岛！」

===Original===
“The home of Matty McIntyre,” I put in. “One of the greatest outside lefts in the history of soccer. He played with the Detroit and Chicago elevens in the American League.”

Mr. Bowron looked apologetic.

“And in that direction,” said Mr. Hanson, pointing again, “is Coney Island, where fashionable New York spends its summers.”

“Except,” said I, “the aristocratic old families who can’t be weaned away from Palisades Park.”

Mr. Bowron interviewed us on the subject of hotels.

===Chinese===
「马蒂·麦金太尔（Matty McIntyre）的老家嘛，」我插嘴，「足球史上最伟大的左边锋之一，在美国联盟（American League）效力过底特律和芝加哥的『十一人』队。」

鲍伦先生露出了抱歉的神色。

「还有那个方向，」汉森先生又指着说，「是科尼岛，摩登纽约人消夏的地方。」

「除了那些死活不肯离开帕利塞兹公园（Palisades Park）的贵族旧家。」我说。

鲍伦先生就旅馆问题对我们进行了专访。

===Original===
“There are only two or three first-class ones,” said Mr. Hanson. “The Biltmore’s fair. It’s got elevators and running hot water.”

“But no electric lights,” I objected.

“Oh, yes,” said Mr. Hanson. “They put in electricity and set the meter the week we left.”

===Chinese===
「像样的饭店统共只有两三家，」汉森先生说，「比尔特莫尔（Biltmore）还算凑合。有电梯，有流动热水。」

「可惜没有电灯。」我提出异议。

「哦，有的，」汉森先生说，「咱们动身那礼拜，他们刚装上电，把电表也调好了。」

===Original===
Breakfast was ready, and for the first time on the trip Mr. Hanson ate with a confidence of the future. For the first time he ordered food that was good for him. Previously it hadn’t mattered.

When we went back on deck, the world’s largest open-face clock was on our left, and on our right the business district of Pelham’s biggest suburb. And immediately surrounding us were Peter James and Ring Once and the lounge steward and the deck steward and the dining-room stewards﻿—in fact, all the stewards we’d seen and a great many we hadn’t.

“We’re trapped,” said Mr. Hanson. “Our only chance for escape is to give them all we’ve got. Be ready with your one-pounders and your silver pieces.”

===Chinese===
早饭备好了。旅途中头一回，汉森先生吃得对未来有了信心。头一回，他点了对身子有好处的菜。从前嘛，反正无所谓。

我们回到甲板上时，左边是世上最大的开面钟，右边是佩勒姆（Pelham）最大郊区的商业区。而把我们团团围住的，是彼得·詹姆斯、「响铃一次」、休息室侍者、甲板侍者、餐厅侍者——总之，见过的侍者全数到齐，外加许多没见过的。

「咱们被包围了，」汉森先生说，「突围的唯一机会，是掏出咱们全部家当。把你们的『一磅炮』——一镑一枚的那种——和银角子都备好。」

===Original===
At the end of this unequal conflict﻿—the Battle of the Baltic﻿—Rear-Admiral Lardner’s fleet was all shot to pieces, most of them the size of a dime, and when Mr. Brennan of Yonkers announced that his car would meet the ship and that he would gladly give me a ride to my hotel I could have kissed him on both cheeks. It took my customs inspector about a minute to decide that I was poor and honest. The baroness, though, when we left the dock, was engaged in argument with half a dozen officials, who must have been either heartless or blind.

===Chinese===
在这场实力悬殊的较量——波罗的海之战——的末尾，拉尔德纳海军少将的舰队被打得粉碎，多数碎片只有一毛钱硬币那么大。这时，扬克斯的布伦南先生（Mr. Brennan of Yonkers）宣布他的汽车会来码头接船，并且乐意捎我去饭店，我恨不得在他两边腮帮子上各亲一口。我的海关关员只花了约莫一分钟，就断定我这个人又穷又老实。倒是我们那位男爵夫人，离码头时还正跟半打官员据理力争，那几位不是没心肝就是没长眼。

===Original===
Mr. Brennan’s chauffeur drove queerly. He insisted on sticking to the right side of the street, and slowed up at busy intersections, and he even paid heed to the traffic signals. In Paris or London he’d have been as much at home as a Mexican at The Hague.

The hotel gave me a room without making me tell my age or my occupation or my parents’ birthplace. The room has a bath, and the bath has two water faucets, one marked hot and one marked cold, and when you turn the one marked hot, out comes hot water. And there’s no Peter James around to make you bathe when you don’t feel the need.

The room has a practical telephone too, and pretty soon I’m going to start calling up acquaintances with kind hearts and good cooks. The first who invites me to dinner is in tough luck.

===Chinese===
布伦南先生的司机开车的路子很怪。他死守街道右侧不放，到了热闹的路口还减速，甚至肯理会交通信号灯。这要是在巴黎或伦敦，他会自在得如同一个去了海牙的墨西哥人。

饭店给了我一个房间，没叫我交代年龄、职业，也没问爹妈生在哪里。房间带浴室，浴室有两个水龙头，一个标着热，一个标着冷，拧开标「热」的那个，流出来的就是热水。而且四下没有一个彼得·詹姆斯，在你没觉得有必要的时候逼你洗澡。

房间里的电话也是真能打通的。回头我就开始给那些心肠好、厨艺好的熟人挨个打过去。头一个请我吃饭的，可要倒大霉了。

===Original===
Friday, October 5. Chicago.

“Miner” Brown, the great three-fingered pitcher, used to be asked the same questions by everyone to whom he was introduced. As a breath-saving device he finally had some special cards printed. On one side was his name. On the other the correct replies:

1. Because I used to work in a mine.
2. It was cut off in a factory when I was a kid.
3. At Terre Haute, Ind.
4. Rosedale, right near Terre Haute.
5. Not a bit.

When he left home in the morning he was always supplied with fifty of these cards, and sometimes he got rid of the whole supply before bedtime.

===Chinese===
10 月 5 日，星期五。芝加哥。

伟大的三指投手「矿工」布朗（"Miner" Brown），从前每逢被人引见，听到的总是同样几个问题。为了省下喘气的力气，他索性印了一批特制卡片。一面印着他的名字，另一面印着标准答案：

1. 因为我从前在矿上干过活。
2. 小时候在工厂里弄掉的。
3. 在印第安纳州特雷霍特（Terre Haute）。
4. 罗斯代尔（Rosedale），就在特雷霍特边上。
5. 一点儿也不。

他每天早上出门，身上总揣着五十张这种卡片，有时不到上床睡觉，就发了个精光。

---

===Original===
I departed from New York Wednesday night. Our train picked up the New York Baseball Club at Philadelphia. I was acquainted with about fifteen of the twenty (odd) athletes. Every one of the fifteen, from Mr. Zimmerman down, shot the same queries at me. Every person I’ve encountered here at home too, and usually in the same order:

1. How’d you like it over there?
2. Did you see any subs?
3. Did you see any fighting?
4. Could you hear the guns?
5. How close did you get to the front?
6. Did you see any American soldiers?
7. How many men have we got over there?
8. How are things in Paris?
9. Were you in England?
10. How are things in London?
11. Were you in any air raids?
12. How long is it going to last?

Now truth may be stranger than fiction, but it’s also a whole lot duller. Most of my answers have very evidently bored my audiences to the point of extinction. Yet I hesitate to start weaving the well-known tangled web. I’d be bound to trip in it sooner or later. Last night, in desperation, I drafted a card along the line of Mr. Brown’s. But it lacked wallop, as you can see for yourself.

1. Oh, pretty well.
2. No.
3. A little.
4. Oh, yes.
5. A mile and a half, on the observation hill.
6. Oh, yes.
7. That’s supposed to be a secret.
8. Pretty gay.
9. Yes.
10. All right, so far as I could see.
11. No.
12. I don’t know.

===Chinese===
我星期三夜里离开纽约。我们的火车在费城捎上了纽约棒球俱乐部。那二十来位运动员里，我认得大约十五位。这十五位，从齐默尔曼先生（Mr. Zimmerman）往下，冲我发射的问题一模一样。回国后在家乡碰见的每一个人也都是这一套，而且顺序通常不变：

1. 那边儿怎么样，你喜欢吗？
2. 见着潜艇没有？
3. 看见打仗没有？
4. 炮声听得见吗？
5. 离前线最近到过什么地方？
6. 见着美国兵没有？
7. 咱们在那边有多少人？
8. 巴黎现在怎么样？
9. 去英国了吗？
10. 伦敦现在怎么样？
11. 赶上空袭没有？
12. 这仗还要打多久？

实情也许比小说离奇，可也乏味得多。我的那些回答，显然大多把听众无聊到了断气的边缘。可我又实在不忍心开始编织那张著名的、越缠越乱的谎网（tangled web），迟早我会在里头绊个跟头。昨夜情急之下，我照布朗先生的路子起草了一张卡片。可惜分量不够，您自己瞧：

1. 哦，还不错。
2. 没有。
3. 有一点儿。
4. 哦，听得见。
5. 一英里半，在观战山上。
6. 哦，有。
7. 这该算机密吧。
8. 相当热闹。
9. 去了。
10. 就我看还凑合。
11. 没有。
12. 不知道。
